SINCE v2022.193.20 - @ 2026-10-08
   CPU on the rpis roughly halved, measured with py-spy, not guessed:
     - beaconloop 25-30% -> 6%  (a 79-tag search whose result was thrown away, per-byte hex,
       a 10 Hz poll that found nothing)
     - display.py 65% -> ~30%  (the whole command list was redrawn for every clock tick)
     - pigpiod 11% -> 5 us only where it is needed (pulse, ultrasound, PWM, IR), 10 us elsewhere
   NO MORE COMMAND LINE in the BLE path:
     - gatttool, hcitool, hciconfig -> raw-HCI ioctls, HCI commands, ATT over L2CAP
     - the shell stays only as the py2 fallback; those tools are deprecated and already missing
       from some BlueZ builds, where the old code failed silently
     - phone presence reads the REAL tx power now, not an ACL handle byte
   SMALLER BUGS, several of which had never worked at all:
     - the camera plot deleted its png before knowing the compressed one existed
     - its contrast window was centred below the data; playsound had a "++" typo
   NEW REPORTING:
     - rPi states and props as tables, one column per rPi
     - an rPi that does not answer ssh is logged as "down" or "hanging, needs a hard reset"

v2022.194.20  @ 2026-10-08
   PLUGIN: PM1, PM4 and PM10 device states are now written. Only PM25 ever was, because the
       compact Ruuvi Air format 06 carries no other particle size - but E1 carries PM1.0, PM2.5,
       PM4.0 and PM10, and the other three states existed on the device and sat empty. Nothing was
       missing in the declarations: _addingstates["PM1"/"PM4"/"PM10"] and the matching
       _stateListToDevTypes[...] = {"BLERuuviAir":1} were all there; only the write was. All four
       now go through one loop in the same idiom and unit (ug/m3). Not to be confused with the
       pm10_standard / pm25_env / particles_03um list further down, which is the PMSA003I sensor.
   RPI beaconloop: ... AND IN COMBINED MODE TOO. probeExtendedScan reset the SCAN radio with plain
       hciconfig reset in two places, so the same mute-controller bug hit a single dongle pinned to
       "scanner BLE4 + BLE5": the extended scan was armed on a radio that delivers nothing, and
       because that radio is ALSO the only legacy receiver in combined mode the rpi lost EVERY
       message, not just E1. Both are now down+up. The second effect was quieter and worse: the
       reset before the LEGACY measuring phase meant that phase counted a mute radio, which is why
       it reported "BLE4 mode 0 from 0 macs" all evening on a dongle that does 56/s, read as "NO
       BLE4 baseline", and let extended win a comparison that never happened. With down+up the
       test finally compares: "BLE5 mode 82 reports from 29 macs, BLE4 mode 186 from 36 macs ->
       81% of the macs" and extended wins on merit.
       THREE PLACES IN ONE DAY had this same bug - qualifyDongle's resetHCI(), the extended
       listener, and the scan radio. hciconfig reset is not a reset on this hardware; it leaves the
       controller accepting every command with status 0 and delivering nothing. Treat it as banned
       and use down+up: there are still other hciDev(..,"reset") call sites worth auditing.
   RPI beaconloop: RUUVI AIR E1 NOW ARRIVES. The extended listener reset its radio with hciconfig
       "reset" and then "up" - but reset leaves some controllers MUTE: they accept every command
       with status 0 and deliver nothing, and the "up" is a no-op because the device never went
       down. qualifyDongle's resetHCI() was changed away from plain reset earlier the same day for
       exactly this reason; the listener still had it. The symptom was not an extended-advertising
       problem at all - the radio delivered NOTHING: measured on rpi#2 with the TP-Link UB500,
       0 legacy and 0 extended reports while beaconloop ran, against 446 legacy and 29 extended
       from the same dongle 12 s after a DOWN+UP. Now down+up, and E1 flows: 56 x 9904E1 beside
       52 x 990406 at the parser in 38 s, where every run before was a flat zero, and indigo gets
       the full set - PM1 0.4 / PM2.5 0.9 / PM4 1.3 / PM10 1.5, CO2 641, VOC 137, NOx 1, and the
       24-bit measurement counter instead of format 06's 8-bit one.
       Found by asking whether the radio received ANYTHING rather than chasing E1 - zero of every
       kind is a dead radio, not a format problem. Three wrong turns before that (the junk-length
       ceiling, an UnboundLocalError that was the test harness's own, the probe cadence) were all
       reasoning ahead of the evidence.
   RPI beaconloop: the extended listener RE-ARMS the scan after a silent minute - event mask,
       params, enable, on the existing socket. Not a radio reset: that is what the old 20 s/40 s
       ladder did and it makes a Barrot deliver one burst and die. The listener is idempotent on
       purpose, so when something else resets its radio the controller reverts to defaults with LE
       event mask bit 12 (extended advertising report) OFF, the socket does not error, and the
       thread reads a scan that is no longer running - forever. Silence stays a legitimate state;
       we just stop assuming the scan we armed is still armed.
   RPI piBeaconUtils: ble4ScanSupported IS DESTRUCTIVE AND NOW RUNS ONCE PER RADIO, EVER. It tests
       BLE4 capability by sending LE Set Scan Parameters (0x000B) and reading the status - 0 means
       capable, 0x0C means extended-only - and it ran on EVERY radio on every HCI refresh, which is
       every few seconds while beaconloop scans. That legacy command puts the controller back into
       the LEGACY command family, and the two are mutually exclusive until an HCI reset, so an
       extended scan on that radio then answers status 0 and delivers NOTHING. Proven on rpi#2: two
       radios scanning AT THE SAME TIME after a reset gave hci0 161 extended reports incl. 14 Ruuvi
       E1 and hci1 842 legacy reports; the identical test without the reset gave hci0 ZERO with
       every command status 0. The verdict is a fixed property of the firmware, so it is now cached
       by mac and persisted in ble4ScanCap.json - verified: 0x200B no longer reaches hci0. The old
       docstring's "cheap enough for every startup" was true of the round trip and wrong about the
       side effect. Note it is a SEPARATE PROCESS problem too: master and BLEconnect hold their own
       copy of piBeaconUtils, so they keep probing until they restart.
   RPI beaconloop: the extended listener can now be observed. Its 60 s report count was behind a
       hardcoded _debugheartbeat = False, so "is this radio receiving anything at all" could not be
       asked without editing source - and the flag read was nested inside the 0x0D branch, so a
       listener receiving NOTHING never armed the one diagnostic that would have said so. Both are
       now file flags, read at loop level: temp/extListener.heartbeat (count per minute) and
       temp/extListener.raw (every packet and every timeout as it arrives, capped at 60 lines).
       Those two are what finally produced "0 extended report(s)/min from 0 mac(s)" and the
       command-complete trace that identified 0x200B.
   RPI beaconloop: probeExtendedScan is THROTTLED to once per 30 s. setupSOCKET calls it on every
       socket setup and the main loop sets the socket up every couple of seconds - measured on
       rpi#2: probes at 19:02:50, :52, :58, 19:03:00, :06, :08, :14, :16, :22. Each one disables
       the scan, re-asserts mask and parameters, re-enables it, and through cmdComplete - which
       reads the socket for up to 1.2 s per command waiting for its command-complete - DISCARDS
       every advertising report it meets. Five commands every two seconds on the socket the reader
       is trying to read: the scan was being dismantled faster than it could deliver.
   RPI beaconloop: the no-message watchdog is not applied within 15 s of a probe. A probe takes
       ~8 s during which the reader cannot receive at all, and the watchdog judged on a clock that
       had run throughout: extended was VERIFIED at 19:05:41.9 and condemned at 19:05:47.2, 5.3 s
       later. With the guard it gets a full window - and then still delivered nothing in 40 s, so
       the delivery failure on this radio is real and not a measuring artifact.
   RPI beaconloop: the legacy fallback RESETS THE CONTROLLER FIRST (down+up, not HCIDEVRESET which
       leaves this radio mute). The two scan command families are mutually exclusive until a reset
       - after extended commands the legacy ones answer 0x0C - so arming legacy on a latched
       controller did nothing at all: the fallback fired at 19:02:42 and 41 s later there was still
       no data. STILL OPEN: extScanForceLegacy does not survive the in-process stack restart, so
       extended is re-proved and the loop can resume; rpi#2 is pinned to scanBLE4 meanwhile.
   RPI beaconloop: NO BLE5 TRAFFIC IS NOT A FAULT, so the stack is no longer restarted for it.
       Extended scanning can be running perfectly and deliver nothing, because nothing on the air
       is sending extended advertisements. The watchdog restarted the whole stack for that and only
       fell back to legacy after TWO such restarts, so rpi#2 simply looped - restart, re-prove
       extended, no messages, restart - for 25 minutes reporting no beacons at all. It now falls
       back on the FIRST empty cycle, re-arms the scan IN PLACE with the legacy family, and leaves
       the program running: extScanForceLegacy stops the probe turning extended back on and
       saveExtScanVerdict(False) makes the next program start re-prove it. The no-message clocks
       (lastMSGwithDataPlain / lastMSGwithDataPassed / nEmptyMessagesInARow) are reset at the same
       time, because the watchdog fires on ">10 s without a message" and that clock has already run
       out by then - without the reset it restarted 2.2 s after the fallback, measured: fell back
       18:55:49.4, restarted 18:55:51.6. With the reset: fell back 19:00:03.5, zero restarts after
       it, and 655 legacy frames reached the parser in the next 40 s.
   RPI beaconloop: THREE CONTRADICTIONS IN ONE STARTUP LOG, all three about radio roles.
       (1) "broadcast role -> hci1 (with the scanner), internal stays free for BLEconnect" was
       logged as a decision and the next block then moved it, so the same startup also said
       "broadcast:hci2". The default is now silent and ONE roles line is logged once everything is
       settled. (2) That roles line hardcoded "scan(EXTENDED)" and printed it one line above
       "BLE4-only scanning on hci1 - pinned to scanner BLE4". It now names the mode it really is.
       (3) A scanBLE4 pin means extended will never be attempted, so there is nothing to clear off
       the scan radio - but the broadcast was moved anyway, for no reason and against the stated
       strategy of keeping the internal radio free for BLEconnect alone. It now stays with the
       scanner unless extended scanning is actually possible. Also "only 2 radio(s) UP, BLE5 needs
       3" sat directly above an hciconfig dump showing THREE radios UP: the third was UP and
       "ignore"-pinned, dropped before the count. It now says "usable" and names the ignored ones.
   RPI beaconloop: the junk-length ceiling is ONE number again, 510, instead of 110-unless-
       scanExtendedMode. A Ruuvi Air E1 frame measures 112 chars after fillHCIdump strips the
       14-char preamble (7 preamble + 6 mac + 1 len + 48 data + 1 rssi = 63 bytes = 126 hex, less
       14) - TWO characters over the legacy limit - so any moment that flag was false dropped every
       E1 frame, and this is the only drop in the pipeline that left no trace at all: no log, no
       trackmac line. A legacy advertisement cannot exceed 110 chars anyway, so the narrow limit
       bought nothing. The drops are now REPORTED, grouped by length, which is what proved the gate
       innocent on rpi#2: no 112-char drops at all. The report EXCLUDES the one length that is
       expected - every set carries a 0-char frame, the command-complete event of our own scan
       re-enable, empty once the preamble is stripped - because reporting that printed the same
       sentence every 8 seconds and said nothing. It is counted, and shown only beside a length
       that does matter. Diagnosing a silent discard by reasoning about it does not work;
       counting it does.
   RPI beaconloop: trackmac was DEAD. readTrackMacRequest checked for temp/beaconloop.trackmac at
       the right path and then opened it at "  > temp/beaconloop.trackmac" - a shell redirect
       pasted into the string literal - so every request raised, was swallowed by the bare except,
       and trackMac was never set. The whole per-mac frame tracer has been unusable.
   RPI beaconloop: the delivery test no longer invents a coverage percentage. With an empty legacy
       phase, 100.0*_uE/max(1,_uL) divided 30 macs by 1 and logged "3000% of the macs", which then
       passed the >=60% test as well - the ASUS went to extended mode on a comparison that never
       happened. A missing baseline is now reported as a missing baseline, and the pass condition
       asks the real question instead: a BLE5-only radio REJECTS the legacy commands with 0x0C
       (proven on the Barrot and the BT540), so commands accepted with zero reports is a failed
       measurement, not BLE5-only firmware. Those two had shared one code path. An explicit
       scanBLE45 / scanBLE5 pin overrules the comparison outright - that is what pinning is for.
   RPI beaconloop: A DELIVERY TEST THAT HEARD NOTHING AT ALL IS NO LONGER A VERDICT. probeExtended-
       Scan compares extended against legacy delivery on the same air, and on rpi#2 it logged "BLE5
       mode 0 report(s) from 0 macs, BLE4 mode 0 from 0 macs, legacy cmd status params:0x00
       enable:0x00" - every command accepted, nothing delivered in EITHER family, on the radio
       qualifyDongle had just measured at 56/s legacy and 24/s extended. The probe had run 9 s after
       a parameter reload, while the stack was still being rebuilt. It then set extScanForceLegacy
       and persisted BLE5:false, so every later probe short-circuited with "disabled earlier this
       session" once a minute forever and the rpi stayed on BLE4 - which is why a Ruuvi Air kept
       arriving as the compact format 06 and the full E1 data set (PM1.0/4.0/10, luminosity, 24-bit
       sequence) never came at all. A legacy phase that hears nothing says nothing about extended,
       so nothing is cached or persisted and the next cycle re-probes. Bounded at EXTSCAN_MAX_RETRY
       = 5, because each probe costs ~8 s of scan downtime - the right price for a startup race and
       the wrong one forever; after that it settles for BLE4 as before. A verified probe clears the
       streak.
   PLUGIN rpi device dialog: "BLEconnect + iphone" is now offered on EVERY radio of an rpi that
       has more than one, internal or USB. It was offered on a USB dongle ONLY when the rpi had no
       internal radio, on the strategy that connect belongs on the internal one because its
       scanning fights wifi while connects tolerate it. That reasoning belongs to the automatic
       pick, not to a manual pin - restricting the override is what defeats the point of the menu.
       rpi#2 has two dongles AND an internal radio, so there was no way to move connect off the
       internal radio at all, even though that radio is one die and one antenna shared with wifi.
       The rpi already honours it: pinnedRadio() checks present, UP and unclaimed and has no bus
       condition, so nothing was needed on the rpi side. A single-radio rpi still gets neither
       BLEconnect nor "ignore" - that radio does every job. nUSB is gone with the gate that read it.
   RPI qualifyDongle v2.9: the legacy-scan and advertise phases now IDLE THE RADIO FIRST, which
       phaseExtended and the connect phase already did. "LE Set Scan Parameters" is disallowed
       (0x0C) by the spec while scanning is enabled, and "LE Set Advertising Parameters" while
       advertising is - and resetHCI's down+up hands the adapter straight back to bluetoothd, which
       re-arms its own background LE scan. So the tool could reject a radio for its own leftover
       state: the ASUS 0b05:1d70 reported legCmdStatus [12,12] and advCmdStatus [12,12,12] in the
       same run as a working extended scan of 32.9 reports/s from 45 macs. Both phases now send
       scan-off, extended-scan-off, adv-off and extended-adv-off (Enable=0, Num_Sets=0 = all sets)
       before touching the parameters, so a 0x0C that survives really is a firmware limit. It did
       survive on the ASUS: that dongle gets no firmware at all on this kernel - see below.
   RPI qualifyDongle v2.9: MAC COVERAGE across the radios of one rpi. countReports keeps a per-mac
       report count and best rssi, and a new section answers what the mac COUNTS could not: "the
       uart heard 59, the tp-link 50" - which 9, and do they matter? A mac the best radio heard >=3
       times in the window has an interval short enough that it had to transmit during every other
       window too, so missing it is a real miss; rarer macs are listed apart instead of being
       scored, because the phases run SEQUENTIALLY and a once-a-minute tag may simply not have
       transmitted. The rssi line is the diagnosis and has THREE outcomes, not two, with a minimum
       of 3 scored misses before it says anything: weaker than what was heard = sensitivity or
       placement, just as strong = dropping reports it can hear, STRONGER = not range at all but a
       mac that was not transmitting in the other windows. That last case is why the minimum
       exists - the first live run compared ONE missed mac at -40 dBm against 66 heard at -81 and
       called it "just as strong", from a single sample and backwards by 41 dB. The line also
       reconciles now: "missing 8 = 1 scored + 7 too rare to judge", because "real misses: 1" next
       to "heard 65/73" read as "only one mac missing" when 8 were. Per-mac data stays local -
       neither the shared dongleCatalogue.json nor qualifyDongle.json carries it.
   RPI qualifyDongle v2.9: THE SCAN ROLE IS DECIDED ON MAC COVERAGE, not reports/s. Two live runs
       on rpi#2 showed why: the onboard radio delivered 821-878 reports in 10 s - more than both
       dongles together - and heard the FEWEST macs, while the ASUS BT500 heard the most at two
       thirds the rate. piBeacon summarises every ~60 s and keeps an AVERAGE rssi, so a tag the
       radio never hears is lost outright and a tag heard 80 times instead of 50 is worth nothing
       extra. The old rate test also contradicted itself: the UB500 lost the role at 39.5/s (needed
       40) while hearing more macs than radios that passed. COVERAGE_GOOD 85% / COVERAGE_OK 65%,
       bands set wide because the two runs moved each radio by ~5 points and nobody should change
       role between runs. The rate threshold survives as the FALLBACK only - coverage is a
       comparison between the radios of one rpi, so an rpi with a single radio has nothing to
       measure against and still uses reports/s. main() therefore measures EVERY adapter before
       judging any of them, with one progress line per adapter so the wait is not silence.
   RPI qualifyDongle v2.9: the onboard radio carries one unconditional note - it is one die and one
       antenna shared with WiFi, time-sliced, so its rate holds only while WiFi is unused and a USB
       dongle is the better scan radio. Stated WITHOUT reading wlan0 on purpose: the state during
       the measurement says nothing about the state next month, and a radio that looks good today
       because wlan0 happens to be down is the wrong choice the moment someone switches WiFi on.
       The rate is NOT demoted - an rpi whose only radio is the onboard one still has to scan.
   RPI qualifyDongle v2.9: ROTATING PRIVATE ADDRESSES ARE LEFT OUT OF THE COVERAGE UNION. The
       phases run sequentially, so a phone that rotates its address between two radios' windows is
       counted as a mac BOTH radios missed, and the union grows with addresses nobody could have
       matched. Measured on rpi#2: the ASUS was charged with missing 66:B0:1E:B3:DA:65,
       69:8C:AE:CA:47:5E and 58:D6:9C:2D:80:7D - three addresses, ~40 msgs each, ALL at exactly
       -67 dBm and all 01xxxxxx, i.e. one or two phones it had already heard under another name;
       23 of the 80 macs in that run were heard by one radio only. addrRotates() reads the
       Address_Type byte from the report ([6] legacy, [7] extended) and, only when it says Random,
       the top two bits of the address's most significant byte: 11 static random is kept, 01
       resolvable private and 00 non-resolvable are dropped. The type byte is read FIRST because a
       public address's first byte is an OUI octet where those bit patterns mean nothing - this is
       the case that cannot be decided from the address alone. The excluded count is printed, not
       hidden, and the roles stop flipping on noise: the ASUS was 1.2 points above COVERAGE_GOOD
       and the UB500 1.2 above COVERAGE_OK before this.
   RPI display.py: the clock-only redraw diagnostic is now SILENT on a healthy display. It reported
       two things that were not faults: "clock is cmd N of M, so the clock-only box cannot cover the
       later commands", which is the normal state of every layout that draws anything after the
       clock, and the "N full-frame send(s) since the last report" count - both printed the same
       sentence every minute for the life of the program. displaySendCount is gone with the line
       that was its only reader. What still logs is only a gate that actually rejects: flipDisplay,
       a non-screen device, a size mismatch, unreadable clock geometry, or a cached command that is
       not an analogClock - each of those is a surprise worth seeing once.

v2022.194.19  @ 2026-10-07
   RPI+PLUGIN: the rpi->plugin socket path, end to end. Four changes, measured not guessed.
       NO MORE BLIND SLEEP BEFORE THE ACK. execSend did "time.sleep(0.2 + min(10, lld/20000))" after
       sendall and before reading the reply - but recv() already blocks until the plugin answers and
       settimeout() already bounds it, so there was no race to protect against and the sleep was
       simply dead time on every message: 0.24 s for a small beacon payload, 0.68 s for 60 tags,
       2.25 s for a BLEanalysis report. For comparison the actual transmit of 279 kB is 22 ms on
       100 Mb and 3 ms on gigabit, and compressing it costs 2 ms. The sleep was three orders of
       magnitude bigger than the thing it sat next to.
       COMPRESSION IS NOW HARDWIRED AT 1000 BYTES and the plugin option is gone - removed from
       PluginConfig.xml, from both copies in Devices.xml, from kDefaultPluginPrefs and from the four
       code sites, and the value the plugin used to push is ignored by the rpi. The pref defaulted to
       20000, which meant that in practice nothing was ever compressed. Compressing costs 0.01-0.02 ms
       for normal traffic, and a 20-tag payload goes 3252 -> 688 bytes, a dongle report 2873 -> 143.
       (The per-rpi override in makeParametersFile never worked anyway: "min(..)" with no assignment.)
       THE FRAME HEADER NOW CARRIES A FIXED 5 DIGIT LENGTH IN 35 BYTES, was a bare number in 30. The
       width therefore depended on how many DIGITS the length happened to have, and "pi_IN_Alive" is
       11 characters, leaving room for exactly 4 - so any alive message over 9999 bytes on the wire
       built a 31 character header, the receiver's data0[:30] clipped "+comp" to "+com", compressed
       bytes were handed to json.loads, and the message died with "error-Json" on all five retries.
       Measured: a 400-tag beacon payload already compresses to 9768 bytes, and an alive message of
       28807 bytes (12879 on the wire) is dropped by the old frame and accepted by the new one.
       Over 99999 bytes the rpi now refuses to send and says so instead of corrupting the frame.
       ONE width only - the plugin reads a 35 byte header and nothing else, so the PLUGIN HAS TO BE
       RELOADED BEFORE THE RPIS ARE PUSHED. An rpi still sending the old 30 byte frame is not
       understood, and a new rpi talking to an old plugin is not either. Verified by a round-trip
       test over three pi names, six payload sizes and six chunk sizes down to one byte per read:
       108 combinations, no failures, and the 28807 byte alive message that the old frame dropped
       now arrives.
       NaN: the receiver found it and then THREW THE REPLACEMENT AWAY - "dataJIn.replace(..)" without
       assigning - so NaN reached json.loads, which happily returns float nan, and indigo does not
       accept it. The identical line 30 lines further up in the same file does assign. Fixed, and the
       sender-side test "find(NaN) > 0" is now "> -1" like every other one (a hit at index 0 was
       missed; json always starts with "{" so it never bit, but the asymmetry was confusing).
       Three logging faults in the handler: "indigo.indigoLOG" (does not exist - it raised
       AttributeError and the enclosing except reported "error-general", hiding the "data too short"
       diagnosis the line was written to give), "dataJIn[:-20]" where [-20:] was meant (it logged the
       whole payload instead of its tail - 100 kB of log for one BLEanalysis message), and two error
       paths that called sendResponse() twice although it closes the socket, so the second call always
       threw and logged a level-30 error that looked like a socket fault but was self-inflicted.
   PLUGIN: a parse failure on the rpi socket is not raised as an error for the first 3 minutes after
       the plugin starts, and is counted in the socket stats as "preUpdate" rather than under the
       first 10 characters of the exception - that put a row called "a bytes-li" with no pi name into
       the table for every rpi still on the old format, one per distinct error text (self.socketQuietSecs, the sibling of hciErrorQuietSecs). On a version bump
       the PLUGIN reloads first and the rpis are pushed afterwards, so for a few minutes every rpi is
       still sending the previous frame format and every single message fails to parse - an old
       uncompressed frame as "bad length", an old compressed one as a decode error at level 40, and
       with compression now starting at 1 kB most of them are the second kind. Both paths are kept
       VISIBLE at info level, naming the rpi and saying it is most likely still waiting for its
       update, and neither is reported as an error. After 3 minutes they are errors again.

   RPI+PLUGIN: "data may be corrupt" now travels as "data_may_be_corrupt", on BOTH sides. dosfsck
       reports a failing sd card with that phrase, master.py forwards it and the plugin matched it
       literally - but the text went out with squeeze on, arrived as "datamaybecorrupt", and the
       match never fired, so a failing card was reported by the rpi and silently dropped. The rpi now
       underscores the message before sending and the plugin looks for the underscore form, which
       survives whatever the transport does to spaces; the old spaced form is still accepted so an
       rpi that has not been updated yet is still heard.

   RPI: sendURL no longer squeezes the spaces out of everything it sends. json.dumps with
       separators=(",",":") already leaves no structural whitespace, so "dataC.replace(\" \",\"\")"
       could only ever strip spaces out of string VALUES - it mangled the text of every message that
       did not explicitly ask it not to. All 38 explicit squeeze= arguments in the code are False,
       and two places had resorted to writing underscores by hand so the text would still be readable
       at the other end. It also broke a check that matters: master.py reports a corrupt sd card by
       sending "... data may be corrupt ..." and the plugin looks for exactly that phrase in the raw
       message - which arrived as "datamaybecorrupt" and never matched, so a failing card was never
       flagged. The default is False now; the underscore comments are marked historical.
   PLUGIN: restartmasterSSH moved from 2 minutes of silence to 3, and stage 2 needed its OWN
       threshold to make that possible. It used to fire at 2*dontcheckFor, which for a ble rpi is
       exactly 180 s - the same number - so the stage 1 window "180 <= silentFor < 180" would have
       been EMPTY, the plain restart would never have run at all, and the reconfigure + reboot would
       simply have taken over at three minutes. reconfigAfter = max(2*dontcheckFor, restartAfter+180)
       keeps both rpi types at 3 minutes to restart master and 6 to reconfigure.

   PLUGIN: every rpi's silence clock now starts at PLUGIN START, and on top of that, for the first
       3 minutes, ANY message from an rpi resets it - parsed or not.
       The first half matters on its own: lastSSDCheck held whenever that rpi's last message had been
       decoded, which after a reload is hours ago - or never, for an rpi this instance has not heard
       from yet - so checkifNewSSD judged the whole fleet as long dead and would have run
       restartmasterSSH and then configure + reboot across all of it within minutes of a reload. A
       value already in the FUTURE is kept, so a plugin restart cannot cancel the quiet period the
       dongle test announced.
       The second half covers the version-bump window: checkifNewSSD judges an rpi by lastSSDCheck, which was only
       refreshed by a message the plugin had successfully DECODED, so an rpi that is up and talking
       but whose frames the plugin cannot read looked dead. That is precisely the state every rpi is
       an rpi that is up and talking but whose frames the plugin cannot read looked dead, which is
       precisely the state every rpi is in after a version bump until it is pushed.
       ONLY in that window, deliberately. Afterwards an unparseable message must not count as proof
       of life either - an rpi that keeps sending something the plugin cannot read is broken and has
       to be recovered, not excused for ever - so from then on only a decoded message refreshes the
       clock, exactly as before. A lastSSDCheck in the FUTURE is left alone in both cases, so a stray
       message cannot cancel the quiet period the dongle test announces in advance.

v2022.194.17  @ 2026-10-06
   RPI: the dongle qualification measured radios that were still in use, and then blamed the radios.
       temp/beaconloop.pause stops the main scan and the advertising, but NOT the extListener on a
       second radio - and a create-connection on a radio that is still scanning is refused by the
       controller with ENOSYS. Measured on two rpis against two different switchbots: the SAME
       dongle gives 0-30% while something is still scanning and 100% once everything is stopped. The
       ASUS on one rpi read "connect FAILED in all 3 attempts" in the august catalogue and 10/10 at
       0.1 s today; the ASUS on the other read 30% and then 100%. Every "connect unreliable" verdict
       produced that way was an artefact of the measurement. runQualifyDongle therefore STOPS the
       radio users instead of pausing them: master.sh/startmaster.sh first (master.sh restarts
       master.py within 90 s, and master.py's killSudos kills the measurement tool's own sudo
       wrapper), then master.py, beaconloop.py and BLEconnect.py. receiveCommands stays alive on
       purpose - it runs the test, it sends the report back, and while it answers the plugin the rpi
       is not completely silent. The kill is VERIFIED and says so in the log if anything survived,
       because a kill that silently did nothing is the one failure that invalidates a whole run
       without looking like an error. master.sh is started again in a finally, so a crash or the
       600 s cap cannot leave the rpi down. The report is written OUTSIDE temp/ now: master.py
       EMPTIES that directory when it starts, which deleted a whole report mid-run (and the runner
       script with it).

   RPI: stopAll.sh never stopped master.py, and said it had. master.py is started from inside the
       piBeacon directory as "python3 -E master.py" - a RELATIVE path - so its command line does not
       contain /home/pi/pibeacon and the "python.*$HOMEDIR" pattern never matched it. Both the kills
       and the status listing used that one blind pattern, so the script printed "all piBeacon
       programs stopped" while master.py was still running with 4h40m of uptime, restarted
       beaconloop/BLEconnect within seconds and killed the measurement tool through killSudos - the
       tool died silently right after "resetting all adapters", with no traceback and no report.
       The pattern now also covers master.py and callbeacon.py, and a second one covers the shell
       helpers under $HOMEDIR (getRaspiConfig.sh and friends), which no python pattern could ever
       see and which survived every previous run. One source feeds both the listing and the kills.

   RPI: a BLE5-only dongle was awarded the one role it provably cannot do. Coverage for the combined
       BLE4+BLE5 scanner test is "macs heard in BLE5 mode / macs heard in BLE4 mode", and a radio
       with no BLE4 scan mode hears 0 in BLE4 mode - whereupon the old "else 100.0" handed it PERFECT
       coverage of a set that does not exist. The UGREEN/Barrot 33fa:0012 therefore reported
       "ROLES: BLE5-listener, scan-BLE4+BLE5, ..." on the very same report that said "BLE4 scan
       commands REJECTED (0x0C) - cannot be the scan radio", and printed "reaches 100% of the macs it
       hears in BLE4 mode (8 of 0)". A combined scanner is one radio REPLACING a BLE4 scanner plus a
       BLE5 listener; with no BLE4 mode there is nothing to replace and nothing to compare against,
       so the role is refused and the reason is named. The percentage is also capped for display -
       extended mode can legitimately hear MORE macs than the legacy run did, which printed "104%".

   RPI: the qualification tool reset the adapters in a way that could break them, and then graded the
       wreck. resetHCI/resetAllAdapters used "hciconfig <hci> reset" - HCIDEVRESET - which does not
       clear every controller and leaves some DOWN. Measured here on a BCM20702A0 (0a5c:21e8): it
       stayed down for the whole run and was reported as "HEALTH: adapter does not come up properly
       - unusable", 0 reports, ADVERTISE FAILED, while dmesg showed it only came back when the USB
       device re-enumerated. The same dongle then did 10/10 connects at 0.2-1.7 s. Both functions now
       do a plain down+up, and resetHCI retries once with a longer settle before calling an adapter
       dead - a controller the kernel has to re-probe is not always back within half a second.
       This is the same lesson beaconloop carries as hciNeedsDownUp / temp/hciMuteOnReset.json.

   RPI: the connect test measured the leftover state of its own scan phases. It runs straight after
       two scan phases and an advertise phase on the SAME radio, and a create-connection on a
       controller that still has a scan running is refused with ENOSYS (SO_ERROR:38) - so the test
       counted its own warm-up as dongle failures. It reported the ASUS at 30% (7x ENOSYS) where the
       same radio does 100% once the scan is stopped, and marked the onboard radio - the proven
       connector of that rpi - as "connect(weak)" off a single ENOSYS. Which radio happens to eat the
       leftover state is luck, not a property of the dongle. The phase now disables legacy scan,
       extended scan and advertising on the adapter first (phaseExtended already did exactly this
       before its own measurement), and spends ONE uncounted warm-up connect before the counted
       attempts. The warm-up result is printed whenever it failed, so nothing is hidden: three
       ENOSYS in a row followed by seven clean connects at 0.23 s is information about the radio,
       not something to bury.
   RPI: "DOWN   <-- not usable" for a radio that then scanned at 94.5/s. resetAllAdapters read the
       status half a second after the up-all loop, and a USB BCM20702A0 needs longer than that to
       come back after a down - the giveaway was on the same line, which already showed its ACL MTU
       as 1021 while hciconfig still said DOWN. It now gives such an adapter a second down+up through
       resetHCI before printing, so the status line reflects the adapter the run actually measures.

   RPI: "rssi mean/max -71/123 dBm" - +123 dBm is not a signal level, it is a byte from somewhere
       else. RSSI is a signed byte with a spec range of -127..+20 dBm, and 127 means "not available";
       a single HCI event can also carry SEVERAL reports (ev[4] = num_reports) while the legacy path
       takes ev[-1], which is then only the LAST report's rssi. Out-of-range samples are now
       discarded instead of being averaged in, and the number discarded is PRINTED: a handful is the
       odd malformed report, a large share means the rssi is being read from the wrong byte and the
       mean - and the "sensitivity poor" verdict built on it - cannot be trusted.
   RPI: a dongle that RE-ENUMERATES mid-run was reported as "ROLES: NONE - do not use this dongle".
       The tool addressed adapters by hci NAME, captured once at the start. A BCM20702A0 (0a5c:21e8)
       dropped off the usb bus while being reset and came back under a new name - "usb 1-1.2: USB
       disconnect, device number 7" / "new full-speed USB device number 8", the same radio
       reappearing as hci3 - while everything was still being sent to hci0, a device node that no
       longer existed: ACL MTU 0, zero reports, ADVERTISE FAILED, and a verdict condemning a radio
       that measures 94.5 reports/s under its new name. Adapters are now followed BY MAC: the
       name->mac map is taken before the first reset (once a dongle has re-enumerated the old name
       yields nothing, so the mac cannot be looked up at the moment of failure), and a radio that
       fails to come up is searched for under every other hci name before any verdict is formed.
       If the mac really is gone from the bus the report says THAT - "no longer on the bus under any
       hci name, it disconnected during the run" - instead of blaming the hardware, and points at
       dmesg and the firmware patch. This is the same identity problem beaconloop solves by pinning
       roles by mac; the hci numbers on one rpi moved twice in an afternoon.
       WORTH KNOWING: neither rpi has a BCM20702A1 firmware patch installed - the kernel logs
       "BCM: firmware Patch file not found: brcm/BCM20702A1-0a5c-21e8.hcd" at every probe on both -
       so these dongles run on ROM firmware, which is the usual reason a BCM20702 drops off the bus
       on a reset. That one missing file plausibly accounts for most of the "mute after reset",
       "deaf", "unusable" and hci-renumbering behaviour chased all day.
   RPI: the tool now LOOKS whether piBeacon is running instead of reminding you that it must not be.
       "REMINDER: beaconloop must NOT be running" was printed on every run, including the ones whose
       output was then read as a verdict on the hardware - by everybody, repeatedly. A radio that is
       being scanned on refuses create-connection with ENOSYS and shares its report rate, so a good
       dongle comes out as "connect unreliable" and "scan too slow". Measured on one rpi within
       minutes of itself: the same ASUS 100% connect with piBeacon stopped, 40% with 6x ENOSYS while
       beaconloop was scanning on it, and the onboard radio's connects slowing from 0.1 s to 0.4 s
       mean / 3.9 s max in the same run. qualifyDongle now pgreps for beaconloop/BLEconnect/master,
       prints what it found and what it means at the top, and - the part that matters - carries
       "MEASURED WHILE PIBEACON WAS RUNNING" as the FIRST note of every adapter, so it travels with
       the entry into the shared catalogue. A contaminated run can no longer quietly become received
       wisdom about a dongle model.
   RPI: the bluetooth PATCH RAM firmware is SHIPPED and installed now, which is the actual cause of
       most of the dongle trouble chased in this release. A BCM20702 whose .hcd patch is missing runs
       on ROM firmware and drops off the usb bus whenever it is reset - and neither rpi had it, since
       Debian/Raspberry Pi OS do not package that file (bluez-firmware carries only the ONBOARD
       radios, BCM43430A1/BCM4343A2/BCM4345C0, which is why the uart radio never misbehaved). What it
       looked like instead: a radio that accepts every scan command with status 0 and delivers
       nothing, "adapter does not come up properly - unusable", a dongle reappearing under a new hci
       number mid-run, and roles landing on the wrong radio because the numbering had moved.
           before:  BCM20702A1 (001.002.014) build 0000      <- ROM, "Patch file not found"
           after:   BCM20702A1 'brcm/BCM20702A1-0a5c-21e8.hcd' Patch
                    BCM20702A1 (001.002.014) build 1764
       Measured after installing: 10 down+up cycles in a row, same hci number, UP RUNNING every time,
       ZERO re-enumerations - where before it was roughly one re-enumeration per qualifyDongle run.
       Both rpis are patched.
       pi/copyme/firmware/BCM20702A1/ carries the file (one sub directory per chip family, with its
       README next to it) and arrives as /home/pi/pibeacon/firmware/... through the "put -r copyme/*"
       that both FTP scripts already do - no change to either .exp. pi/installBTfirmware.py puts the
       file into /lib/firmware/brcm/ on EVERY rpi, whether that dongle is plugged in there or not:
       it costs 36 kB, it does nothing until a matching controller is probed, and it means moving a
       dongle to another pi - or plugging in a spare - just works, instead of that controller coming
       up on ROM firmware and dropping off the bus on its first reset with nothing to say why. Only
       the RE-PROBE is conditional, since only a dongle that is actually present can be re-probed;
       where one is, the usb device is re-probed so the driver loads the patch and the build number
       is checked to confirm it took. master.py calls it at startup, BEFORE beaconloop is started,
       so the radio is patched before anything uses it.
       It decides what to do WITHOUT reading dmesg: dmesg keeps the whole boot, so the old "Patch
       file not found" block sits there above the new successful one for ever, and a dmesg-driven
       script would re-probe an already patched radio on every master start - taking the scan radio
       down each time for nothing. It compares the shipped file with /lib/firmware/brcm/ byte for
       byte and looks up whether that usb id is plugged in; dmesg is read afterwards only to CONFIRM.
       Nothing is ever downloaded.
       It REPORTS to the indigo log at level 30, through the existing data->warning channel, but only
       when something actually CHANGED: a patch installed and loaded, a patch staged on an rpi that
       has no such dongle yet, or a failure. The "already installed" case says nothing, so this runs
       at every master start and is silent from the second one onwards - a level-30 line on every
       boot of every rpi would be noise that trains you to ignore the one time it matters. The
       staging line is reported because it happens exactly once per rpi and because without it there
       is no way to confirm "the firmware is on every rpi" short of logging into each one by hand,
       which costs a password prompt per pi. So one rollout produces one line per rpi - a census of
       which ones had the dongle and which were only armed for later - and nothing after that.
       NOTE: the .hcd is proprietary Broadcom firmware, not packaged by Debian, carried here as a
       binary blob.

   RPI: qualifyDongle stops piBeacon itself now, instead of only warning about it. Run by hand - which
       is how every contaminated measurement in this release happened - it stopped nothing, and
       "REMINDER: beaconloop must NOT be running" was read straight past every time. It now stops
       master.sh/master.py/beaconloop/BLEconnect for the measurement and starts them again when it
       ends. The restart is registered with atexit AND with a SIGINT/SIGTERM/SIGHUP handler, because
       the failure here is the worst one this tool has: nothing on an rpi brings master back by
       itself - no cron entry, no systemd unit, callbeacon.py runs only at boot - so a tool that
       stopped piBeacon and then exited through an exception or a ctrl-c would leave the rpi dead
       until somebody noticed. Ctrl-c during a measurement is not hypothetical, it happened while
       testing this. "stop=no" is for a caller that already stopped piBeacon and restarts it itself:
       receiveCommands passes it, because otherwise the tool would restart master at the end of its
       own run, master.py would wipe temp/ on startup, and the structured result would be gone before
       receiveCommands had read it.
   RPI: the firmware installer believed its own verification too early, and the fleet rollout showed
       it: "PATCH LOADED, firmware build now 0000" on two rpis - 0000 being exactly the UNPATCHED rom
       build - and "has not confirmed the patch yet" on two more. The probe prints three lines
       SECONDS apart (build 0000, then "'brcm/..hcd' Patch", then the new build), and the check
       looked once right after the bind and took the LAST "build NNNN" in the window, which was
       still the pre-patch one. Measured on an rpi afterwards: the Patch line at 15.18 s and the new
       build at 16.53 s, 1.35 s later. It now WAITS for the confirmation, reads the build number only
       from AFTER the Patch line, and distinguishes the three outcomes instead of calling them all
       success: loaded (build changed), sent but still reporting build 0000 (did NOT take), and never
       reported at all.
       AND IT SELF-HEALS. The old "already installed - nothing to do" asked only whether the FILE was
       there, so an rpi whose re-probe had not taken kept its file, kept its rom firmware, and would
       have reported "nothing to do" for ever. Being installed and being LOADED are now two separate
       questions: if the dongle is present and the kernel's last word on it was still "Patch file not
       found", it is re-probed again on the next master start, without reinstalling the file and
       without claiming it was missing.
       Worth knowing for the fleet: once the .hcd is in /lib/firmware/brcm/ the kernel loads it at
       EVERY probe by itself - an rpi that showed one of those messages is patched after its next
       reboot regardless, measured on rpi-1: "'brcm/BCM20702A1-0a5c-21e8.hcd' Patch" at 15.4 s into
       the boot, build 1764 at 16.7 s, with no program involved at all.
   PLUGIN: the dongle qualification menu can now do ALL ble rpis in one go - "Qualify the BLE dongles
       of one / all RPI..". The rpi field uses filterPiDInclAll, so it gains an "all" entry; sensor
       rpis are not in that list at all (the field filters on "blue") and never will be, which is
       what we want - they have no bluetooth to qualify and stopping their master for a measurement
       would cost beacons for nothing.
       ONE AT A TIME, never together. Every rpi STOPS its own master/beaconloop/BLEconnect for the
       run, so firing the fleet off at once would leave every room blind at the same moment. Each
       rpi is given a slot (sized for THREE adapters, the common case - the one-adapter estimate
       shown in the dialog would overlap the runs if it were used as the spacing) and sleeps it out
       on the rpi BEFORE it stops anything, so the gaps never overlap. The wait does not count
       against the tool's 600 s cap, which measures the run. The silent-rpi suppression covers each
       rpi's wait AND its run (delay + 660 s), or an rpi far down the queue would be "rescued" by
       checkifNewSSD, reconfigured and rebooted while it was still waiting its turn.
       The dialog says how long the whole sweep will take: ten rpis at 10 s per phase with a 10x
       connect test is about 77 minutes, the same ten without a connect test about 16.
       The parameter channel stays backwards compatible - the old "secs;mac;tries" form still parses,
       with no delay.
   RPI: a radio that was NOT THERE was still given a role. The fleet sweep printed "ROLES: connect"
       for an onboard radio that had dropped off the bus mid-run: every measurement below returns
       zero for a dead adapter, so it collected "no BLE5", "scan too slow" and "cannot advertise" as
       though those were findings about the dongle, and the ACL MTU fallback at the bottom then
       handed it "connect" from a fingerprint taken BEFORE it vanished. verdict() now returns no
       roles and ONE note for an adapter that vanished or never came up, and the report says "none
       judged - the radio was not on the bus" instead of "NONE - do not use this dongle", which is a
       verdict on hardware that was never measured.
       The disappearance hint is bus-aware too: the onboard UART radio is not on the usb bus at all,
       so "usb re-enumeration?" and "dmesg | grep usb" were the wrong advice for exactly the case
       that produced it - an rpi whose B8:27:EB onboard radio disappeared while that same rpi was
       reporting under-voltage and current throttling. For a UART radio it now points at the
       bluetooth service and at power ('vcgencmd get_throttled').
   PLUGIN: the plugin rebooted the rpi in the middle of its own measurement. An rpi running the
       dongle test is deliberately quiet for minutes, checkifNewSSD saw an rpi not heard from for
       2*90 s, decided it was broken and ran "configure rpi + reboot" on it. qualifyDongleCALLBACKmenu
       now pushes that rpi's lastSSDCheck past the end of the run (600 s cap + 60 s), and says so in
       the log; a negative "silent for" is the signal that the silence was announced in advance.

   PLUGIN: a dead master is now answered with a RESTART, two minutes in, not with a reconfigure and a
       reboot ten minutes in. When master.sh/master.py die they take the whole rpi with them and
       nothing on the rpi brings them back - there is no cron entry, no systemd unit, and
       callbeacon.py runs only at boot - so the rpi stays down until something external acts. The
       first stage of checkifNewSSD now just sends restartmasterSSH after 120 s of silence, at most
       once per window and only to an rpi that still answers a ping. The existing
       "configure + reboot" path is unchanged and still handles an rpi that is quiet after it.

v2022.194.16  @ 2026-10-05
   RPI: the adapter fallback gave each ALTERNATE radio exactly one attempt while the primary got
       ENOSYS_RETRIES - and ENOSYS is precisely the error that clears by itself: it usually means a
       stale LE link still holds the controller's single initiator slot, which the diagnostic prints
       ("links: LE <mac> handle:64 state:1 out"). Live: the primary radio failed with a 40s
       create-connection timeout, the fallback hit ENOSYS on the good radio, gave up, and the job
       failed - while 0.4s later that same radio connected that same curtain in another job. The
       alternate now gets a second try 0.5s later on ENOSYS only; a timeout will not clear in 0.5s
       and is not retried.

   RPI: moving the BLEconnect role in the device dialog only half worked. beaconloop restarts itself
       when a pin changes and republishes the assignment, but BLEconnect reads beaconloop.hci once,
       at STARTUP - so it kept using the old radio indefinitely. Live: the pins were flipped,
       beaconloop restarted and published connect onto the other dongle, and BLEconnect carried on
       with the previous one for half an hour, which made the change look as if it had not worked.
       BLEconnect now checks the published role every 30 s and restarts to follow it - never while a
       switchbot command or a beep holds a session, and only towards a radio that is really present
       and UP, so a stale file cannot restart-loop it. (The restart passes python3=usePython3: the
       default is False, which would have relaunched it under python2 - no pybluez there any more
       and the ATT engine needs py3 sockets, so it would have come back crippled.)

   RPI: an rpi could report NO beacons at all for as long as it stayed up, restarting its bluetooth
       stack every ~35 s for ever. The no-message recovery only ever RESET the scan radio
       (HCIDEVRESET), and that does not clear every controller. Measured on an ASUS USB-BT500
       (Realtek 0b05:190e) that had previously been doing extended scanning: it accepted LE Set Scan
       Parameters and LE Set Scan Enable with status 0 - everything looked perfect - and delivered
       zero reports, through reset after reset. Setting the LE event mask did not help, nor did
       disabling advertising. A full down+up fixed it instantly: 298 reports from 42 devices in 8 s,
       same commands, same radio, seconds later.
       So after the first fruitless restart the scan radio is now taken DOWN and UP instead of
       reset - AND the radio is remembered. The first version only worked around it once: it did the
       down+up and then called startBlueTooth, whose normalStartHCI resets the radio on its way in
       and promptly re-muted it, so the log showed one recovery and then the same silence. The two
       were fighting each other once per restart. normalStartHCI now honours that per-radio flag and
       starts such a radio with down+up as well. Only radios that have actually shown the fault take
       that path; a reset stays the default everywhere else.
       AND IT NO LONGER COSTS A MINUTE OF SILENCE. Two things made it slow: the flag lived only in
       memory, so every program restart re-learned the fault the hard way, and the escalation waited
       for a SECOND fruitless restart before trying the stronger action. The fault is now written
       down BY MAC (temp/hciMuteOnReset.json - hci numbering changes across a power cycle or a swap,
       as this rpi showed twice today) and read back as soon as the radio inventory is known, so
       such a radio is started with down+up from the outset. The escalation also fires on the FIRST
       fruitless restart: by then a reset has already demonstrably failed to produce a single
       message, so waiting bought nothing and cost a full extra cycle.
       The "went mute after a reset before" line was logged at logLevelStart, which is 10 whenever
       thisHCI is passed - and the restart path always passes it. So the one line proving the
       workaround was in effect could never be seen in the log. Level 20 now. Only the "socket" acquisition path was affected - the hcidump path already asked for a
       hard reset. Without this the rpi kept answering pings, kept running, kept reporting its own
       state, and simply never saw a single beacon: the curtains moved on command (that goes over a
       different radio) while no position ever came back, which is exactly how it was noticed.

   RPI: the radio that WORKS is now remembered across restarts, and one that keeps failing is no
       longer tried first for ever. ENOSYS marks a radio bad outright, but the radio that caused all
       of this failed with plain create-connection TIMEOUTS, which are never a verdict on their own
       - so every single job paid its 40s on that radio before the fallback rescued it, and a job
       whose retry budget (3) had already been spent on earlier attempts reported "could not
       connect" to indigo although the fix was one radio away. Seen live twice.
       Now: two consecutive no-link failures on a radio and a radio PROVEN to connect is used first
       instead, and the proven mac is written to temp/BLEconnect.goodRadio so a restart does not
       re-learn it the hard way. It stays a PREFERENCE applied after the assigned radio has failed -
       never an override of the role the master assigned.
       (The first version of the reader called U.readFile, which does not exist - only writeFile
       does. The NameError was swallowed by its own except and the memory would have stayed empty
       for ever in silence. Caught by round-tripping it: write, forget, recall, plus empty and
       corrupt input.)
   RPI: BLEconnect ignored the radio role the master assigned it after a POWER CYCLE, because the
       role was matched by hci NAME. hci numbering is not stable across a power cycle or a dongle
       swap: beaconloop.hci still named "hci2", that matched no radio, so BLEconnect fell through to
       its local pick and bound to a radio the master had deliberately left out - one that cannot
       originate LE connections. Every switchbot command then failed with a 40s create-connection
       timeout (ECONNREFUSED/ECONNABORTED) while the radio the master HAD chosen sat beside it
       scanning happily and reporting messages normally. Seen live: published role mac
       5C:F3:70:6D:D8:E9, BLEconnect binding BC:FC:E7:26:07:5D.
       It now follows the published MAC and only uses the name as a fallback, which is what
       beaconloop already does for pinned radios - a mac identifies a radio whatever it is called
       after the next boot.

   RPI: the CONNECT role could be auto-picked onto the radio that is actually SCANNING. Live log:
       "BLEconnect role PINNED to hci2 (auto pick was hci1)" while hci1 was the scan radio - and
       connecting on the scan radio stalls the scan, after which beaconloop restarts its own stack
       over "no messages", forever. The pick ran with no exclusion at all; selectHCI cannot work it
       out for itself because it only re-derives a scan CANDIDATE from the radio list and never
       learns which radio this beaconloop really took, least of all when a dialog pin overrode the
       auto pick. It now excludes the real scan radio, and falls back to sharing it only when
       nothing else is UP - which is the correct 1-radio behaviour, arbitrated by the
       beaconloop.pause handshake (refusing it outright left BLEconnect with no radio at all,
       "BLE STACK is not UP", seen on pi#15).
   RPI: a switchbot closing the link mid-command logged a six-line traceback
       (ConnectionAbortedError from the pending ATT write) for what is simply the curtain doing as
       it was told. One line now - "ATT link dropped while sending ... the device closed the
       connection, retrying with a fresh session" - and the stale session is closed so the retry
       opens a clean one. Unexpected exceptions still get the full traceback.

   RPI: RADIO ROLE ASSIGNMENT - two ordering faults, both found from live logs, both fixed. This is
       what was really behind the switchbot failures, and it explains "it worked before" too.
       1) CONNECT preferred a CLONE dongle over sharing the scan radio. One rpi ran for weeks with
          "auto-select role:connect -> hci1 (aclMTU:310)" - the clone signature - and every
          switchbot command died on a 40s create-connection timeout while the scan radio beside it
          connected the same curtain in 0.4s. The code already classified clones and its own
          comment said they "cannot reliably open LE connections"; the order just did not act on
          it. A clone now ranks BELOW sharing the scan radio: sharing costs scan time, a radio
          that cannot originate links costs the whole job.
       2) a BLE5+BLE4 dongle (ASUS-class) was parked at the very back with the BLE5-ONLY ones, so
          connect fell onto the scan radio - and beaconloop then restarted its stack over "no
          messages", because connecting on the scan radio stalls scanning. A dual-mode radio
          connects fine; it only has to stay free when it is the ONLY radio that could do extended
          listening. Order is now: internal > good external > BLE5+BLE4 (while a BLE5-ONLY radio
          exists for the listener) > share the scan radio > clone > BLE5-only.
       And the listener role now goes to a BLE5-ONLY radio FIRST. Candidates used to be taken in
       dict order, so with two BLE5-capable radios the DUAL-MODE one could win it and leave the
       BLE5-only dongle - which can do nothing else, BLE4 scan answers 0x0C - with no job, while
       the role it was holding was the only decent connect radio on the rpi.
       Checked against both real configurations of the rpi that showed this: 3 radios (broadcom +
       dual-mode + BLE5-only) now gives scan=broadcom, connect=dual-mode, listener=BLE5-only; the
       2-radio september layout (broadcom + clone) now shares the scan radio instead of handing
       connect to the clone.

   MAC+RPI: the radio-role PINS in the rpi dialog never worked - three faults in one chain, all fixed.
       1) the "BLEconnect + iphone" option was only ever offered on the INTERNAL radio, so an rpi
          whose radios are ALL USB dongles could not assign that role at all: the menu showed the
          scanner options and "ignore" and nothing else. The strategy is unchanged - the internal
          radio is where connect belongs, its scanning fights wifi while connects tolerate it, and
          it is still the only radio offered the role whenever one exists. A USB radio is now
          offered it only when the rpi has no internal radio to give it to.
       2) nothing on the MAC side ever built the parameter the rpi reads. The dialog wrote one prop
          per radio (hciRole_hci0 ...), beaconloop reads one "hciRolesPinned" dict, and no code
          bridged the two - so every pin ever set was silently discarded and each rpi always used
          its own auto pick. makeParametersFile now sends the dict ("auto" left out: absent means
          the rpi decides, which is also what older rpi code does with an unknown key).
       3) radio roles are assigned ONCE, at beaconloop startup, so a pin arriving later was stored
          and changed nothing. beaconloop now restarts when the pins really change - not on the
          first read of the parameters file, where "differs from {}" only means it was just read.
       Measured on the rpi that started this: its connect dongle accepts 13.8 adverts/s against the
       scan dongle's 47.6, and hears 22 of 40 devices in 10s against 40 of 40. A radio that misses
       45% of the devices around it is a poor place for create-connection, which has to catch an
       advert and answer inside ~150us.
   RPI: "version: undefined v1.0" in the log - the version line named no program, which is its only
       job. Several programs call U.setLogging() BEFORE assigning G.program, and "undefined" is that
       global's default. It now falls back to the calling MODULE's own filename, which is always
       right and needs no cooperation from the program; master is still recognised as master (and
       still publishes the shared module versions) when it has not set G.program yet.

   MAC: an rpi reading "on" in the device list instead of "up" - FOUND, two changes. The wording
       indigo renders is the uiValue of a state, and a uiValue is only written when that state is
       written. For rpi/beacon/BLEconnect the "up" uiValue is written by the status path, and only
       when status CHANGES - so on a pi that just keeps running, nothing ever refreshes it.
       What put "on" there: applyOnOffDisplay, called after ANY device-config save. It ran the on/off
       scheme over these devices too, and they have no onOffui* props, so getpropForonOffText
       returned its historical default "on" and that went into the onOffState uiValue. The next
       restart rebuilt displayStatus from status, so displayStatus read "up" while the uiValue -
       what is actually on screen - still read "on", and it stayed that way until the pi next went
       down and came back. The two bulk passes already skipped this family; the config-save caller
       did not. It now returns early for them: their display belongs to the status path.
       startupFIXES1 repairs the ones already poisoned - it rebuilt displayStatus but never the
       uiValue - by forcing the padded up|down string onto onOffState and status at every startup.
       Diagnosed from "print rPi dev states": displayStatus said "up" on all 14 pis while
       onOffState.ui said "on" on exactly the two that looked wrong. That table now also prints
       "*displayStateId" and "*shownInColumn", the two facts no state carries.
       Cosmetic throughout - status, triggers, groups and positions read the state VALUE, which was
       right all along. The pis were up and sending normally.

   MAC: the plugin repairs pngquant's EXECUTABLE BIT at startup instead of relying on a copy to
       preserve it. It arrived as -rw-r--r-- on the indigo server after the bundle was copied there,
       so every plot compression failed with "Permission denied" - and silently, because the plot
       scripts just keep the uncompressed png (which is what they do now; before today they deleted
       the png first and lost the image entirely).
       Copy tools, archives, sync clients and macOS all have ways of dropping that bit, and a bundle
       gets copied far more often than anyone reads a readme, so fixBundledExecutables() checks it
       on every startup and chmods +x when needed. No password required - it is our own file. It
       logs ONLY when it had to change something, and says "MISSING from the plugin bundle - a
       partial copy?" when the file is not there at all.
       The quarantine flag is the other half of the same problem and was already handled next to
       this call; that one does need sudo, so the two stay separate.
       Tested: a 644 copy is raised to 755 and reported, a second run is silent, and a missing file
       is reported rather than raising.
   MAC: an rPi that does not answer ssh is now diagnosed as DOWN or HANGING, and said so in the log.
       The two look identical to anything that only tried ssh, and they need opposite responses: a
       down rPi comes back by itself or needs power, a HUNG one sits there for ever until something
       hard-resets it. The case that prompted this: a reboot that does not complete because a
       program - display.py, say - is still holding on, so "sudo reboot" never finishes. The kernel
       keeps answering ICMP while sshd is already gone.
       SEQUENCE: ping, wait 30 s, RE-TEST SSH, ping again.
         no ping and no ssh          -> "IS DOWN: no ping, no ssh ... powered off, crashed, or off
                                        the network"
         ping answers, ssh does not  -> "IS HANGING, NEEDS A HARD RESET ... typically a reboot that
                                        did not complete because a program was still running"
       The SSH RE-TEST after 30 s is what keeps this quiet shortly after a boot, when ssh is briefly
       unavailable and nothing is wrong: if ssh is back by then the rPi was simply still starting,
       and that is logged as information instead of as a fault. Two pings 30 s apart rather than one
       so a single dropped packet or a momentary wifi gap is not reported as a dead rPi.
       NON-BLOCKING, because it has to be: it waits 30 s and the callers of testPing are in the
       middle of pushing files to every rPi in turn. It runs in a daemon thread and testPing returns
       at once, exactly as before. Hooked inside testPing itself rather than at its three call
       sites, so every caller gets it and none of them changed.
       ONE VERDICT PER rPi PER 10 MINUTES, and the limit gates the THREAD, not just the log line -
       otherwise a push loop would start a fresh diagnosis on every attempt.
       AND ICMP IS BACK, in one place and for one reason. testPing itself still does NOT ping: it
       asks what its callers need, "can I ssh to this rpi". That ping answers a different question
       is exactly what makes it useful HERE - ping alive plus ssh dead is a specific, nameable
       state. Raw ICMP needs root, so pingHost goes through /sbin/ping; there is no unprivileged
       stdlib way.
       Tested by driving the real functions with a stubbed ping and ssh: all four outcomes (down,
       hung on both pings, hung on one ping, back-within-30 s) produce the right line, a second call
       inside 10 minutes logs nothing, and a call after 10 minutes runs again.

v2022.194.15  @ 2026-10-04
   MAC: the three plot scripts, and one of them had a bug that destroyed its own output.
       makeCameraPlot DELETED THE PNG BEFORE KNOWING THE COMPRESSED FILE EXISTED: it removed the
       original, then renamed the .xxx over it, unguarded, in a script with no try/except anywhere
       near. So a pngquant that was missing, not executable or simply unhappy with the image left
       NO image at all, and a traceback as the only sign. pngquant --ext .xxx writes its output
       ALONGSIDE the original, so the rename alone is enough and nothing needs deleting first -
       which is exactly what makeLidar360Plot already did correctly, so that shape was borrowed.
       makeBeaconPositionPlots had the same delete-first order; its outer try/except meant the
       failure was logged rather than fatal, but the image was lost just the same. Fixed the same way.
       A stale .xxx is now removed BEFORE compressing too, or it would be renamed over the new png.
       THE DYNAMIC-WINDOW CENTRE WAS WRONG in makeCameraPlot: "vmid = (mm-ma)/2. + mm" is
       mm - (ma-mm)/2, ie BELOW the minimum of the frame. For a frame spanning 18..34 it put the
       centre at 10.0, so a +-5 window was 5..15 and every pixel sat above it - the image saturated
       to one colour and the setting only ever washed the picture out. Now (mm+ma)/2.
       AND "pnGsize": "except: pnGsize = 0" in makeCameraPlot and makeBeaconPositionPlots, where
       the next line logs pngSize - so a missing file raised NameError instead of logging 0.0. Two
       files, same typo.
       Plus the unused imports pyflakes flagged (patches/numpy/cm in camera, patches/copy in lidar),
       a no-op module-level "global logging, logger", and the .encode('utf8') py2 leftovers on the
       paths that were touched. All three are pyflakes-clean now.
   MAC: DELETED the stale duplicates Server Plugin/ruuviPrint.py, irRecord.py and toshibaIR.py.
       All three also live in Server Plugin/pi/, which is the authoritative copy and the only
       directory the push sends, and all three mac-side copies had drifted behind: irRecord 15 KB
       against 37 KB, ruuviPrint 7 KB against 13 KB, toshibaIR 21.0 against 21.6 KB. Nothing on the
       mac imports them and the only mention in plugin.py is a docstring that already says
       "pi/irRecord.py", so they were a trap rather than a backup - hand-editing the mac-side copy
       changes nothing on any rpi and nothing says so.
   MAC: and makeCameraPlot's min/max sentinels are gone. It started from ma = -999 / mm = +999 and
       max/min'ed against them, so a frame with any reading below -999 kept mm = -999 and the window
       centre - the whole displayed contrast range - was computed from a number that is not in the
       data. min/max of the data itself cannot be wrong at any scale.
       An EMPTY frame is now caught and falls back to the fully dynamic scale: max() of an empty
       sequence raises ValueError, which in this script means no image at all.
       Checked across all five cases: a normal 18..34 frame gives centre 26 and a +-5 window of
       21..31; frames entirely below -999 and entirely above +999 now scale correctly; a single
       empty row is ignored; all-rows-empty takes the fallback instead of raising.

   MAC: the rejected/ feature is REMOVED - it was dead - and five shell calls in the plugin were
       broken in the same way as beaconloop's "hciconfig restart".
       FIVE QUOTED GLOBS, none of which ever did anything: "rm '<dir>/rejected/rejects*'" (twice),
       "mv '<dir>/reject*' '<dir>/rejected'", "rm '<dir>/param*'" and "rm '<dir>/interfa*'". The
       glob sits INSIDE the single quotes, so the shell never expands it and the command looks for a
       file literally named "rejects*". Demonstrated rather than assumed: three matching files
       survive the quoted form and all vanish with the unquoted one. subprocess.call discards the
       exit status, so each failed silently - the rejected/ housekeeping never ran once.
       THE FEATURE IS DEAD, so the rotation, the cleanup and the mkdir are gone rather than fixed:
       nothing in the plugin ever WROTE a rejected/rejects file - only the rotation and the mkdir
       ever named them. updateRejects.py was the only reader of such files, so with no producer left
       it no longer ships either - it and testps.py, which nothing imported or launched, are gone.
       The param*/interfa* pair belonged to a migration from an old layout. Removed rather than
       repaired: repairing it would delete real files for the first time, years late.
       CONVERTED to python, with what each one was hiding:
         - the dataVersion rm was BACKGROUNDED with "&", so neither its result nor its failure was
           observable; now os.remove, and a failure is logged.
         - "/bin/kill -9 <self>" -> os.kill(pid, 9). The literal 9 because signal is not imported
           in this file and adding an import for one call is not worth it.
         - MAC2Vendor's shell mkdir -> os.makedirs, which can also make parents (the shell mkdir
           without -p could not).
       AND testPing NO LONGER PINGS. Every caller asks it one question - "can I ssh/sftp to this
       rpi" - and ICMP answers a different one: it can be filtered while sshd is fine, and more to
       the point an rpi mid-boot or with a wedged sshd answers ping happily while every ssh attempt
       then times out. It is a TCP connect to port 22 now, which is the actual precondition, and it
       drops two shell+fork per check on a path that runs for every rpi. The two-stage timing is
       kept in spirit: 0.25 s, then 1.5 s, so a busy rpi is not written off on 40 ms.
       VERIFIED against a listening sshd (returns 0 immediately) and an unroutable address (returns
       1 after ~1.9 s). NOT verified against an rpi: this mac's LAN access was flapping at the time
       - ssh and a raw socket both got EHOSTUNREACH for a pi that was up - so loopback and a
       blackhole address stood in.
       Still shelled out on the mac side, deliberately: curl|tail|cut x3 in MAC2Vendor (a genuine
       pipeline in a maintenance utility) and the 13 .exp expect scripts, which are their own
       decision.

   RPI: the rest of the BLE cli, and a survey of every other shelled-out command in pi/*.py.
       BEACONLOOP'S 14 hciconfig CALLS now go through the ioctls too, via a one-line local wrapper
       (hciDev -> U.hciDevCtl) so all of them log the same way: the hard-reset path, normalStartHCI,
       reuseHCI, the scan-socket reopen, BLEAnalysis, the beep HCI picker and the extended-scan
       probe. Only BLEconnect had been converted before; beaconloop is where most of them were.

       usage message and did nothing. A recovery path that only looked like one, and it survived
       because the output was never tested. Both are now down+up, which is what the rest of the file
       does for a restart and what was meant.
       normalStartHCI's RETRIES were decided on TEXT - "RF-kill" anywhere in the reset output, and a
       non-empty stderr after the up. They are decided on the return value now, which is strictly
       better: it covers rfkill (the kernel answers ERFKILL and hciDevCtl logs it by name) and every
       other reason the command did not take, which the string match let through silently.
       ONE DEAD SITE LEFT DEAD: the beep path had 'ret = ["",""] # readPopen(cmd)', ie a reset
       somebody deliberately disabled, with a "redo reset" test behind it that could never fire.
       Turning it back on would be a behaviour change, not a cleanup, so only the dead scaffolding
       is gone and the comment says why.
       NOT CONVERTED, and flagged instead: hciconfig noleadv / noscan / leadv 3 (two sites). Those
       are not adapter up/down/reset but advertising and scan control, so they need HCI commands
       rather than the device ioctls - the same treatment startHCIBroadCast got, but on paths that
       interact with the scan/broadcast state machine. Worth doing deliberately, not in passing.
   RPI: net-tools. The survey's surprise was how little of it is live: ifconfig, route and one
       iwgetid reader all sit inside an "else: # pre os v 8" branch, and the four interface up/down
       sites are already gated "if osVersion < 8" with an "ip link set" branch - so on a current
       image none of that code runs. Exactly two sites were ungated:
         - master's "ifconfig wlan0 down; ifconfig wlan0 up" wifi cycle, on every os version. On a
           current image, where net-tools is not installed by default, that cycle did nothing at
           all. Now U.ifUpDown(), which uses "ip link set" and keeps ifconfig as the fallback.
         - the SSID read via iwgetid (wireless-tools, also not installed by default). iwgetid stays
           as the primary and "iw dev wlan0 link" is the fallback, parsed on the "SSID:" LINE only -
           the full output also contains "Connected to xx:xx:.." and a find(":") over all of it
           would have picked up the bssid instead.
       CORRECTION, because I claimed a breakage that is not one: net-tools and wireless-tools ARE
       installed on this fleet - checked on an rpi, net-tools 2.10-1.3 and wireless-tools

       earlier "MISSING" readings came from "command -v" in a non-interactive ssh shell, where
       /sbin is not on PATH. Both changes are FORWARD-LOOKING (net-tools is no longer part of a
       default Debian install), not repairs.
       AND NO OUTPUT PARSING WAS ADOPTED, which is what makes the ip swap safe: measured on an rpi,
       ip and ifconfig both print NOTHING on success and both write to stderr on failure ("Cannot
       find device" vs "ERROR while getting interface flags"), so ifUpDown only has to test whether
       stderr is empty - it never has to understand either wording. The ssid fallback is the one
       place a format IS parsed, and it is parsed on iw's "SSID:" line specifically. The pre-os-8
       branches keep their ifconfig/route parsers and the os>=8 branches keep their ip parsers; the
       two are not mixed.
