   RPI/MAC: piNeedsFineGpioRate now walks the DEVICE LIST instead of self.RPI[piU]["input"]/
       ["output"], and without this the IR fix above would not have worked at all.
       self.RPI's input/output dicts are written only by the validateDeviceConfigUi_* callbacks, and
       only for some types: OUTPUTthermostatIRac is never put there. makeParametersFile reaches it by
       iterating indigo.devices instead - which is why the rpi's parameters file lists the IR output
       while self.RPI does not. So the first implementation missed exactly the one device that most
       needs the fine rate, and rpi38 would have come back from reload+push still on 10 us with its
       IR still dead. Found by checking the data path instead of trusting that the parameters file
       and self.RPI say the same thing - they do not.
       It now uses the same two facts makeParametersFile uses: dev.deviceTypeId, and the rPi from
       piServerNumber / RPINumber. Verified that every fine-rate type carries piServerNumber,
       OUTPUTthermostatIRac included.
       VERIFIED by running the real method against the fleet's actual device sets, read from each
       rpi's own parameters file:
         rpi2  5 -> 5    rpi5  5 -> 5    rpi0  5 -> 10
         rpi1  10 -> 10  rpi4  10 -> 10  rpi38 10 -> 5   <- the IR fix, now actually reached
       Both IR rpis (5 and 38) resolve to 5, and a DISABLED device does not force the fine rate.

   RPI: OUTPUTthermostatIRac added to _GlobalConst_pigpioFineRateTypes, UNCONDITIONALLY, and this
       was a LIVE BREAKAGE for the ~5 minutes between an rpi reboot and finding it: rpi38 came up
       with pigpiod -s 10 and has an IR AC output, so its IR sending was dead.
       IR is the sharpest timing case of all and it is NOT PWM, so the GPIOpwm pref does not cover
       it: irReplay.send() builds the 38 kHz carrier from pigpio.pulse() pairs of 13 us on / 13 us
       off, and pigpio realises wave delays as MULTIPLES OF THE SAMPLE RATE. At 10 us a 13 us pulse
       becomes 10 or 20 us, the carrier lands between 25 and 50 kHz, and the receiver's bandpass
       around 38 kHz rejects it. IR is one way with no readback, so nothing logs an error - the AC
       simply stops responding. At 5 us it comes out near 38 kHz, which is what has always worked.
       My classification error: I built the list from "what uses pigpio for timing" and filed IR as
       an output rather than as a waveform. Found because the rpi owner asked whether the gpio-out
       IR leds on .38 should go to 10 us - they should not.
       Noted for later, not changed: even 5 us quantises 13 us to 15 us, ie ~33 kHz rather than 38.
       It has always worked at 5 us so it stays there, but if IR range is ever marginal, -s 1 gives
       an exact 38.5 kHz.
   RPI: and OUTPUTgpio-1-ONoff REMOVED from _GlobalConst_pigpioPwmCapableTypes, a second error in
       the same list and in the opposite direction. It is declared <Device type="relay">, so indigo
       only ever sends it on or off - there is no brightness to become a duty cycle. Keeping it there
       meant an rpi whose only gpio output is an ON/off relay paid the fine rate for a dimmer it does
       not have, which is precisely what rpi0 was doing, with pigpiod as its single largest consumer.
       OUTPUTgpio-1 (<Device type="dimmer">) is the real PWM one. The deprecated multi-pin OUTPUTgpio
       types stay: they are "custom" types whose actions are not constrained the way a relay's are.
       PREDICTED AGAINST THE REAL FLEET, by reading each rpi's configured device types out of its own
       parameters file and running the decision on them:
         rpi2  5 -> 5   fine: ultrasoundDistance
         rpi5  5 -> 5   fine: OUTPUTthermostatIRac
         rpi0  5 -> 10  nothing timing-critical  (was only the ON/off relay)
         rpi1  10 -> 10 nothing timing-critical
         rpi4  10 -> 10 nothing timing-critical
         rpi38 10 -> 5  fine: OUTPUTthermostatIRac   <- the IR fix
       So after a reload+push: two rpis change, both in the right direction, and the four 5/10
       values that are already right stay put. Each needs its pigpiod restarted (or the rpi
       rebooted) for the new rate to apply - a running daemon keeps the arguments it started with.

   RPI: every program now has a VERSION and reports it, with the versions of the SHARED modules it
       actually loaded, in one line at startup:
           version: display.py v1.0  [utils 1.0, hciRawSocket 1.1, gattAttClient 1.5]
       The shared modules are the point. A program is only as current as piBeaconUtils,
       hciRawSocket and gattAttClient on the same rpi, and a partial file send leaves a MIX - which
       has bitten this code three times, each time as behaviour nobody could explain for hours. One
       line at startup turns that into a grep.
       NOT 81 NEW CALLS: setLogging() does it, because 81 programs already call that as their first
       act. So no program has to remember, and none can forget. The version is read from the
       CALLER's VERSION global via sys._getframe, so nothing is passed in and nothing can be passed
       wrongly. U.versionLine() builds the string and U.logVersion() logs it for anywhere that wants
       it again later (eg after importing more modules - the list only shows what is loaded at the
       time, which the test below shows).
       VERSION ADDED TO 97 FILES, placed in the top section. Found by AST, not by line number: the
       insertion point is the first top-level statement that is not the module docstring, backing up
       over the comment block that belongs to it.
       TWO THINGS CAUGHT BY CHECKING AFTERWARDS RATHER THAN ASSUMING:
         - 28 files got it on LINE 1, ABOVE THE SHEBANG, because the back-up-over-comments walk ran
           past "#!/usr/bin/env python" - which is only honoured on line 1, so those programs would
           have lost their interpreter line. Moved below the shebang and the coding line; re-checked
           that all 103 files with a shebang still have it on line 1.
         - smbus2.py is NOT versioned: it is third-party (MIT, Karl-Petter Lindegaard), not ours to
           stamp. 110 of 111 files now carry a VERSION.
       ONCE PER PROCESS, and that needed its own flag: setLogging() has no entry guard and MOST
       programs call it two or three times (display.py, INPUTpulse.py and ~20 others call it three
       times), so the line would have appeared 2-3x per program start. G.loggerSet cannot serve - it
       is set at the END of setLogging and nothing reads it on entry, which is why those repeat
       calls have gone unnoticed all along.
       AND ONLY MASTER LISTS THE SHARED MODULES. They are a property of the rpi, not of the process:
       all 7 programs on rpi2 load the same piBeaconUtils.py / hciRawSocket.py / gattAttClient.py,
       so 7 lines saying "utils 1.0" state one fact 7 times. master prints them and publishes them
       to temp/moduleVersions; every other program prints its own version only.
       The one case where a process REALLY can differ is kept: a program that started BEFORE an
       upgrade keeps the old module in memory until restarted. sharedVersionMismatch() compares this
       process's loaded versions against what master published and logs, eg,
       "SHARED MODULE MISMATCH ... utils: mine 1.0 vs master 1.1 -> restart it". Only modules BOTH
       have loaded are compared, so a sensor that never imports gattAttClient is not called stale.
       All 111 files were parse-checked after the edit, and the behaviour was run on rpi2:
       master printed "master.py v1.0 [utils 1.0, hciRawSocket 1.1, gattAttClient 1.5]", a sensor
       printed "simplei2csensors.py v2.3" with no shared list and exactly once despite three
       setLogging calls, and with master's published list doctored to utils 1.1 the mismatch line
       fired naming the module and both versions. Test files removed from the pi afterwards.

   RPI PROGRAM VERSIONS for the socket/ioctl work above: beaconloop 21.4 -> 21.5
       (startHCIBroadCast by socket), BLEconnect 12.0 -> 12.1 (hciconfig/hcitool-con ioctls, real
       txPower, ATTCLIENT_MINVERSION 1.5), gattAttClient 1.4 -> 1.5 (HCI Read Transmit Power Level).
       hciRawSocket NOW HAS a VERSION (1.1; 1.0 was the pybluez scan-socket subset, 1.1 adds the
       adapter ioctls) and a staleness check to go with it, because the file now carries code other
       programs depend on and a partial send has left a stale copy three times already.
       The gate gives BOTH tests: VERSION >= 1.1 AND hasattr(hci_dev_up)/hasattr(hci_connections) -
       a version number only helps when the file carrying it is the one that got sent. When it
       fails, hciRawPresent goes False, everything falls back to hciconfig/hcitool as before, and
       U.hciRawStale holds the complaint: logged by BLEconnect at startup (recovery may not run for
       hours, so a stale file should be visible before then) and once more on first actual use.
       TESTED against the real failure, which rpi2 was already in: with the OLD hciRawSocket.py on
       the pi the gate reported VERSION (none) -> 0.0, hci_dev_up False, hci_connections False,
       hciRawPresent False, and produced exactly the intended log line.
       Plugin version deliberately NOT bumped: it stays 2022.194.15.
   RPI: the pigpiod fine-rate type list, corrected after checking what each program ACTUALLY uses
       rather than what the device name suggests. The NEOPIXELS came out - neopixel2/3.py drive
       ws281x/PixelStrip, which does its own PWM/DMA and never touches pigpio, so pigpiod's sample
       rate cannot affect them. setStepperMotor came out too: no program drives it through pigpio.
       And the PWM case is no longer guessed from the device type: any gpio output pin can be given
       a duty cycle instead of a level, so it is decided by the GPIOpwm PREF combined with any
       OUTPUTgpio-* device (_GlobalConst_pigpioPwmCapableTypes) - an rpi with a plain ON/off relay
       does not pay for a dimmer it does not have. Fine rate is therefore: INPUTpulse,
       INPUTcoincidence, ultrasoundDistance (pigpio throughout, and the echo time IS the distance),
       plus PWM-driven gpio outputs. INPUTgpio-* stays on 10 us - buttons are milliseconds apart.
   RPI: VALIDATED ON rpi2 (192.168.1.22), from copies in /tmp that were deleted afterwards - a
       stale gattAttClient on an rpi is the failure this code has hit three times:
         - HCIDEVUP on hci0/hci1/hci2 -> EALREADY(114): the ioctl number is right and nothing
           changed. A nonexistent hci9 -> ENODEV(19), so errno propagation works.
         - hci_connections on a SETTLED link -> handle 0x000b VALID, mac 5C:AD:BA:16:C6:BE, ACL,
           state 1, out. Caught mid-page it returned handle 0x0F00, which is above the 12-bit HCI
           limit - the "connection still pending" garbage classicPresenceRSSI already guards for,
           not a parse error.
         - classicPresenceRSSI against the iPhone: page answered in 0.6 s, handle 0x000b, Read-RSSI
           -14 dBm, Read-TxPower 12 dBm, 5-byte answer decoded correctly. The field really was the
           handle low byte before: calcDist was being fed 11 instead of 12 dBm.
       HCIDEVRESET and HCIDEVDOWN are still untested - both would have taken a live adapter down
       (hci2 runs BLEconnect, hci0/hci1 are beaconloop's scan and BLE5 listener). Same
       _IOW('H', nr, int) derivation at consecutive numbers, and 201 proving right makes 202/203
       near-certain, but that is inference. A wrong number degrades to the hciconfig fallback with
       an errno in the log, not a silent failure.

   RPI: phone/watch presence now reads the REAL transmit power over the socket, so getting txPower
       back does not mean going back to hcitool.
       The field was never a transmit power on the socket path: BLEconnect filled "txPower" from
       byte 1 of the Read-RSSI command-complete, which is the ACL HANDLE LOW BYTE - the comment in
       the code said so. hcitool's "tpl" read the real thing with a second HCI command, so that is
       what classicPresenceRSSI does now: HCI Read Transmit Power Level (opcode 0x0C2D = OGF 3,
       OCF 0x2D, params handle + type 0x00 = current) on the raw HCI socket it already has open,
       over the ACL link that is already up. One command round trip on an existing connection.
       APPENDED AS A 5TH BYTE, not substituted: the first four bytes keep the exact layout every
       existing caller unpacks, and BLEconnect reads byte 4 only when the answer is 5 bytes long.
       On an older gattAttClient the field is left at its -999 "unknown" default instead of
       reporting handleLo as if it were a power. gattAttClient 1.4 -> 1.5, and
       ATTCLIENT_MINVERSION with it, so a partial file send says so at startup as usual.
       The all-zero guard still tests the handle low byte, which is what it was always testing back
       when that byte was mislabelled txPower - an answer with handle 0 and rssi 0 is the "nothing
       really read" case.
       VERIFIED on the mac as far as it can be: the command packet encodes to 01 2d 0c 03 2a 00 00
       (opcode 0x0C2D, param len 3), a synthesised command-complete decodes +4, -12, +20 and -30
       dBm correctly as a signed byte, and a legacy 4-byte answer still unpacks with txPower falling
       back to -999. The actual HCI exchange needs a pi and a phone.

   RPI: startHCIBroadCast (the iBeacon broadcast) now sends its four HCI commands through
       bluez.hci_send_cmd instead of hcitool. These were the LAST hcitool commands that actually ran
       on a socket-mode rpi: LE Set Advertise Enable 00, LE Set Advertising Data, LE Set Advertising
       Parameters, LE Set Advertise Enable 01 - all plain HCI commands, which is what hci_send_cmd
       sends, on the backend beaconloop already imports for scanning.
       WHY: hcitool is deprecated upstream and already missing from some BlueZ builds, so on such an
       image the iBeacon broadcast just stopped, with nothing in the log saying so - every one of
       the four was sent with a trailing "&" and its output logged but never TESTED, so an HCI
       status of 0x0C (Command Disallowed) read exactly like success. The status byte is now read
       per command and a non-zero one names the command that failed, with 0x0C called out.
       The "&" is gone too: shell backgrounding meant the next command could be sent before the
       previous was processed, which is the likely reason the explicit "stop advertising first"
       workaround was needed at all. It is kept anyway - it costs one command and the comment
       recording why it exists stays true.
       The payload is byte-for-byte what the hcitool strings built, CHECKED rather than eyeballed:
       both byte lists were compared against the old format strings for pi=2 - adv data 32 bytes
       (1 length + 31 data), adv params 15 bytes, major 0x0001, minor = the rpi number. The magic
       numbers now carry their meaning in comments (flags AD, Apple 0x004C iBeacon type 2,
       ADV_NONCONN_IND, little-endian intervals 0x3000/0x4000, channel map 0x07).
       The hcitool path REMAINS as the fallback when bluezPresent is False (py2 without pybluez, or
       no AF_BLUETOOTH), building its hex from the same two byte lists so the two paths cannot drift
       apart. Same two-engine shape as the gatt code and the hciconfig ioctls.
       hcitool now runs ONLY in: tryToConnectCommandLine (classic BR/EDR presence, commandLine mode
       only), restartLESCAN (hcidump acquisition mode only), the py2 fallback in U.hciConnections,
       and scanRateTest.py, where comparing the old pipeline is the point.

   RPI: BLEconnect no longer shells out to hciconfig or hcitool for adapter control. All five
       hciconfig sites (two ATT-retry resets, the down/service-restart/up recovery, the down;up
       bounce when the adapter is found down) and both "hcitool con" diagnostics now go through
       ioctls on a raw HCI socket, in hciRawSocket.py beside the pybluez replacement that was
       already there: HCIDEVUP / HCIDEVDOWN / HCIDEVRESET and HCIGETCONNLIST, derived as
       _IOW/_IOR('H', nr, int) and written out so the numbers can be rechecked.
       WHY, and it is NOT cpu - these run on recovery, a handful of times an hour at worst:
         - hciconfig and hcitool are deprecated upstream and already missing from some BlueZ
           builds. On an image without them BLE recovery silently did nothing at all. Same
           argument that retired gatttool.
         - every one of those five sites threw the shell output AWAY, so a failed reset looked
           exactly like a successful one. The ioctl answers with an errno, which is the difference
           between "already up, fine" (EALREADY, counted as success, as hciconfig does) and "this
           dongle is gone" (ENODEV) on the one path that runs when BLE is already wedged. The
           down/up recovery now logs the result of each step.
         - ENETDOWN on a reset means the adapter is down, so hciDevCtl brings it UP instead, which
           is what the caller wanted. hciconfig papered over that case.
       U.hciDevCtl(hci, "up"/"down"/"reset") and U.hciConnections(hci) keep /bin/hciconfig and
       hcitool as the fallback: hciRawSocket is py3.5+ and needs AF_BLUETOOTH, so py2 rpis keep the
       old path - the same two-engine shape as the gatt code.
       TESTED as far as this mac allows: hciDevId parsing ("hci0", "hci11", " HCI2 ", 3, "0" and
       the rejects "wlan0", "", None) and the hci_conn_info layout round-tripped through
       struct.calcsize (16 bytes per entry, 4-byte header) with a two-link buffer decoding back to
       the right macs, handles and ACL/LE types. THE IOCTLS THEMSELVES ARE UNTESTED - they cannot
       run off Linux. First thing to watch on a pi: HCIDEVRESET on a DOWN adapter, which should log
       "was down, brought it up instead of resetting it" rather than failing.
       STILL CLI, deliberately: hcitool cc/rssi/tpl in tryToConnectCommandLine. That is classic
       BR/EDR phone presence, not BLE, and it only runs in commandLine mode, which these rpis do
       not use. Retiring it belongs with retiring gatttool and the gatttool command grammar that
       attExecGattCmd still parses - one decision, not three.

   MAC: second new menu button next to the state table: the pluginProps of all rPis, same layout -
       one line per prop name, one column per rPi, then the rPI-Sensor children, both rPi types.
       Props and states are deliberately TWO reports, not one merged table: props are what the user
       set, states are what the rpi reported back, and the two disagreeing is itself the thing worth
       seeing - a prop changed in the dialog that the rpi has not picked up yet.
       statesTable became keyValueTable and now takes DICTS instead of devices, since dev.states and
       dev.pluginProps are both just dicts to a table. One renderer for both reports and for both
       sections of each; the state report was re-rendered in the harness afterwards to confirm the
       refactor did not change it.

   RPI: pigpiod sat at a constant 11% of a core on an rpi with no gpio device in use. It samples
       every gpio on a fixed timer whether or not anything is listening, so it costs the same idle
       as busy, and the rate is the default -s 5 (5 us). All 11 start sites now pass -s 10, which
       halves the wakeups and so roughly halves the cost: master's startPigpiod, the safety net in
       INPUTRotarySwitchIncremental, and receiveCommands, tf, ultrasoundDistance, apds9960,
       Wire18B20, as3935, checkForIncl-py3. They ALL had to change: whichever one wins the race
       sets the rate for the whole system, so one missed site would quietly restore the default.
       What it costs: gpio edge timestamps and PWM/servo steps resolve to 10 us instead of 5 us.
       For what goes through pigpio here - buttons, rotary encoders, touch pads, an ultrasound
       echo - that is far finer than anything being measured; an encoder detent is milliseconds
       apart. Hardware PWM frequencies do shift with the sample rate, so if a neopixel or a servo
       ever looks wrong, this is the first thing to put back to 5.
       AND IT IS NOW A PER-rPi SETTING, in the device edit dialog of BOTH rPi types ("pigpiod gpio
       sample rate"), defaulting to AUTO: 5 us only when that rPi actually has a device whose
       measurement or output waveform depends on the rate, 10 us otherwise. The fine-rate types are
       the ones where the rate IS the measurement or the waveform - INPUTpulse, INPUTcoincidence,
       ultrasoundDistance (10 us of jitter is ~1.7 mm of echo distance), the OUTPUTgpio-* outputs
       driven as PWM, setStepperMotor and the neopixels - listed in
       _GlobalConst_pigpioFineRateTypes. Everything else goes through pigpio for events that are
       milliseconds apart. "10 us always" and "5 us always" remain selectable per rPi.
       Validated in the plugin before it is sent: pigpiod accepts only 1,2,4,5,8,10 and refuses to
       start on anything else, which would take every gpio device on that rPi down with it. And if
       the detection itself throws, the fallback is the FINE rate, not the cheap one - a wrong
       guess there costs cpu, the other way costs measurements.
       On the rPi the rate is read by U.pigpiodStartCmd() straight from the parameters file rather
       than from a global, because master calls startPigpiod BEFORE readNewParams on purpose (every
       program picks its gpio backend at import and pigpiod must exist by then). The file is
       already on disk from the previous run, which is what is wanted.
       SIX FILES WOULD HAVE NameError'd: in apds9960, Wire18B20, checkForIncl-py3, receiveCommands,
       tf and ultrasoundDistance the pigpiod start runs at IMPORT time, above the piBeaconUtils
       import - checkForIncl-py3 never imports it at all - so those sites do a local import of it.
       Checked by comparing each call's line number against the import's.
       NOT DONE: skipping pigpiod altogether when nothing needs it. Only
       INPUTRotarySwitchIncremental truly requires it; the rest fall back to gpiozero/lgpio. With
       auto now choosing 10 us on an rPi with no timing-critical gpio, the remaining saving is
       smaller than the risk of getting that set wrong.
   ALSO: display.py's diagnostic logged startIdx before it was assigned, so it printed "replaying
       cmds 0..36" and read as if the background cache was off when it was not. It reports
       displayBGclockIdx now. The send counter it gained settled the open question: 60 sends in 60
       seconds, ie ONE full-frame send per tick, so the earlier "three sends per tick" was wrong
       and sending once changed nothing - there was already only one. At ~290 ms per full-frame
       send at 1920x1080 that single send is nearly all of display.py's remaining 50%.

v2022.194.14  @ 2026-10-03
   RPI: display.py, round 3, and this one found the actual reason sendImage was 56% of the program:
       THE SEND BLOCK IS INSIDE THE COMMAND LOOP. Every command that does not carry
       display:"wait" pushes the whole framebuffer - 6.2 MB through tobytes(), blit and flip at
       1920x1080, measured at about 61 ms each on this rpi. Before the background cache that was up
       to 37 sends per pass; with the cache it was still one per replayed command, and the live
       layout replays three (the clock is cmd 35 of 37), which is exactly the 1100 self samples the
       profile showed. Two captures in a row were identical because the cache was working and this
       was never the thing the cache addressed.
       SO: on a CACHED tick only one command sends. That tick exists to show one finished frame.
       The chosen command is the last one that WOULD send, not simply the last one - a final
       command carrying display:"wait" never enters the send block, and picking it would mean the
       frame is never pushed to the screen at all. Deliberately NOT done on a rebuild pass: a
       layout may show a sequence of images in turn and the display:"wait" convention says the
       author controls that, so progressive behaviour is untouched wherever it may be intended.
       The skip also skips the alive-file heartbeat and the display.inp break checks that sit after
       the send block at the same indent - harmless, since the sending command and every later one
       still reach them, so the heartbeat still happens on every tick.
       WHAT DID NOT WORK, recorded so it is not tried again: a single dirty rectangle covering the
       clock AND the commands after it. Differencing the previous frame against the background
       bounds them exactly - a harness confirmed restoring that union is pixel-identical to a full
       redraw at all 60 second positions - but in the real layout the later commands sit far from
       the clock, so the one box containing both came out at 77% of the frame and restoring it
       measured SLOWER than copying the whole image (1.32 ms against 0.83 ms at 1920x1080). One
       rectangle is the wrong shape for two distant regions. The clock-only box is still used when
       the clock IS the last command, where it is 4% of the frame.
       Also: a one-a-minute log line now states whether the clock-only redraw is in use and, when
       it is not, exactly which condition rejected it. Two captures were spent guessing that, and
       the line found the per-command send on the first run.
       AND THE DIFFERENCING IS GONE, not just unused. When the union branch was dropped its
       consumer went but the ImageChops.difference()/getbbox() that fed it stayed, and the next
       capture caught it burning 149 + 19 self samples - 5.4% of the program - to compute a box
       nothing reads. Removed with its global, its resets and the import.
       EXPECTED: three full-frame sends per tick down to one, so roughly two thirds of 56%.
       NOT MEASURED - display.py does not run on the mac.

   RPI: display.py, round 2. The cached background took it from 65% to 52% cpu and did what it was
       meant to - font rasterising 1164 -> 315 self samples, base64+crop 773 -> 102,
       analogClockInit 169 -> 3 (the double face draw) - but it left the OUTPUT side as the whole
       remaining cost: sendImage 1156 self samples + tobytes 589 = 56% of the program, and the
       background copy I added a further 427 (13.8%).
       That 427 corrected an earlier claim of mine. I had measured the full-frame copy at 9.6 us
       and written off the region-restore alternative as saving 3 us; that was at 480x320. This
       display is 1920x1080, so every frame copies AND converts 6.2 MB, and the clock box is
       295x295 = 4% of it. Measured at the real resolution: full copy + tobytes 0.82 ms, region
       restore + crop/tobytes 0.06 ms, 13x less work.
       SO: the clock's bounding box only, in both places. imData is kept and only the box is
       restored from the background, and sendImage takes box= and crops/blits/updates just that
       rectangle instead of tobytes()+flip() on the whole screen. The box is the whole disc
       (centre +- radius + half the box line width + 4 px for caps and antialiasing), not the
       wedge the hand sweeps: the wedge is harder to get right and leaves a sliver of old hand the
       moment the geometry is a pixel off, and the disc is already 4% of the screen.
       GATED, because only the box gets its background back - any other command redrawn this tick
       would be painting over its own old pixels outside the restored region. It needs: the cached
       clock command to be analogClock, to be the LAST command in the list, flipDisplay off (it
       replaces imData with a transposed copy further down, breaking both the persistent image and
       the box coordinates), a screen/pygame device, and an imData of the right size. Anything
       else falls back to the full copy and a full flip, as does every rebuild pass, and the 10 s
       forced rebuild is a full-frame repaint in any case.
       NO X HERE - pygame runs on the framebuffer (SDL fbcon), set_mode with FULLSCREEN and no
       DOUBLEBUF, so flip() is just a whole-screen update() and a partial update is legitimate.
       But what SDL hands back depends on the driver, and on a DOUBLE-BUFFERED surface the back
       buffer does not hold the previous frame, so updating one rectangle would leave garbage in
       the rest of the screen. sendImage therefore ASKS the surface - get_flags() & DOUBLEBUF - and
       takes the full-frame path if it is set, rather than trusting the flags passed to set_mode.
       analogClockBox() returns None rather than raising when the params are not usable, and clips
       to the image - checked at both corners and on degenerate radius.
       EXPECTED, NOT MEASURED: ~2000 of the 3093 remaining samples, so roughly 52% -> under 20%
       cpu. display.py does not run on the mac; this needs a capture on the pi to confirm, and the
       thing to watch is a stale rectangle around the clock, which would mean the box is too small.

   RPI: display.py used ~50% cpu, all of it to redraw a frame in which only the second hand had
       moved. py-spy, 60 s on a live rpi: generic text items 33.9% (draw.text -> PIL draw_text ->
       getmask2, ie font rasterising), sendImage 31.9%, embedded images 10.6% (base64 decoded and
       the png re-parsed EVERY pass), and the clock itself only ~13%. imData is built fresh with
       Image.new() inside the loop, so the whole command list was replayed for every tick.
       CACHED BACKGROUND now: the background is snapshotted once and copied per tick. The copy is
       what erases the old hands - no blanking step, no stale-pixel bookkeeping - and it must be a
       copy OF THE BACKGROUND, never a fill with black, which would also erase the clock box, the
       ticks and anything drawn behind the clock. Measured, 480x320 RGB: full copy 9.6 us against
       6.5 us to restore just a 140x140 clock box, so the region version saves 3 us and buys a
       bounding-box off-by-one that leaves a sliver of old hand behind. Full copy. (For scale,
       imData.tobytes() inside sendImage is 52 us.)
       SNAPSHOT TAKEN AT THE FIRST CLOCK COMMAND, not at the end of the list, so DRAW ORDER DOES
       NOT CHANGE: background = the commands before the clock, replayed per tick = the clock and
       everything after it. A list with no clock command never caches, so non-clock displays are
       untouched.
       INVALIDATION was the part worth getting right. Keying on new input data alone would have
       been wrong, because the static part moves on its own in three more ways, and all are in the
       fingerprint: dateString items render datetime.now().strftime(), so "%M" changes once a
       minute and "%S" once a second with no new input (their RENDERED text is fingerprinted,
       which is what makes "%H:%M" reuse the background 59 ticks out of 60); every item is gated
       on onDecision(), so a blinking item appears and disappears on its own schedule; and the
       light sensor moves multIntensity/intensity, from which every fill colour is computed.
       Geometry and device are in there too. Verified in a harness: %H:%M:%S rebuilds across a
       real second, %H:%M does not, a blink flips on its own period, a light-level or resize
       rebuilds, and a changed text VALUE is caught by the command-list compare (the fingerprint
       alone does not see it). Fingerprint cost 6.8 us for 19 items.
       Plus a FORCED REBUILD every 10 s: if a dependency has been missed the display self-heals
       within 10 s instead of staying wrong until the next input, for about a tenth of the saving.
       NOT CACHED for lcd1602 (its text is accumulated into TextForLCD, not drawn into imData, so
       skipping the text commands would blank it) and not when scrollPages > 1 (a scrolling frame
       has no static part). npage is carried forward so the clock and later commands see the value
       they would have seen.
       offONTime parsing is now ONE function (cmdOffOnTimes) used by both the drawing loop and the
       fingerprint: two copies would be a silent-staleness bug, the fingerprint calling an item
       unchanged while the loop draws it differently.
       ALSO: "if True or cmd != analogclockInitialized" drew the clock face TWICE a second -
       analogClockInit ends by calling analogClockShow itself, and analogClockShow is called again
       a few lines below. The "True or" was pointless while imData was rebuilt every pass; with
       the cached background the guard does its job, so it is gone.
       NOT YET MEASURED ON THE PI - display.py cannot run on the mac, so the numbers above are the
       profile and the harness, not a post-change capture.

   RPI: the adaptive beep poll (v.12) DID NOTHING, and the second profile said so: the loop was
       still at 10 Hz and loopCheckBeepBattery unchanged at 4.1% on a beaconloop that demonstrably
       had the other changes in it. My bug: the "is there work" test was "beepBatteryBusy > 0", and
       beepBatteryBusy is not a work-in-progress flag. checkWhichHCIForBeep sets it to 1 on its
       fall-through path whenever beep has to SHARE the beacon radio, and to 0 only when it finds a
       free radio - so on an rpi without a spare radio it sits at 1 for ever, the test was true on
       every turn, and sleepFor never left 0.1 s. Only 2 means a beep or battery read is actually
       running, which is exactly why the wait in the main loop tests "> 1".
       Now "> 1", and read BEFORE the 2 -> 1 decay a few lines above it, or the test would be dead
       code: that decay drops a 2 set at the top of the same turn back to 1 before the sleep is
       chosen. The real work signals (checkIfBeep, checkIfBat) were always the main term; the busy
       term is the net for a beep still running in another process.

   RPI: stringFromPacket did its hex conversion one byte at a time, on every packet - py-spy put
       it at ~8% of beaconloop, the largest single leaf left once the tag search was fixed. Per
       byte it was doing an isinstance test, a "%02x" format and a string concat, and on PY2 a
       struct.unpack call as well, because iterating a py2 str yields one-character strs so the
       isinstance(c, int) test is false for every byte. That makes the py2 path the slower one by
       almost 2x - measured 5.22 us vs 2.87 us for a 40 byte frame.
       Now bytes.hex() on py3 and binascii.hexlify on py2 - the whole conversion in C, in one
       call. hexlify is the py2 answer specifically: it takes a str or a bytearray, returns a str,
       and needs no per-byte python. 0.06 us for the same frame, ~48x on py3 and ~75x against the
       py2 shape. Both give lowercase exactly as "%02x" did, and both callers .upper() anyway.
       The byte loop is KEPT as the last fallback: pkt is whatever the socket handed us, so if it
       is ever something neither path accepts the old behaviour applies instead of an exception
       reaching the read loop. py2 itself is NOT installed here, so the py2 timing above is the
       py2 code shape measured on py3, not a py2 run.
   MAC: new menu button, "Print various info to logfile" -> states of all rPis as a TABLE:
       one LINE per state name, one COLUMN per rPi. Side by side is the whole point - the device
       dump already prints one rpi after another, which turns the question you actually ask here
       ("why is THIS rpi different") into a scrolling exercise. In columns the odd one out shows.
       TIMESTAMPS have their century dropped - "2026-10-03 11:22:33" prints as
       "26-10-03 11:22:33" - which buys exactly the two characters the seconds need. At 16 wide the
       column cut it at "2026-10-03 11:.." : hour present, minute half there, seconds gone, the
       wrong half to lose when the question is how long ago something last reported. A fractional
       second is dropped for the same reason. The test is structural, not a regex (re is not
       imported here): separators must sit exactly where a timestamp puts them and the rest must be
       digits, so a note like "20 devices seen" is returned untouched.
       Lines stay under 300 characters: 30 for the state name, 17 per column, 15 columns to a
       block, and
       more rPis than that are printed as further blocks rather than wrapped - wrapping would
       destroy the alignment that is the only reason to print a table.
       The state list is the UNION over all rPis, so a state one rpi is MISSING is visible as "-"
       instead of being silently dropped; a state no rpi in a given block has is left out of that
       block, where it would have been a row of dashes saying nothing.
       A legend above the table gives each rPi's number, device name and whether it is enabled.
       SECOND SECTION for the rPI-Sensor children - separate from the first table, not more
       columns in it: they are different devices answering a different question, with their own
       state names, and there are far more of them than there are rpis, so mixing them would have
       produced a table that is mostly "-". Within the section they are ONE table, exactly like
       the rpis: the state names of a sensor on one rpi and a sensor on another are the same
       names, so grouping per rpi only broke the comparison the table exists for.
       Their columns are NUMBERED (#1, #2, ...) with the names in the legend above: a name like
       "s-BLEswitchbotContact-door-2-right" does not survive a 16 wide column, and a column headed
       "s-BLEswitchbotCo.." does not say WHICH contact it is. The legend carries each sensor's rPi
       number, so the table still says which rpi a column belongs to; a sensor with no rPi
       assigned shows "-" there rather than being dropped.
       The table renderer is now one shared method (statesTable), since both sections want exactly
       the same table and differ only in what goes in the columns.

v2022.194.13  @ 2026-10-02
   RPI: beaconloop ran at ~25% cpu and now runs at ~10%. ONE GUARD did it.
       When a configured device's own tag did not match a particular frame - a SwitchBot sending
       its FF flavor instead of its service-data flavor, an Apple device whose find-my payload
       had rotated, any multi-flavor advertiser - checkIfTagged fell through to its fallback
       search and walked ALL 79 knownBeaconTags, calling testAdvMatch / testComplexTag on each.
       Then it threw every answer away. The only consumer of that search is the acceptance test
       inside the loop, and with accept-new off and the device already carrying a real tag, all
       of its terms are false BEFORE the loop starts. py-spy on a live rPi: testComplexTag 18% +
       checkIfTagged 16% + testAdvMatch 7% = ~41% of the process, almost all of it discarded.
       The guard (searchCanAccept) runs the search only when something could actually be accepted
       from it. Accept-new on, or a device still tagged "" / "other", searches exactly as before -
       that is what the search is for. No outcome changes, only the work done to reach it.
       Pass 2 already tested just the one tag from onlyTheseMAC[mac]["typeOfBeacon"], and sensors
       already went through BLEsensorMACs[mac]["sensor"], so no mac->type index was needed.
       NOT CAPPED, deliberately: an existing device left tagged "other" still searches all 79 on
       every frame, because searching is the only way it would ever get a better tag.
       Also corrected the FF4C00 filter comment, which asserted "25% is what beaconloop costs
       here". Its conclusion was right - the find-my pre-filter was never the cost - but the
       number would have been read as an irreducible baseline. It was not.

   RPI: loopCheckBeepBattery polled ten times a second, for ever, to find nothing. py-spy on a
       live rPi put it at 19% of beaconloop's cpu, with os.path.isfile a further 7% - and this
       loop is a large part of that, because beep() and getBeaconParametersInteractive() each
       begin with an os.path.isfile on a marker that indigo writes perhaps twice a day. Twenty
       stat calls a second, from here alone, to learn that nobody wants a beep.
       ADAPTIVE NOW. Idle turns ramp the sleep 0.1 -> 0.5 seconds; ANY work - a beep, a battery
       request, or beepBatteryBusy still set from one in progress - drops it straight back to 0.1,
       so a command and everything following it is handled exactly as promptly as before. It ramps
       rather than jumping so a burst of commands stays responsive between its parts. Idle turns
       fall from 100 per ten seconds to about 23, and the stat calls from 20 a second to 4.
       WHAT IT COSTS, stated plainly: worst-case latency on the FIRST command after an idle spell
       goes from a tenth of a second to half a second. It is a beep somebody asked for from
       indigo; nobody can tell the difference, and everything after it is immediate again.
       THE PROFILE IS WHY THIS WAS FOUND AT ALL. Four functions in this file had already been
       optimised by reading them - parsePackage, the 08/09 decode, checkIfTagged and BLEAnalysis -
       and parsePackage turns out to be 2% of the time while this loop was 19%. The measurement
       went where the reading had not.
   RPI: beaconloop can profile its own read loop on demand. Create temp/profile.start, optionally
       containing the number of seconds (default 60, clamped 5..600), and the loop profiles
       everything it does for that long, writes temp/profile.txt sorted two ways - by TOTTIME,
       which is where the cpu actually is, and by CUMTIME - and stops. Nothing is left enabled.
           echo 60 > /home/pi/pibeacon/temp/profile.start
       The marker is checked every two seconds rather than every turn, and deleted as soon as it
       is seen: this loop runs many times a second, and an os.path.isfile on each of them is a
       cost paid for ever to enable something used twice a year.
       WHY IT IS WORTH HAVING: four functions in here have now been optimised on the strength of
       reading them - parsePackage, the 08/09 name decode, checkIfTagged and BLEAnalysis - and
       between them they save a few microseconds a packet. The 25% cpu this rPi sits at is not
       explained by any of it, and the one previous attempt to explain it (disabling find my
       entirely) moved nothing. Guessing has had its turn.
       IT SEES THE MAIN THREAD ONLY - cProfile is per-thread and beaconloop runs others. If the
       loop turns out to account for little of the cpu, that is itself the answer: the time is in
       another thread, and py-spy, which samples all of them and needs no code at all, is the tool
       for that.
   RPI: BLEAnalysis() - the duplicate test and the tag loops. The dedup walked all of a mac's
       stored messages doing a SUBSTRING search on each, and rebuilt its key - "line[:-3].strip()",
       a slice and a strip - for every one of those comparisons, when it does not change across
       the scan. One dict lookup now, keyed on the same normalised line. Measured on a synthetic
       capture of 2976 lines over 40 macs: 0.96 ms to 0.49 ms, half the time, same result.
       IT IS ALSO MORE CORRECT. The old test matched on CONTAINMENT, so a short message was called
       a duplicate of a longer one that happened to contain it - a false duplicate. The dict
       matches on equality, which is what "have I seen this packet" means. The index is kept
       OUTSIDE the MACs dicts: those are deep-copied and json.dumps'd into the BLEAnalysis files,
       and this is working state, not a result.
       hexStr[12:-2] and mac.replace(":","") were rebuilt INSIDE both loops over knownBeaconTags -
       79 turns for every message of every mac in the capture - and hexStr[12:] was sliced twice
       per message. All hoisted. MACs[mac] was indexed about fifteen times per line and
       parsedData[mac]["analyzed"] several, both read once now.
       Every new local checked statically to be assigned before it is read, after the advBody
       mistake in checkIfTagged.
   RPI: BLEAnalysis() threw away every ADVERTISED tx power, and reported the wrong thing instead.
       There was a LOCAL max_TX, reset to -99 at the top of each line, and the block ended with an
       unconditional "m['max_TX'] = max_TX". So a message carrying a TxPowerLevel stored it
       correctly, the next test asked whether the STORED value was still -99 (it no longer was),
       the local was therefore never refreshed, and that last line wrote the untouched -99
       straight back over the real figure.
       The result was exactly backwards: a beacon that ADVERTISES its tx power reported -99, and
       only beacons WITHOUT one got a number, taken from the packet trailer - which is the weaker
       of the two sources and the only one that survived.
       Accumulated in place now, no local. The advertised value wins where there is one, the
       trailer is the fallback, and the -99 test means the trailer can never overwrite an
       advertised value picked up from an earlier message of the same mac.
       Six scenarios checked against the old code: a beacon advertising -4 reported -99 and now
       reports -4; advertising -4 then -8 reported -99 and now reports -4; one with no tx power at
       all reported -59 from the trailer and still does. Four of the six were wrong before.
   RPI: checkIfTagged() - the per-tag loop no longer rebuilds constants 79 times per packet.
       hexstr[12:-2] WAS SLICED INSIDE THE LOOP. It appears at four points, two of them in the
       walk over knownBeaconTags, and that walk runs up to 79 times for every advertisement - so a
       packet that got to the end of the tag list copied the whole advertisement string 79 times
       to hand the same value to testComplexTag each turn. It is not even a rare path: 77 of the
       79 tags carry advMatch, testAdvMatch fails for all the ones that are not this beacon, and
       the failure falls straight through to testComplexTag(hexstr[12:-2], ...). Sliced once now.
       knownBeaconTags[testTag] was indexed FOUR TIMES per tag - "pos", "advMatch", "subType",
       "posDelta" - and is one local now. The "(mac == trackMac or trackMac == '*') and
       logCountTrackMac > 0" test appeared eight times and is hoisted, as in parsePackage.
       parsedData[mac] was indexed seventeen times and its ["analyzed"]["text"] seven, both now
       read once.
       MEASURED on the loop's own bookkeeping, 79 tags with no match: 12.77 us to 8.54 us, 33%
       off, 4.2 us a packet. At 300 such frames a second that is 1.3 ms/s - about 0.13% of a core.
       Real, and still not what anybody would notice: testAdvMatch and testComplexTag are
       untouched and are where the time in this function actually goes.
       ONE BUG CAUGHT ON THE WAY: the first cut replaced every hexstr[12:-2] in the function
       INCLUDING the hoist line it had just written, leaving "advBody = advBody" - a NameError on
       the first advertisement. Found by a static check that every new local is assigned before it
       is read, which is now how these edits get verified.
   RPI: parsePackage() tidied, and ONE PIECE OF IT HAD NEVER RUN. The "FF" branch tested
       section[0:8] == "4C00" - EIGHT hex characters against a FOUR character string, which cannot
       be equal - so its body never executed once. Fixing the slice would not have helped either:
       all it did was build an "iBeacon" string whose assignment into analyzed was commented out,
       and every reader of that value elsewhere in beaconloop is commented out too. FF is the
       commonest section there is, so this was a slice and a compare on nearly every advertisement
       for nothing. Removed; the section is still recorded under "FF" by the generic code above,
       exactly as before.
       "(mac == trackMac or trackMac == '*') and logCountTrackMac > 0" was written out at four
       points, THREE OF THEM INSIDE THE PER-SECTION LOOP, so a packet with eight sections
       evaluated it twenty-five times. None of its terms can change while we are inside one
       packet; hoisted to one local. sectionsData was assigned and never read - the format string
       below it only LABELS a field "sectionsData" and prints something else - so that is gone too.
       DO NOT EXPECT THE CPU TO MOVE. Measured over 200k runs on a three-section advertisement:
       1.514 us before, 1.396 us after. 8% of this function, and 0.12 us a packet - about 0.035 ms
       per second at 300 frames a second, which is nothing. The same was already true of the find
       my filter above it: beaconloop at 25% cpu was blamed on that, find my was disabled
       entirely, and the cpu did not move. These changes are worth making because a branch that
       never ran is worth deleting, not because they buy time.
       Verified byte-for-byte: parsedData is identical before and after for six frame shapes - an
       apple manufacturer frame, a switchbot iBeacon, an eddystone TLM, a name with null padding,
       a truncated frame and one too short to parse.
   RPI: the 08/09 name decode in parsePackage no longer runs a codec per byte. When the whole
       name fails to decode as utf-8 - binary in the name field, which some beacons do and the
       code calls normal noise - it fell back to hex2str PER BYTE, and hex2str is a codecs.decode
       plus a .decode. int(x,16) answers the same question directly: a byte under 0x80 IS its own
       utf-8 character and one at or above it is never valid utf-8 alone, which is exactly what
       the old code was discovering the expensive way. Building a list and joining once also drops
       the quadratic "dd += c". 10.2 us to 2.3 us on a 20 byte binary name, 77% off that path.
       TWO THINGS DELIBERATELY NOT CHANGED, both because measuring said so.
       "if sectionCode in ['08','09']" is ALREADY OPTIMAL - CPython folds a list of constants in a
       membership test into a constant, and the bytecode is identical to the tuple form. It is
       written as a tuple now because that is the honest way to say "constant", but it buys
       nothing.
       AND THE PADDING STRIP STAYS AS IT WAS. Counting the trailing nulls and slicing once looks
       cheaper than "while s.endswith('00'): s = s[:-2]" and measured 21% SLOWER on a padded name:
       endswith is a C call and the slice it saves is a short copy, while the replacement does
       python-level slicing every turn. It was written, measured, and reverted.
       Equivalence checked over 10015 inputs in both sensor and non-sensor mode - every byte value
       0-255, multi-byte utf-8, embedded and trailing nulls, odd-length and non-hex input - with
       identical output in every case.
   RPI: get_raspi_config.py's tables reconciled against a 2026 raspi-config. THREE SETTERS AND
       TWO GETTERS NAMED FUNCTIONS THAT NO LONGER EXIST: do_boot_wait, do_rgpio and
       do_memory_split in SET, get_rgpio and get_boot_wait in GET. The GET pair is the one that
       mattered - that table IS used, so RGPIO and BOOT_WAIT had been reporting "unknown" for ever
       with nothing to say why. Removed. get_serial stays: it is the SERIAL_CONSOLE_OLD entry and
       is there for older systems on purpose.
       SUDO_PASS ADDED TO BOTH HALVES, which is what started this. Its argument is INVERTED from
       do_ssh and friends where 0 enables, because the thing being enabled is the PASSWORD:
             do_sudo_pass 0   admin password REQUIRED   - removes the nopasswd files
             do_sudo_pass 1   admin password DISABLED   - writes /etc/sudoers.d/010_$USER-nopasswd
       and it builds that filename from $USER, so a caller running as root has to pass the real
       user through - "sudo env USER=pi raspi-config nonint do_sudo_pass 1" - or root gets a file
       it does not need while the pi user goes on being asked. Both facts are in the table now,
       beside the entry, because getting either wrong fails silently and plausibly.
       get_sudo_pass READS THE POLICY, not whether sudo would prompt right now: sudo remembers a
       password for about fifteen minutes, so the obvious test answers "no password needed" when
       the rule is not there at all. Measured on a live rPi 2026-10-01.
       EVERY GET NOW HAS ITS SET. RPI_CONNECT, LEDS, FAN, BLANKING_SCREEN, PCI, PI4VIDEO,
       COMPOSITE_VIDEO and OVERSCAN_KMS_SCREEN could be read but not written; all eight exist in
       raspi-config and are in SET now. 25 setters, 37 getters, every function verified present.
   RPI: the expect scripts no longer hardcode /usr/bin/python to start master. That path does not
       exist on Bookworm or later unless python-is-python3 happens to be installed, and when it is
       missing master simply never starts - with the same silence as the sudo prompt: the line is
       sent, the shell says "command not found" into a session nobody reads, and the rPi looks
       dead. Same cause, different trigger, and it would have bitten on the next fresh SSD.
       PY=$(command -v python3 || command -v python) in restartmasterSSH.exp and upgradeSSH.exp.
       python3 first because every current image has it and none has python2, and master.py is
       py2/py3 compatible either way.
       WHICH PYTHON THE SENSOR PROGRAMS USE IS NOT DECIDED HERE and is unchanged: master reads
       usePython3 from the parameters file and hands it to master.sh, which is the watchdog that
       keeps master.py alive from then on. This is only the bootstrap - the one interpreter choice
       that had to be made before any of our files or settings existed on the machine.
       DONE IN THE REMOTE SHELL rather than as a new expect argument, deliberately: these scripts
       pick the ssh option out with [lindex $argv $argsCount-1], so argument positions are already
       load-bearing and fragile, and adding one would have made that worse for no gain. Verified
       through tclsh that the escaping transmits the intended command.
   RPI: a NEW SSD could not be brought up at all, and nothing said so. restartmasterSSH.exp ends
       with "nohup sudo /usr/bin/python -E master.py &" - and on a fresh image sudo asks for a
       password. Nothing in that script answered it: the line was sent, sudo printed "[sudo]
       password for pi:" and waited, expect slept 2 seconds and closed the session. master never
       started and the rPi looked dead, with no error anywhere, because from the plugin's side the
       command had been sent successfully.
       IT ALSO MADE enableSudoNoPassword() UNREACHABLE in exactly the case it was written for: it
       lives in master, and master could not start.
       TWO LINES IN THE EXPECT SCRIPT, before master is started. "sudo -v" with its prompt
       answered from $password, which the script already has for the ssh login - that caches sudo
       for about fifteen minutes, enough to get master up. Then "sudo env USER=<user> raspi-config
       nonint do_sudo_pass 1", which makes it permanent so the programs master launches later, and
       the next restart, do not ask again. If raspi-config is too old to have the function it
       fails quietly and master's own fallback writes the sudoers file, visudo-validated, as soon
       as it is up - which it now can be.
       IT IS IN initSSH.exp AS WELL, and that is the one that matters: it runs FIRST in the action
       list ("initSSH", "updateAllAllFilesFTP", "restartmasterSSH"), so sudo is sorted before any
       file is pushed rather than at the end. The copy in restartmasterSSH.exp stays, because the
       "restart master" button can be used on its own without a push, and both fall straight
       through where sudo is already passwordless.
       initSSH.exp NOW SPAWNS SSH ON BOTH BRANCHES, as its name has always claimed. The else
       branch spawned SFTP - which is why it got away with it for years, since mkdir, rm and exit
       are valid sftp commands as well as shell ones, so the directory setup worked either way and
       nobody had cause to look. What does not work in sftp is anything else: there is no shell,
       so sudo cannot run at all. argsCount > 5 only ever decided whether an ssh OPTION was passed
       in, not which program to run, and that distinction had been lost. Nothing parses this
       script's output - only checkIfInstallOkSSH is parsed - so the change is contained.
       NOT IN updateAllAllFilesFTP.exp, which is an SFTP session with no shell: sudo cannot run
       there whatever is piped into it.
       NOTHING THE PLUGIN PUSHES IS NEEDED, which is the point of using raspi-config rather than a
       script of ours: in initSSH this runs BEFORE any file has been transferred, and before even
       /home/pi/pibeacon exists. It uses only sudo, env and /usr/bin/raspi-config, all of which
       ship with the OS. On an image with no raspi-config at all the call fails, 2>/dev/null
       swallows it, the marker still comes back and the flow carries on - the sudo -v cache covers
       the push and the master start, and master's own fallback writes the sudoers file after
       that. Both expect blocks answer a password prompt, not just the first: if the sudo
       timestamp has not taken for any reason, the second command would otherwise sit there until
       it timed out and then send the rest of the script into a password prompt.
       THE MARKERS ARE SPLIT ("SUDO''DONE") so expect cannot match the echo of the line it has
       just typed and move on before sudo has said anything. Where sudo is already passwordless
       both steps fall straight through: sudo -v returns silently and the marker appears at once.
   RPI: master makes sure the pibeacon user has PASSWORDLESS SUDO at startup, which is what "when
       new programs are pushed" comes to - a push restarts master.
       MOST rPis WILL NEVER REACH THE WRITE, and that is deliberate. A stock Raspberry Pi OS image
       already ships /etc/sudoers.d/010_pi-nopasswd, so the FIRST thing this does is test whether
       the rule is already there and return if it is. It tests for the RULE and not a filename, so
       any file, any name and anything in /etc/sudoers itself counts.
       IT ASKS THE POLICY AND NOT THE CACHE, which is not the obvious way round. "sudo -n true"
       answers a different question - would sudo ask me RIGHT NOW - and sudo remembers a successful
       password for about fifteen minutes, so that probe reports passwordless when the rule is not
       there at all if anything used sudo in the last quarter of an hour. Measured on a live rPi
       2026-10-01: the rule was deleted and sudo carried on working until "sudo -k" dropped the
       timestamp. The probe is therefore raspi-config's own, lifted from its get_sudo_pass:
       "sudo -l -n -U <user> | grep -q '(ALL) NOPASSWD: ALL'". No timestamp is involved. The
       verification after do_sudo_pass uses it too, and needs it more - that runs moments after a
       sudo of our own, which is exactly when a timestamp test would lie. Without that check a stock image
       would end up with two files saying the same thing and ours would need explaining for ever
       after. What is left is the case it is actually for: a newer image that dropped it, or a
       renamed user who never had it.
       IT OVERRIDES raspi-config, and now says so. That tool's "password required" setting IS the
       deletion of its own /etc/sudoers.d/010_pi-nopasswd - confirmed by watching it go and sudo
       start prompting again - so switching it on and restarting master puts the permission back
       through OUR file, and the setting looks as though it did nothing. The line is therefore
       logged EVERY time it writes rather than once, and it names raspi-config and the file to
       delete: a deliberate override must be findable in the log, not silent.
       IT GOES THROUGH raspi-config NOW, which owns this and does it better than we would.
       do_sudo_pass is in every current version:
             do_sudo_pass 1   writes /etc/sudoers.d/010_$USER-nopasswd   (admin password DISABLED)
             do_sudo_pass 0   removes it                                 (admin password required)
       THE POLARITY IS INVERTED from do_ssh and friends, where 0 means enable - the thing being
       enabled here is the PASSWORD, so 1 is the permissive one. Read out of the script rather
       than assumed: getting it backwards puts a password prompt in front of every sudo on a
       headless machine.
       IT DOES MORE THAN OUR OWN FILE DID. It names the file after the ACTUAL user, so a renamed
       user is handled properly, and it also clears the legacy 010_pi-nopasswd and the cloud-init
       90-cloud-init-users, either of which can grant the same thing from somewhere nobody thinks
       to look. Writing sudoers by hand, visudo-validated, is kept as the fallback for an older
       raspi-config without the function, and for the case where the call does not take.
       USER IS SET EXPLICITLY on that call because the script reads $USER to build the filename
       and master runs as ROOT - left alone it would write 010_root-nopasswd, granting root
       something it already has while the pi user went on being asked for a password. Nearly every pi program shells out
       to sudo somewhere: hciconfig, mount, hwclock, i2c, kill. Raspberry Pi OS used to ship that
       for the pi user and newer images do not always, and a renamed user never has it - and when
       it is missing those calls sit waiting for a password nothing is going to type, on a machine
       with no terminal attached, so the program looks hung rather than broken. Done on the rPi
       rather than from the mac, so it needs no second ssh session and no password on the wire.
       THREE THINGS HAD TO BE RIGHT, because getting any of them wrong breaks sudo ENTIRELY and
       the only way back is a keyboard and a monitor on the rPi:
         1 VALIDATED BEFORE INSTALLING. The line is written to temp/ and put through "visudo -c"
           there; if visudo will not take it, it is thrown away and nothing is touched. A
           malformed file in sudoers.d takes out every sudo on the machine, including the ones
           that would repair it.
         2 ITS OWN FILE, /etc/sudoers.d/020_pibeacon-nopasswd, never an edit of /etc/sudoers. It
           can be deleted to undo this completely and it cannot corrupt what was already there.
         3 root:root and 0440, set by "install" in one step. sudo IGNORES a file in sudoers.d
           that is group- or world-writable and complains on every call, so a sloppy mode is both
           ineffective and noisy.
       The user is SUDO_USER, falling back to the owner of the pibeacon directory, which is the
       renamed-user case. Idempotent and silent: if the file already says exactly this, nothing is
       written and nothing is logged, so a restart does not produce a line every time.
       WHAT IT GRANTS, plainly: that user can run any command as root without a password. It is
       the Raspberry Pi OS default for the pi user on a dedicated appliance and it is what this
       plugin has always assumed. Delete the file to revoke it.
       Tested: the generated line accepted by visudo for pi, for a renamed user and for one with a
       hyphen, and three malformed variants all refused, so the guard installs nothing.
   RPI: an rPi that answers a ping but refuses ssh - rebooting, or sshd not up yet - no longer
       fills the log. fixHostsFile() dumped the WHOLE expect command, all of stdout and all of
       stderr at level 20 BEFORE it looked at anything, every time stderr was not empty. For the
       one thing it can actually repair, a known_hosts conflict, that detail is right. For a shut
       port it is five lines of expect internals and a stack trace from the .exp script saying
       "connection refused" three different ways, and nothing is fixed by any of it.
       AND IT CAME OUT TWO OR THREE TIMES per send, because everything after that call is a retry
       - once immediately, once more after a 2 second sleep - which is right for a transient or a
       host key just repaired, and pointless three times over for a machine that is rebooting. A
       shut port will still be shut.
       sshUnreachable() now tells "could not get a connection" apart from "failed once it had
       one", on ssh's own words: connection refused, no route to host, host is down, network
       unreachable, timed out. That case logs ONE line naming the rPi and the reason, at most once
       a minute while it stays down, and skips the retries. The counter resets when it answers
       again, so the next outage is reported straight away.
       DELIBERATELY NOT MATCHED: "Permission denied" and a changed host key. Those are
       reachable-but-refused-us, ie a real configuration problem, and they keep the full loud
       report they always had. Tested against the real log text plus permission denied, a changed
       host key, a successful transfer and a full disk on the pi - 7 cases.
   SHUTDOWN: the StopThread tracebacks are gone for good, filtered at the logger instead of
       patched at 25 call sites. Fixing the two THREAD LOOPS stopped the plugin being force-killed
       on reload, but it could not stop the NOISE: about 25 helper functions wrap a self.sleep()
       in a generic "except Exception: log the traceback", and a stop landing inside one of those
       prints a stack trace at level 40 from wherever it was. Seen from testPing, twice in one
       shutdown.
       ONE FILTER ON self.indiLOG drops any record whose exc_info carries a StopThread. The
       alternative was "except self.StopThread: raise" ahead of every generic handler - 25
       mechanical edits to a hand-edited file - and it would NOT have finished the job, because a
       re-raised StopThread then meets whatever generic handler sits above that one and gets
       logged from there instead. The filter catches every one of them, present and future, at
       the only point all those records pass through.
       IT SILENCES THE LOG AND CHANGES NOTHING ELSE. The helper still returns early, which is the
       right thing to do while stopping, and the thread loop above it still exits on its next
       sleep. Nothing worth seeing is hidden: a StopThread is never an error, it is how indigo
       says "stop", and the plugin already says so itself when it stops.
       Tested: a StopThread traceback is dropped, a real exception traceback still logs, and
       ordinary lines are untouched.
   RPI: getIPNumber() reported every program start as an IP address change. It compares the
       stored address against G.ipAddress - a MODULE GLOBAL, and every pi program is its own
       process, so it starts empty and the first call always logged "found new IP number:
       1.2.3.4, old:" with nothing after "old". Dozens of programs call this at startup, so that
       was the normal case on every restart, and a REAL address change looked exactly the same
       and was lost among them. The old number was never "not found" - there was nothing to find
       yet.
       Now the first read in a process says "IP number is 1.2.3.4" at level 10, which setLogging
       emits only when debug is on, and an actual change says "IP number CHANGED: old -> new" at
       20. The "no ip number defined" line is untouched: a missing or invalid file is a real
       fault and belongs at 20.
   RPI: playsound.py never played anything. One line built the command as
       ...+"soundfiles/"++ cmd["file"]+..., and the ++ parses as + (+name) - unary plus on a
       string - which raises TypeError before the player is reached. The same on python2 and
       python3, so this has been dead since the character went in. Nothing noticed because
       receiveCommands launches it with "&" and drops the exit code; the traceback went to the
       rPi log where nobody was looking for it.
       THE PATH IS QUOTED NOW TOO. It goes straight into a shell, so a sound file with a space in
       its name failed just as completely and more quietly still - the player got two arguments it
       could not use. cmd["player"] is deliberately left unquoted: it can carry its own options,
       eg "omxplayer -o local".
   RPI: copyToTemp.py is a THREAD IN master.py now, not a separate root process. It did one small
       thing - mirror the parameter files into temp/ when they change - in a half-second poll, and
       needed a process, a pid, a kill-the-old-one dance at startup and a liveness check in the
       periodic sweep to do it. As a thread it needs none of those: it cannot be orphaned, it
       cannot be started twice, and it dies with master instead of outliving it. A daemon thread,
       so master never waits for it on the way out - there is no state to flush.
       THE LOOP BODY IS SHARED, in U.copyParamsToTemp(), rather than copied into master. One pass,
       returning the touchFile mtime to carry forward and whether it copied; the SLEEPING is left
       to the caller, so the thread and the standalone file cannot drift apart on the thing that
       matters and each can still throttle itself. The two sleeps are the old ones - half a second
       between looks is what makes a parameter change feel immediate, ten seconds after a copy
       stops a burst of touches becoming a burst of copies.
       STARTED AND RESTARTED IN THE SAME TWO PLACES the process was: once at startup, and from
       checkStandardPGMs where checkIfPGMisRunning("copyToTemp.py") used to sit, which is now an
       is-alive test that restarts the thread.
       "copyToTemp" STAYS IN G.programFiles and must: that list is what killOldPrograms() kills at
       master startup, so an upgrade kills the old process before the new thread starts. Without
       it both would mirror the same files. copyToTemp.py itself is kept and still works if run by
       hand - it calls the same shared pass - it is simply no longer started by anything.
       The shell-outs went with it: "sudo cp" per file became U.copyFile, and "chmod a+w -R" plus
       "chown -R pi:pi" became U.makeAccessible, which is what the rest of the pi side already
       uses. Tested offline: a fresh temp/, no change, a changed file with touchFile moved, a
       deleted temp/parameters, the whole tmpfs wiped, a missing source file, and temp/ gone
       entirely - 9 cases.

v2022.194.11  @ 2026-09-29
   FIND MY: numberOfUp, numberOfMembers, the six kind counters and the per-room Pi_NN_Tags are
       SETTLED now, the same way numberOfActive always has been. numberOfActive is time-weighted
       over stableSecs precisely so a mac rotation never reaches indigo as a change; these were
       instantaneous, and they blip for a LONGER reason - an unmatched rotation gives one tag two
       up devices until the old one serves out memberDownDelaySecs. One number on the device was
       carefully steady and the rest were not, which was an inconsistency rather than a decision.
       SETTLED AS ONE SET, which is why findMySettleSet() takes a dict. These counters have
       arithmetic between them - the three "up" kinds add to numberOfUp, all six add to
       numberOfMembers, the room counts add to numberOfUp - and settling each on its own would
       take numbers from different instants and break all three relations at once. Settled
       together, whatever is written was true AT ONE MOMENT: the answer is always a set that
       really occurred and never a combination that did not. Under churn so bad that no set holds
       a majority it returns the single set observed longest, which is still one real moment.
       numberOfTotal is summed from the SETTLED six so it agrees with the settled numberOfMembers
       beside it. numberOfActive and numberOfActiveRaw are untouched - they count MACS in the
       union and have their own history.
       HOW BIG THE WINDOW MUST BE, and the obvious answer is wrong. It is a time-weighted
       MAJORITY, so a blip is absorbed only while it takes up less than HALF the window - a window
       merely longer than the blip does nothing. Measured against the real event of 2026-09-30
       19:00, where numberOfAirtagsUp went 5 -> 8 -> 5 over 84 seconds: stableSecs 90 still
       committed the 8, 120 still committed it, and only 180 absorbed it.
       SO THE RULE IS stableSecs > 2 x memberDownDelaySecs, and it is in the tooltip now. At the
       defaults (60/60) a rotation blip is absorbed. With a 90 second down-delay, as here, it
       needs the 300 option. Shortening the down-delay instead is not free - it has to stay
       comfortably above sendEverySecs or a tag goes down between two reports that were never
       going to mention it.
       Tested: 10 cases - the 19:00 surge at four window sizes, a short blip, a change that holds
       being committed, the arithmetic closing on whatever is written, a set always being one that
       was observed, the first call returning raw so a restart shows something, and 0 disabling it.
   FIND MY: EVERY RPI THAT CAN HEAR A TAG now competes for closestRPI, not only the ones counting
       it as a member. Membership is a COUNTING decision and closest-rPi is a DISTANCE one, and
       letting the first gate the second was a bug: closestRPI was picked over "heard", which is
       built from each rPi's member list alone, so an rPi hearing a tag at -81 against a join of
       -80 was treated as though it could not hear the tag at all - when it is very likely the
       NEAREST one.
       WHAT IT COST, measured on a house where nothing had moved for 20 hours: four tags sat within
       3 dB of the join level on rPi 2 (-79, -81, -82, -83). Each time one crossed, that rPi
       dropped out of the contest, the tag was said to have moved to the other rPi, and the
       per-room counts flipped - 2,3,2,3,4,5,4,5 with not a single tag touched. moveHoldSecs only
       damped how fast that happened; it could not stop it, because each flip is a genuine
       rPi-to-rPi change as far as that test can see.
       I ARGUED AGAINST THIS WHEN THE WEAK READINGS WERE ADDED that morning, on the grounds that a
       tag held at -90 by an rPi that counts it should not be lost to one reading -85 that does
       not. The case is real but it is the hysteresis case, and the answer there is still right:
       -85 IS nearer than -90. Which rPi counts the tag is what Pi_NN_State says and it is not this
       question.
       A MEMBER READING TAKES AN EXACT TIE, whichever order the rPis are walked in - both are the
       same distance evidence and the one that also passed minPackets and the join level is the
       better measurement. A tag that is DOWN is never moved by a faint reading: closestRPI stays
       as "where it was last", which is deliberate and unchanged. Tested: 12 cases, including the
       straddle that started this, a real move, both tie orders and the down case.
   FIND MY: the two signal settings are named for what they do - "min signal to accept new tags"
       and "signal below is ignored (leave)" - and -85 is in the join menu, which is where a house
       whose tags read -79 to -83 on the far rPi needs to be. The leave menu's last option said
       "-100" over a value of -95; it says -95 now, which is what it always set.
   FIND MY: "max number of member devices" defaults to 15 and 15 is in the menu, between 10 and
       20. 30 was chosen when the only failure anybody had seen was running OUT of slots; with the
       guidance now being "what you expect in range, plus 2 to 10", 15 is the middle of the range
       for a normal house and 30 was headroom nobody needed.
       CHANGED IN ALL FOUR PLACES so the two sides cannot tell different stories: the dialog's
       defaultValue, findMyMaxSlots()'s fallback and its except-branch, the value put in the
       parameters file, and both the documented and the coded default in pi/findMyGroup.py.
       NO EXISTING DEVICE MOVES. A group device that has been saved carries this setting in its
       props and keeps whatever it says - the new default reaches only new group devices, and
       devices whose props predate the field, of which there are none this side of the feature
       being written.
   FIND MY: a member device stranded above the cap is now MOVED DOWN into a free slot instead of
       only being reported. Lowering "max number of member devices" leaves everything above the
       new number unvisited - reported and counted as down since earlier today, but still sitting
       there carrying its mac, tagIds, previousMacs and history while an empty slot below the cap
       goes unused. Now it simply takes that slot and is in use again.
       ON SAVE AND AT STARTUP, never on the message path, and that is the whole reason it is safe:
       lowering the cap IS a save, so this runs at the one moment nothing is in flight - no mac is
       being given a slot in the same pass and nothing is mid-rotation through the slot being
       vacated.
       ONLY DEVICES ABOVE THE CAP MOVE, and only into slots no device holds. Devices that already
       fit are left exactly where they are: a general compaction would renumber devices that are
       working perfectly for no reason beyond tidiness, and findMySlot being written in ONE place
       is most of why the NN in a member's name cannot lie about which slot it is on.
       A NAME THE USER CHOSE IS KEPT. Only a name this plugin gave is rebuilt for the new slot -
       and then it must be, or it would claim a slot the device no longer holds, which is the one
       thing that numbering is good for. The address follows by itself, since the update loop
       already rewrites it from the slot every pass.
       WITH NO FREE SLOT nothing moves and that is said plainly instead: raise the setting or
       delete them. Tested: 10 cases, including a user-named stray (moves, keeps its name), more
       strays than free slots (lowest rescued, the rest reported), a cap above everything (no-op),
       and that devices which already fitted are never renumbered.
   FIND MY: a find my device that gets NO member device now says so in the event log. When the
       cap is full and no slot is ripe for reuse, a mac that is being heard and counted gets no
       slot at all - it is in numberOfActive and appears nowhere else. That was visible only at
       level 10 behind the FindMy debug switch, which is the wrong place for it: every other
       consequence of a full cap is cosmetic and this one loses a real tag. Without debug on, all
       anybody sees is numberOfActive climbing past numberOfMembers with nothing to explain it.
       Level 20, once an hour - the condition is re-evaluated every message and will not fix
       itself while the cap stays put. It names how many missed out and the strongest five with
       their signal, and says which of the two settings to change: raise "max number of member
       devices", or lower "reuse a down member device after" so slots come free sooner. Level 10
       still carries the per-pass detail for anyone watching a test.
   FIND MY: LOWERING "max number of member devices" used to strand every device above the new
       number, silently and for ever. The member loop runs slots 1..maxSlots, so devices on higher
       slots were not visited at all: never updated, never counted, never tidied away - and since
