       always kept when it goes down, because it is the record of what was there. Two down
       airpods count as two.
       AND SPLIT UP FROM DOWN RATHER THAN PICKED BETWEEN. One counter per kind had to answer both
       "how many airtags are HERE" and "how many airtags does this group KNOW ABOUT". Those are
       different numbers - they differ by exactly the ones that have gone quiet - so whichever
       population it was counted over, it was the wrong answer to the other question. Counted
       over the union it said 0 while two were sitting there; counted over every device it would
       say 2 while none was here. Two counters per kind, and neither number is inferred.
       IT ALSO REMOVES A PIECE OF MACHINERY. A union tally could never add up to a settled count,
       so the breakdown had to be FROZEN whenever the raw and settled numbers differed and
       re-taken when they agreed - a whole mechanism whose only job was to hide a mismatch
       between two populations. There is no mismatch left to hide: the three ...Up add up to
       numberOfUp and all six to numberOfMembers, on every pass, with nothing held back.
   NEW: numberOfTotal - all six added up, so the breakdown CHECKS ITSELF at a glance rather than
       in your head. It equals numberOfMembers by construction, every device landing in exactly
       one of the six, and is written from the SAME walk the six come from - so if it ever
       disagrees the disagreement is real and not an artefact of reading states a moment apart.
   MEASURED: the four battery words are NOT a linear scale. Airpods taken straight off the
       charger report "medium", which indigo shows as 50%. That is the second of four steps and
       not half a battery - what the two bits actually mean is the device's business and nobody
       has published it. So a "battery low" trigger belongs on a FALL from one word to the next,
       never on the absolute number, and 50% on an airpods case is not a reason to go looking for
       a charger. Confirmed live, with the battery chain working end to end: full, medium and low
       have now all been seen reaching indigo's own battery column.
   NEW: a member device is NAMED FOR WHAT IT IS, not just numbered:
         findmy_NN_airpod-case    07+12, the pairing frame a case sends with its lid open
         findmy_NN_phone-mac      it also sends nearby info (10) or handoff (0C) - live apple
                                  device messages, which a tag cannot send
         findmy_NN_other          find my plus something without a name of its own, AND a mac
                                  nothing is known about yet
         findmy_NN                12 and nothing else - the one label with NO suffix, because
                                  that is an airtag OR any other find my accessory OR an
                                  offline iphone. There is nothing true to add, and a confident
                                  wrong name is worse than a dull right one
   VALIDATED ON THE HOUSE: the take-over option, after a real battery change. The tag came back
       as a new device and the rPi refused to match it - correctly. A battery swap reboots the
       tag: new key, new mac, a gap far longer than any rotation, and the battery word going
       "low" -> "full", which is itself one of matchRotation's six tests. Every signal that could
       say "same tag" says the opposite, and merging on none of them would mean merging a tag
       that vanished with an unrelated one that appeared. So the refusal is the design, the
       manual migration is the remedy, and it copied the states across and deleted the newcomer
       exactly as intended. The device dialog now says all of this where somebody with a fresh
       CR2032 in their hand will actually read it.
   FIX: A ROTATION NO LONGER GROWS A NEW INDIGO DEVICE. This is the one that made a handful of
       tags into fifteen member devices.
       The lineage claim - "a mac whose rPi lineage a device already owns IS that device's tag
       under a new mac" - ran SECOND, after the pass that lets a slot keep its current mac, and
       it skipped any slot already in use. But during a rotation the OLD mac is still in the
       union: expireSecs keeps it for a few seconds after its last frame, and the summary that
       carries the new mac usually carries the old one too. So the slot was always "in use", the
       claim was always refused, and the newcomer always got a fresh slot and a fresh device.
       MEASURED, one tag, one afternoon: lineage 5-4 walked slot 09 -> 10 -> 11, a new device
       each time, each mac living almost exactly the 15 minutes of a rotation period - while the
       rPi had matched every rotation correctly and kept the tagId throughout. Three devices,
       two "previousTagIds: 5-4" headstones, one airtag. The rPi was right and this side threw
       its answer away.
       THE CLAIM NOW RUNS FIRST, before any slot is handed out, and a slot only keeps its own mac
       when that mac still carries the slot's lineage. AND THE OUTGOING MAC GETS NO SLOT AT ALL:
       it is the same physical tag on its way out, it expires within seconds, and giving it one
       would recreate the duplicate device under the other half of the rotation.
       Tested against four cases: both macs in the union (the bug), nothing rotated, a genuinely
       new tag beside an unchanged one, and a rotation whose old mac had already gone.
       VALIDATED ON THE HOUSE, 15 minutes after a clean start: four tags, four devices, and TWO
       ROTATIONS ABSORBED - slot 01 EF:EB:B6:CD:BF:26 -> D0:F0:07:20:FD:93 at 16:45:04 and slot
       03 C9:17:5A:79:F5:B1 -> F6:22:50:81:66:DD at 16:45:15. Both kept their slot, their device
       and their tagIds; previousTagIds stayed empty on all four, so no lineage moved at all.
       Under the old code those two rotations would have become slots 05 and 06 with two dead
       devices behind them. The afternoon before the fix reached fifteen devices.
   FIX: APPLE TYPES NOW ACCUMULATE ON THE DEVICE, not only on the mac - and a member device
       stops flipping its name back and forth. Seen doing it, twice inside 73 seconds:
         slot 08 renamed "findmy_08_airpod-case" -> "findmy_08",  from: 12  find my only
         slot 08 renamed "findmy_08" -> "findmy_08_airpod-case",  from: 07|12  airpods/case
       THE RPI COLLECTS TYPES OVER A MAC'S LIFE, for the good reason that a device does not put
       them all in one advertisement. But a find my mac ROTATES EVERY 15 MINUTES, so on its own
       that knowledge is destroyed at every rotation and relearned from nothing - 12 on the first
       frame after the rotation, 07 a minute later - and anything downstream that believed the
       current frame flapped in step with it.
       A FRAME WITHOUT 07 IS NOT EVIDENCE AGAINST 07. The device now merges what it is told into
       what it already knew, so a type seen once is known from then on.
       FORGOTTEN ONLY WHEN THE SLOT CHANGES HANDS, and that is decided on the LINEAGE and not on
       the mac: a rotation keeps at least one tagId - that is what a tagId is for - so only a
       slot whose new tagIds share NOTHING with the old ones is a different physical tag, and
       only then does the device start learning again from scratch.
       IT IS NOT JUST THE NAME. The airtag/airpods/other breakdown was counted from the live
       frame too, so those counts flipped in step with the names; they read the device's
       accumulated types now, and only fall back to the live frame for a device that has none yet.
       ONE PREFIX, "findmy_", for every one of them - the suffixed names spent a few minutes
       spelled "find_my_NN_..." and that is gone; a device list with two spellings of the same
       prefix sorts into two clumps and reads as two different things.
       AND ONE TEST FOR "IS THIS STILL A NAME WE GAVE IT", where there were three. The other two
       were literal lists of findmy_NN and its collision form, written before a member could be
       born as findmy_NN_airpod-case - so the slot allocator would have called every suffixed
       device "renamed by the user" and protected it from reuse, while the rename code went on
       treating the same device as its own. They would have fought, quietly, over exactly the
       devices this change creates. All three now call findMyMemberNameIsOurs(), which builds
       the set from the suffix map itself and so cannot fall behind it again.
       THE WORDS COME FROM findMyAppleTypes(), the same ones already in the appleTypes state, so
       the name in the device list and the state on the device agree BY CONSTRUCTION rather than
       by being kept in step. Not from findMyKind(), which has three buckets and would have
       called a phone "other".
       NAMED LATE AS WELL AS AT BIRTH, because apple types ACCUMULATE: a mac can join sending
       only 12 and reveal the 07 frame a minute later, by which time the device already exists.
       The rename happens on the next update.
       AND NEVER OVER A NAME THE USER TOUCHED. The test is exact - the four strings the plugin
       could itself have produced for that slot, nothing that merely resembles them - so the
       moment a device is called "Karl's airpods" the plugin stops having an opinion about its
       name for good. That is the same principle findMyMemberDevices() has always followed by
       looking devices up on their PROPERTIES and never on their name.
       A slot changing hands to a different kind of tag renames it again, which is correct - it
       is a different physical thing - and every rename says so in the log with both names.
   NEW: hintByte - a member state, and a field on the wire. It is the LAST BYTE of the 25 byte
       separated payload, two hex digits, and blank for a tag that has only ever sent the 2 byte
       nearby payload - which has no such byte, so blank means "never said" and not "said zero".
       It has been parsed on the rPi for as long as find my has existed here and went no further
       than the frame log and the calibration csv.
       NO PUBLISHED MEANING beyond "0x00 on ios", and it is named for what it IS rather than for
       what it might be: a label that turns out to be wrong is worse than no label.
       WHAT IT IS, MEASURED THE SAME DAY THE COLUMN WAS ADDED - and it is NOT what started this.
       The idea was that the hint holds the last byte of the address a tag rotated away from,
       from two eyeballed coincidences: hint 8D beside a mac ending 8D, hint 96 beside a mac
       ending 96. WRONG, and the calibration log said so in one pass: over 151 rows carrying a
       hint, it matched the last byte of another mac present at the time 3 times - at or below
       what coincidence produces for one byte against a dozen macs. Eyeballing a few rows will
       always find the pair that matches; that is what the log is for.
       IT IS A 15 MINUTE EPOCH MARKER, AND NOTHING TO DO WITH THE KEY. Every true transition - a
       hint changing while the MAC stayed put - landed on a 15 minute boundary, synchronised
       across unrelated devices: 16:14:32, three independent macs together at 16:15:03, then
       16:30:03 and 16:30:13.
       THE CLINCHER, straight out of the new raw_hex column: two separated frames from one tag
       19 minutes apart, spanning the 16:30 boundary, differ in EXACTLY ONE BYTE of 31 - the
       hint, 83 -> 7B. Status, all 22 key bytes and the key-bits byte are byte-identical. The
       key sat still while the hint moved, so the hint is not key material and is not derived
       from it. That comparison was only possible because the whole frame is now logged; a week
       ago it would have taken new logging and another day of waiting.
       SO IT CANNOT MATCH ROTATIONS, and matching on it would be actively harmful: at an epoch
       boundary every hint changes, which is exactly when a rotation is most likely to happen.
       It stays logged and displayed because it costs nothing, and NOTHING READS IT.
       Written only when the rPi actually sends one, so an rPi on an older findMyGroup.py leaves
       the state alone rather than blanking a byte that is already there.

================================================================================
THE CALIBRATION CSV
================================================================================
   NEW: raw_len and raw_hex, last on every row. raw_hex is the WHOLE advertisement of the most
       recent frame from that mac - everything the other columns were decoded out of - and
       raw_len is its length IN BYTES, before it, because the length is the thing you filter on:
       25 is a separated payload and the only kind with a hint byte, 2 is nearby.
       WHY IT CHANGES WHAT THE FILE IS FOR: until now a theory about a byte could only be tested
       by adding logging and waiting for the tag to come round again. With the frame itself on
       the row, a theory can be tested against rows already written. It is also what makes the
       abbreviations below acceptable - nothing in them is the only record any more.
       Last on the row because it is much the longest field, and a variable-width column in the
       middle makes every row after it unreadable.
   CHG: the "hci" column is GONE. It dates from when an rpi ran several ble dongles as equals;
       the radios have had fixed roles for a long time - one scans, a ble5 dongle only listens -
       and findMyGroup.py never had a per-frame hci to put in it anyway, so it wrote the column
       EMPTY for its whole life. host already says which rpi. findmy_scan.py still knows its
       radio from --hci and still says so in its startup line and its state file; it just stops
       repeating it on every row of a file that is about one radio.
   CHG: EVERY VARYING COLUMN IS NOW A FIXED WIDTH, so the file can be read by eye. It could not
       be before: "nearby" sat under "separated" and "low" under "critical", so every column to
       the right of them moved on every row.
       THE THREE THAT DO NOT FIT ARE ABBREVIATED RATHER THAN WIDENED, which is only reasonable
       because raw_hex now carries the frame they were decoded from:
         distanceIndicator   sep / near / lNN
         battery             full / med / low / crit
         appleTypes          ONE LETTER PER TYPE in code order - a=05 airdrop, p=07 pairing,
                             h=0C handoff, n=10 nearby info, f=12 find my. "ahnf" is a phone or
                             a mac, "pf" an airpods case, "f" alone a tag. An unknown type adds
                             "?", because a type nobody has a letter for is the one worth
                             noticing. 4 characters against 11 for "05|0C|10|12".
       COUNTS ARE ZERO PADDED, measurements are space padded: packets 3, age_s 4, raw_len 2 read
       as one number where a leading space would read as a missing field. rssi_median and
       rssi_smoothed are written -xx.y and never were whole - a median over an even number of
       packets is a half and strongHalf averages, so "-59" on one row and "-62.5" on the next was
       two widths AND two apparent precisions for one measurement. rssi_min and rssi_max stay
       whole, because they are single measured packets.
       PADDED, NEVER TRUNCATED: a width is a minimum. A marker longer than 8 or an age past 9999
       pushes its own row out and leaves every other row lined up, which is the right way round.
       NOT PADDED, and not needing to be: timestamp is a fixed 21, mac a fixed 17, and host is
       constant within a file - one rpi writes one file.
       findmy_scan.py got the identical treatment: one schema, not two.
   CHG: THE MARKER IS WRITTEN ONCE, not on every row. It is an EVENT - "from here on the tag is
       at the street door" - and it was being repeated on every row of the session, which is the
       same sentence a thousand times in a file capped at 200 kB on a 2 MB tmpfs. A ten minute
       calibration run at one position wrote the label several hundred times to say one thing.
       WRITTEN WHERE IT CHANGES, AND RESTATED WITH EACH REPEATED HEADER BLOCK. The second half
       matters as much as the first: these files are read by scrolling into the middle of one,
       where a marker written once at the top is exactly as useless as a header written once at
       the top - which is why the header is repeated in the first place. It also survives
       trimCsv(), which drops the OLDEST rows: a marker written exactly once would eventually be
       trimmed away and orphan every row after it.
       "-" MEANS CLEARED, and is the one thing a blank cell cannot say. A blank means "same as
       the row above" to anything forward-filling, so clearing the marker needs a mark of its own.
       Only ever written on the row where the clearing happened.
       findmy_merge.py FORWARD-FILLS it, per file - one file is one rpi's log, and carrying a
       marker from the end of one into the start of another would label rows with a session they
       were not part of. It also now strips every field it reads, because the fixed-width columns
       put the padding INSIDE the value: "sep " and "kitchen " arrive with their spaces, and
       comparing them raw silently matched nothing.
       Measured on a round trip: a run that wrote the label on 10 of 10 rows now writes it on 4 -
       two changes, one header restate, one clear - and the calibration table comes out identical.
   NEW: BATTERY, as indigo's OWN battery level. A member device now carries indigo's batteryLevel,
       which is what gives it the battery column in the device list, the low-battery warning and
       dev.batteryLevel in a script - none of which a state holding the word "critical" can do.
       It is NOT a declared state and is not in piBeaconConstants: it comes from the
       SupportsBatteryLevel PROPERTY, exactly as every beacon in this plugin gets its battery.
       A FIND MY FRAME CARRIES NO PERCENTAGE. The status byte has TWO BITS of battery and its
       other six have no confirmed meaning, so full / medium / low / critical is the entire
       resolution that exists. Those four are rendered 100 / 50 / 20 / 1 %: 100 does not mean the
       tag measured 100%, it means "full", the top of four. The word itself stays in the
       "battery" state, unchanged.
       NO lastBatteryReplaced ON THESE DEVICES, deliberately. Switching SupportsBatteryLevel on
       brings that state with it - setstatesCategoriesForDevtype adds it to anything with a
       battery - and it is meaningless here: "when was this battery last replaced" needs a device
       that is ONE PHYSICAL THING for months, and a member device is a SLOT. It changes hands
       whenever a mac rotates without being matched, and the "delete members down too long"
       housekeeping deletes it outright. A date on it would be a date for whatever happened to be
       holding the slot when it was written. It is left off the state list rather than left
       empty, so there is no blank column in the sql logger and no row in the members table.
       THE FIRST VALUE IS QUEUED, NOT WRITTEN ON THE SPOT. replacePluginPropsOnServer returns
       before indigo has rebuilt the device's state list, so writing batteryLevel straight after
       it raced and lost - "device findmy_01 state key batteryLevel not defined", twice for every
       device made. A sleep was the first attempt and was both unreliable and rude: this runs in
       the message thread, and half a second per device is ten seconds when a houseful of tags
       arrives at once. It goes through the delayed-action queue instead, which already skips
       quietly if the state is still not there - and then the next ordinary update writes it.
       THE SUPPORT APPEARS WITH THE FIRST READING, not when the device is made: indigo defaults
       an integer state to 0, 0 is a flat battery everywhere it is shown, and there is no
       "unknown" to stamp instead the way -999 and 999 cover it for a signal and a distance.
       An unknown word later leaves the last percentage standing - the same rule every other
       reading here follows.
       Devices made by an earlier version are picked up at startup and seeded from the battery
       WORD they already carry, which matters most for a tag that is DOWN: no message is coming
       to fill it in.
   CHG: the last _GlobalConst_findMyMacHistory (5) macs a slot has held are kept in previousMacs,
       newest first, with lastMacChange for when it last moved. Never the same mac twice in a row:
       a tag that drops out and comes back would otherwise fill the list with itself and push out
       the rotations, which are the entries the history exists for.
       The STATE IS THE STORE - indigo persists it already, so there is nothing else to keep in
       step or reload at startup.
       WHY IT IS WORTH KEEPING: the current mac says nothing about which tag a device is. The
       trail of them is what separates "this tag rotated its mac" from "this slot now holds a
       different tag" - the one question a named member device cannot answer on its own.
   CHG: appleTypes is ONE String state carrying the codes and what they add up to - "12  tag",
       "07|12  airpods/case", "05|0C|10|12  phone/mac". A tag sends only find my, so a lone "12"
       is the mark of a real tag and the word after the codes says what anything longer is.
       The per-code names live in _GlobalAppleContinuityTypes in plugin.py.
   CHG: ADDRESSES AND NOTES. The group's address is "on Pi:2,5" and the member's is "findmy-03";
       the NOTES on both are left empty.
       The group had no mac to put in the address, so the pi list - the one fact worth showing
       about it - had gone into the notes, and notes are the USER'S field. The member's address
       used to carry the group's device id, which nothing needs and which filled the Address
       column with a number that means nothing to a reader.
       WHY NOT THE MAC, which every other BLE device here is addressed by: a find my mac rotates
       every ~15 min, and changing an indigo address means replacePluginPropsOnServer - a server
       round trip that can bounce the device's comms. Twenty devices rotating four times an hour
       is real churn for something the "mac" state already shows live and for free. So the address
       can be stable and uninformative, or informative and rotating, and it cannot be both.
       Existing devices are corrected in place on the next update, and the old notes are cleared
       ONLY when they still hold exactly what this plugin wrote - never a note somebody typed.
   FIX: the member's state column showed the wrong moment. It took the LATER of lastStatusChange
       and lastMacChange, so a mac rotating under a device that had not gone anywhere pushed the
       stamp forward - and the column then read "up" beside a time it had not gone up at.
       A ROTATION IS NOT AN UP/DOWN CHANGE. The column is "up since" / "down since" now: the
       moment the status itself last changed, and nothing else. lastMacChange is still on the
       device for anyone who wants it, and previousMacs still carries the trail.
       Both places that build the string were changed together - the live path and
       findMyMemberDisplay(), which repaints it after a config save. They have to produce the
       identical string, which is why that rule lives in one method.
   CHG: both device types show "status" in the indigo STATE COLUMN, with a timestamp of the LAST
       CHANGE - "3 tags  09-23 14:22:31", "up  09-23 14:22:31". A clock ticking once a minute on
       every device says nothing; "down  09-23 04:12:07" says when it went.
       For a member, what counts as a change is going up/down OR the slot's mac being replaced
       underneath it, whichever happened LATER. For the group it is lastCountChange.

================================================================================
FIND MY - SEVERAL RPIS FEED ONE GROUP DEVICE
================================================================================
   NEW: the single "select rPi-server doing the counting" menu is replaced by the same rPi
       checkbox block every other BLE sensor has. Devices saved before this keep working -
       piServerNumber is still read as a fallback.
   THE COUNTS ARE NOT ADDED, and this is the whole point. Two rPis hear overlapping areas, so a
       tag in the hall heard by both is ONE tag. The join key is the MAC - a find my mac is
       identical on every rPi at the same instant - so the house count is the UNION of the macs.
       Adding the per-rPi counts would have double-counted exactly the tags that matter most.
       - a quiet rPi DROPS OUT of the union after 2x its send interval, so a dead rPi cannot hold
         tags in the count forever. State pisReporting says "2 of 3"
       - state seenBy2Plus: how many tags more than one rPi can hear. That is much better evidence
         of "really inside" than any single rssi
       - the STRONGEST rPi wins the readings for a tag: nearest radio, least marginal decode
   FIX: OPENING AN AIRPODS CASE BROUGHT UNRELATED DEVICES BACK TO LIFE, including one for a tag
       in another room. Taking the buds out puts THREE new find my macs on the air at once - case
       and both buds, each with its own identity - and a new mac took the LOWEST free slot.
       "Free" does not mean empty: a slot whose tag has gone still carries its member DEVICE, with
       the name somebody gave it. So the three newcomers landed on the three lowest-numbered
       departed tags and those devices came up describing airpods.
       Lowest-first spends the slots people care about FIRST, which is exactly backwards. It now
       hands out the least disruptive slot instead:
         0  a slot with NO device on it - nothing to disturb at all
         1  a device still under its birth name findmy_NN - nobody has claimed it
         2  a device somebody RENAMED - taken only when nothing else is left
       and inside each tier the one unheard LONGEST, since that is the likeliest to be really
       gone. The slot number is only the final tiebreak - predictable numbering was the one thing
       lowest-first was buying, and it still holds when the slots are otherwise equal.
       "RENAMED" IS AN EXACT TEST, not a prefix, and the difference is not academic: "findmy_03-k2"
       starts with "findmy_" and is very much a name somebody chose. It is compared against the
       two names createFindMyMemberDevice can give a device and nothing else. Checked against a
       real slot table - the first version got this wrong and would have treated every renamed
       device here as unclaimed.
       This does not make a slot permanent, and cannot: with every slot carrying a device the
       longest-gone one is still taken over. It stops the CHEAP slots being spent last.
   CHG: SLOTS ARE ASSIGNED IN THE PLUGIN, not on the rPi. They had to be: rPi-2's "01" and rPi-5's
       "01" are different tags, so per-rPi slots collide the moment a second rPi is enabled. The
       mac is the only thing that identifies a member across the house, so the slot is handed out
       once, over the union - ONE MEMBER DEVICE PER TAG.
       Sticky as before (a mac keeps its slot while it is there) and rebuilt from the member
       devices themselves on every pass, so it survives a plugin restart with no stored state: the
       device holding mac X IS slot X's device.
       The rPi still keeps its own sticky slots and history for its own state file and log; the
       plugin works out previousMacs / lastMacChange on its own side, because a per-rPi history
       cannot see a shared slot change hands.
   NEW: A TAG KEEPS ITS INDIGO DEVICE WHEN ITS MAC ROTATES. New per-member field "tagId" in the
       rPi's message, and a new member state "tagIds" holding them as "2-1043, 5-77".
       THE TWO KEYS DO DIFFERENT JOBS AND NEITHER REPLACES THE OTHER: the MAC joins two rPis at
       one INSTANT - the same tag carries the same mac everywhere at the same moment - and the
       TAGID joins one rPi across TIME, because the rPi keeps it when the mac changes. .46 was
       right to stop using per-rPi slots as the house slot (rPi 2's "01" and rPi 5's "01" are
       different tags) but it dropped the second job with them, and nothing replaced it: a
       rotation has looked like a new tag ever since, with a new device to go with it.
       ONLY THE RPI CAN SUPPLY IT. The plugin sees a summary every sendEverySecs, so a rotation
       reaches it as "somewhere in the last minute one mac left and another arrived". The rPi sees
       the old mac's last packet and the new one's first a fraction of a second apart, and that
       resolution is the entire evidence.
       matchRotation() in pi/findMyGroup.py INFERS, and refuses rather than guesses. Nothing in a
       find my frame identifies a tag - the mac is key-derived and every other field is either the
       same on all tags or undocumented - so a carry-over needs ALL of:
         - the old mac stopped within ROTGAP (6 s) of the new one starting
         - AND HAS BEEN SILENT ROTQUIET (3 s) SINCE. This is the one that matters: two tags side
           by side at the same signal level were once read as one tag rotating, and the claim had
           to be withdrawn. A tag that is still advertising has not rotated into anything
         - the smoothed signal within 8 dB - it has not moved in that second
         - battery and appleTypes identical. Neither identifies anything alone; together they
           throw away most wrong candidates for free
         - and EXACTLY ONE lineage fits. Two means we do not know, and a wrong carry-over silently
           renames somebody's tag - worse than an extra device appearing
       Tested against all of it: clean rotation, rotation beside a live tag at identical rssi, a
       genuinely new tag, battery/appleTypes/signal mismatches, two lineages going quiet together,
       and a rotation noticed only after the old mac had expired. 11 cases, all as intended.
       ROTQUIET was 5 s first and refused the clean case: the new mac JOINS after minPackets
       adverts, ~4 s, so at 5 s the real rotation landed on the wrong side of the test.
       THE ID IS NEVER REUSED - a monotonic counter per rPi, not the 20-slot pool. Slot 03 an hour
       from now is a different tag, and that doubt is the thing this exists to remove. It is
       persisted in findMyGroup.state with the slot map, and the counter is never allowed to go
       backwards over a restart: a reused id would read as a rotation of whatever last held it.
       Plugin side: a mac that no device holds, but whose lineage a device already owns, keeps
       that device and slot - previousMacs and lastMacChange record the handover as before. One
       lineage to one mac and one mac to one lineage, so the rPi's refusal to guess is not undone
       here. An rPi on an older findMyGroup.py sends no tagId and simply gets the old behaviour.
       It also settles the down-delay's held slot: a rotated tag reclaims its OWN held slot
       through its lineage, so the hold now keeps only genuinely unrelated macs out, which is what
       it was for. NEEDS THE PI FILES PUSHED.
   ACCEPTED LIMIT: a tag that leaves range entirely and comes back gets a new lineage, and that
       correlation is lost. Nothing can recover it - the owner's own devices re-identify a tag by
       deriving the address from the rolling key in their keychain, and find my uses key-derived
       random static addresses precisely so that nobody without that key can. Everything above is
       inference from continuity, not identification, and it is bounded by that on purpose.
   FIX: "3 tags" while the member devices showed 2 up and 2 down, righting itself a minute later -
       with only ONE rPi running, so none of the multi-rPi machinery was to blame. The count was
       being SETTLED TWICE. The rPi settles its own count already, and settling it again here can
       only add delay: this side cannot sample faster than messages arrive, so 60 s of hold became
       120 s while the member devices, which have no such delay, showed the new picture throughout.
       Now: ONE rPi reporting -> take its memberCount, lastCountChange and previousCount as they
       come. TWO OR MORE -> settle the union here, because no rPi can see the house count.
   FIX: and THAT settle could stick, which is worse than the delay it replaced. It only ever
       committed a raw count that had held UNCHANGED for stableSecs, and it restarted its own
       clock on every sample that differed - so where raw is never steady for a minute, which is
       any house with macs rotating and members sitting either side of joinRssi, it could not
       commit anything at all and memberCount froze at whatever it had last managed to hold.
       Caught in the act: memberCount 6 against memberCountRaw 5 for 24 minutes, the state column
       reading "6 tags" with five macs in the house, and no way back on its own.
       IT NOW COMMITS WHAT WAS TRUE FOR MOST OF THE WINDOW, time-weighted. A rotation blip still
       cannot win - one sample of "6" while the old mac expires is a second or two out of sixty,
       which is the entire job stableSecs was added for - but an oscillation resolves to its
       majority instead of stopping the clock, and the count can never be stuck for longer than
       the window. TIME-weighted and not one vote per sample, because the rPis are not in step
       and a "members" trigger fires whenever membership moves: samples are irregular by design,
       and counting them equally would let three in five seconds outweigh a minute of quiet.
       Checked against steady, blip, real change, the stuck case above, and a true 50/50
       oscillation - which commits rather than freezing.
   FIX: A LIVE TAG COULD HAVE ITS LINEAGE STOLEN AND NEVER GET ANOTHER, which is what was really
       behind member devices multiplying. matchRotation() hands a lineage to what it believes is
       the same tag under a new mac and clears it off the old one, so it cannot be held twice -
       but the pool it chooses from includes LIVE candidates that have merely been quiet for
       ROTQUIET, and at roughly one advertisement every 2 s that is a SINGLE MISSED PACKET. A mac
       that had gone nowhere lost its lineage to a newcomer and carried on transmitting with none.
       AND IT NEVER GOT ANOTHER ONE: the assignment runs only at JOIN and that mac had long since
       joined. It reported no lineage for the rest of its life, so the plugin could not follow its
       NEXT rotation and made a new indigo device for it every single time - which is the device
       count climbing towards 20 that looked like a slot-allocation problem.
       Caught twice within two minutes of a restart in a real house: tagId "2-8" on two member
       devices at once, and "2-5" on two more, none of them having ever changed mac.
       Now a member found without a lineage takes a FRESH one. Not the old one back - that has
       moved on and must not be held twice, and undoing the match would put the duplicate back.
       The trail really was broken, and a new lineage from that point is what is true.
   FIX: THE APPLE-TYPES TEST REFUSED ROTATIONS IT SHOULD HAVE ALLOWED, and airpods most of all.
       matchRotation required the old mac's types and the newcomer's to be EQUAL - but types
       ACCUMULATE over a mac's life, so the test was biased against the very candidate it judged:
       the old mac had collected its set over minutes of frames, the newcomer had minPackets of
       them. An airpods case sends 07 and 12 and does not put both in every frame, so a new mac
       that had so far only shown "12" was refused a lineage it plainly owned, and earned a new
       indigo device every single rotation. Airpods sleep in the case and wake when taken out,
       which is why they multiplied faster than anything else in the house.
       SUBSET NOW, NOT EQUALITY: everything the newcomer has sent, the old mac sent too. A type
       the old mac NEVER sent is a real difference and still refuses - so a tag cannot inherit a
       phone's lineage, which is the whole reason the test is there. Checked against the eight
       cases that matter, including "07|12 does not fit within 12" and an empty set either way.
   FIX: A MAC IS NOW REMEMBERED AS OWNING ITS OWN LINEAGE FOR AN HOUR (OWNKEEP), separately from
       the 2 minutes a DIFFERENT mac may inherit it within (GONEKEEP). The two were one number
       and should never have been: the second is a guess and must stay short, the first is an
       exact mac match with nothing to get wrong.
       MEASURED: airpods earpieces stop advertising and come back ON THE SAME MAC every 3 to 4
       minutes - four returns of one earpiece in twelve minutes, at 3.0, 4.0, 4.3 and 4.2 min,
       every one of them past GONEKEEP. So each return found its own lineage already thrown away,
       re-entered matchRotation as a mac nobody had ever seen, and competed for OTHER tags'
       lineages. THAT is where the "could be any of .." refusals came from: not ambiguous tags,
       amnesiac ones - and they were blocking the genuine rotations happening beside them.
       A reclaim is logged as "ROT=", so it is visible next to the ROT/ROT?/ROTX lines.
       OWNKEEP IS TWO DAYS, not an hour. A SEPARATED tag rotates only once a DAY, so anything
       shorter guarantees amnesia for precisely the devices that rotate least - and an earpiece
       left in its case overnight is the ordinary case, not the awkward one.
       BOUNDED AT 500 MACS (OWNMAX), oldest dropped first, because a two day memory is not pruned
       by time in any useful way: a house has nothing like 500 devices but the air outside it
       does, and a dict with no ceiling is a leak with a long fuse.
       WRITTEN TO THE STATE FILE, so two days of memory is not undone by one restart - compact
       [tagId, last] pairs rather than named fields, about 20 kB at the cap, which the 2 MB tmpfs
       carries easily beside the 200 kB csv. An older state file without it is read as before.
   FIX: A ROTATION MATCH IGNORED THE DISTANCE INDICATOR, and that was the missing discriminator.
       It is the TAG'S OWN STATEMENT about whether its owner is with it, and a mac rotation does
       not change it - the owner walking away does. Two macs disagreeing about it at one instant
       are therefore not one tag, and matchRotation was not looking.
       Caught doing real damage: D3:3C (nearby) and FF:C5 (separated) were handed tagId 1 back
       and forth TWICE IN SEVEN SECONDS, and every other test passed them both - same battery,
       same types, both -59 dBm, both inside the gap. Two lookalike tags stealing each other's
       identity, with no reason to prefer either.
       A blank on either side is not a disagreement: an unknown payload length reads as "lenNN"
       and an entry from an older state file has none, and neither of those is evidence.
   NEW: ROTBACK (120 s) - a lineage that has just moved A -> B may not move B -> A within it.
       A rotation is ONE WAY, so a reversal is two wrong matches rather than two right ones, and
       it is the exact shape a pair of lookalike tags makes. A backstop for the indicator test
       above: the indicator alone would have refused both of those matches, but nothing else in
       the five tests could ever have noticed the loop.
   CHG: ROTGAP 6 s -> 15 s, MEASURED rather than guessed. With the refusal logging on, ten
       minutes in a real house produced 23 rotations refused on the gap alone, with gaps of 6.2
       to 13.3 s - and the next candidate up was 55.7 s, an unrelated mac. The whole band from
       13.3 to 55.7 was EMPTY, so the new threshold sits in open space instead of on a judgement.
       The gap is not the tag's doing: the rPi does not hear every packet, so the old mac's last
       HEARD frame is already seconds before it really stopped and the new mac's first HEARD frame
       is seconds after it really started. Both errors add, and 6 s could not absorb them.
       ROTQUIET STAYS AT 3, and the same log says why: all 34 of its refusals were between 0.0
       and 2.8 s, every one of them an old mac that had transmitted within the last three seconds
       and had therefore gone nowhere. Those refusals are right, which is also why raising it to
       5 earlier in this version was wrong.
   NEW: every lineage decision is logged, and the refusals say WHY. ROT matched, ROT? refused as
       ambiguous, ROTX nothing matched - all three prefixed "ROT" so one grep finds them:
           grep "findMyGroup ROT" /var/log/pibeacon
       ROTX names every candidate it turned down and which of the five tests failed, with the
       numbers: "2F:19 gap 9.4s>6; C8:BF rssi -79vs-71". Five tests refuse a rotation and the
       line used to say only that none fitted, which left ROTGAP/ROTQUIET/ROTRSSI to be tuned by
       guesswork - and guessing at ROTQUIET once already cost an afternoon and a wrong value.
       Level 20 when the rPi debug menu is 1, level 10 otherwise, the same deal logSummary makes:
       a few lines an hour, and needing the whole pi in debug to see them meant nobody did.
   FIX: A MAC THAT EXPIRED AND CAME BACK LOST ITS OWN LINEAGE - the third thing behind member
       devices multiplying, and the most routine of them. expireSecs is 10 s and these tags go
       half a minute without being heard all the time, so they expire constantly. On expiry the
       mac leaves cands, its lineage leaves tagIdOf and is parked in goneTags. Coming back on the
       SAME MAC it was therefore a mac nobody had ever seen: it went to matchRotation, which could
       hand it somebody else's lineage while its own sat in goneTags unclaimed. Two member devices
       both holding tagId "2-2" is what that looks like from indigo.
       IT COULD NOT RECLAIM ITS OWN THROUGH matchRotation EITHER, and that is a certainty rather
       than a near miss: its own goneTags entry is at least expireSecs old by definition and
       ROTGAP is 6 s, so the one lineage guaranteed to be right is the one entry the gap test
       always threw away. It is taken directly now - no guessing, it is the same mac.
       matchRotation also skips a mac's own goneTags entry now. It already skipped its own entry
       in cands; the goneTags half of the same pool had no such test.
   FIX: (plugin) tagIds is written even when EMPTY, while the mac is in the union. It used to be
       left alone when the rPi sent no lineage, so a device went on showing one it no longer had -
       and findMyAssignSlots matches rotations against exactly that state, so a stale lineage
       could claim a rotation belonging to another slot and hand a device to the wrong tag.
       A device that is DOWN still keeps its last lineage: that is what lets a rotation reclaim
       the slot it is being held on, and it is only stale once the mac is heard again saying so.
   NEW: (pi) a "members" send trigger - the rPi reports when WHICH macs are members changes, not
       only when HOW MANY changes. A tag rotating its mac swaps one member for another and leaves
       the count untouched. Harmless with one rPi; WRONG with several, because the plugin merges
       by mac and a stale mac beside a fresh one is two members for one tag. Held to MINSENDSECS.
   FIX: rPis that are switched off, or have no ip, were showing in the rPi checkbox selector. The
       hidden isRPIONnn fields default to true and the visible binding believes them, and
       getDeviceConfigUiValues only set them for types in a hand-kept list the dialogs had drifted
       away from. It now asks the dialog's own xml for the RPI_ENABLE_BLOCK marker.
       FIVE device types were affected, not only the new one: BLEconnect,
       BLEXiaomiMiTempHumSquare, BLEXiaomiMiVegTrug, BLEinkBirdPool01B, BLEfindMyGroup.
       The menu-style selectors were never affected - filterPiD already skips offline rPis.
   FIX: "is this a multi-rPi device" was decided by testing for rPiEnable0 ALONE, in SEVEN places.
       buildRpiEnableFieldsXml emits every number, so rPiEnable0 ought to be there - but a device
       saved by an older version, or whose props were written when fewer rPis existed, can carry
       rPiEnable1.. with no rPiEnable0 at all. The test then called it single-rPi and every branch
       downstream took the wrong path: no parameters sent to its rPis, no rPi list in the action
       menus, the address rebuilt in the old "Pi-n" format, and in syncSensors no rPi picked up
       its sensor at all. New isMultiRpiDevice(props) scans the whole range and is used at all
       seven. Found on the find my line; the other six were the same latent bug, older.

================================================================================
FIND MY - FILES, LOGGING AND DEBUG
================================================================================
   THE STATE FILE: temp/findMyGroup.state, written on every send, INDENTED, with each member a
       dict of named fields rather than a positional list. That file is read by a person far more
       often than by the program - "cat temp/findMyGroup.state" is the first thing anybody does
       when the count looks wrong - and one unbroken line of json answers nothing.
       temp/ IS A TMPFS, so it does not survive a reboot. The same file is therefore copied to the
       pibeacon directory every PERSISTSECS, and at startup restoreSlots() COPIES IT BACK
       into temp/ when temp/ has none, so the file is there before the first send rather than up
       to sendEverySecs later. master spares temp/findMyGroup.state when it empties temp/, so a
       master or beaconloop restart keeps the current state.
       One file, one format, two places - not two files with two formats and two loaders.
       PERSISTSECS IS 200 s, down from 300: a few kB 432 times a day is nothing an SD card
       notices, and it is what a reboot can lose that is worth shortening.
   THE CALIBRATION CSV: /home/pi/pibeacon/temp/findMyGroup.csv - ONE file with a fixed name,
       beside the state file, CAPPED AT 200 kB with the OLDEST rows dropped.
       IT IS COPIED OUT TO THE PIBEACON DIRECTORY TOO, on the same PERSISTSECS tick as the state
       file, and restored from there when temp/ comes up empty. It needs this more than the state
       file does: the state file is rebuilt within seconds by the tags themselves, while the csv
       is a TRAIL - a row that is not written again is gone, and a reboot took all of them.
       ONLY WHEN IT HAS CHANGED, by size and mtime. The csv runs to 200 kB, so copying it on
       every tick would be ~80 MB a day onto a card that has to last, nearly all of it rewriting
       bytes that had not moved. With the calibration log off nothing is written to it, so
       nothing is copied and it costs nothing at all.
       THE RESTORE HAPPENS BEFORE ANYTHING CAN APPEND, and that ordering is the whole safety of
       it: a freshly empty temp file copied out over the full one in the pibeacon directory would
       finish the job the reboot started. A csv that IS already in temp/ is the live one and is
       ahead of the copy, so it is never overwritten by it.
       It was a file per day in /var/log, cleaned up by age. Everything about that is gone and
       each part of it for its own reason:
       - /var/log meant creating a new name in a root-owned directory, which needed the
         writeFileAsRoot + makeOwnFileWritable dance on the first write of each day. temp/ belongs
         to the pi user, so it is now a plain append
       - a file per day meant a name to work out before reading one. "cat temp/findMyGroup.csv"
         needs nothing looked up
       - an age-based cleanup answers the wrong question. temp/ is a 2 MB TMPFS shared with
         findMyGroup.state, so what matters is that this cannot grow into that - not how old its
         rows are. A size cap needs no schedule and leaves nothing to sweep up
       The newest rows survive a trim and the oldest go: this is a rolling record of what is on
       the air now. Cut on LINE boundaries, never mid-row, and the column header is put back at
       the top so a trimmed file still starts by saying what its columns are.
       master is told NOT to delete it when it empties temp/ - it is in the keep list beside
       findMyGroup.state. BUT temp/ IS A RAM DISK: a reboot loses it. Copy it off first if it
       matters. The dialog says so.
       Verified by running 2500 write cycles through it: 10 trims, the file peaked at 204783
       bytes against the 204800 cap and never crossed it, the header stayed on line 1, and every
       line had an identical column count - ie no row was ever cut in half.
       "keepLogDays" is gone from the parameters, the rPi and the plugin's block: it configured
       the age cleanup that no longer exists, and a setting that does nothing is worse than none.
       The COLUMN HEADER is repeated every 10 minutes, in front of real rows only, because a file
       that runs for a day is read by scrolling into the middle of it where a header on line 1 is
       no use. NOTE for anything parsing these: the repeated header is a DATA ROW to a csv reader,
       skip lines starting with "timestamp,". The epoch column is gone and "timestamp" carries
       TENTHS instead - "2026-09-23T15:57:12.6".
       findmy_scan.py writes the identical schema, field for field, so the two writers cannot
       drift apart. findmy_merge.py - which DOES read these files, and was already broken - reads
       the timestamp with or without tenths and skips the header rows. It lives in
       Contents/Server Plugin/ - not pi/, because it runs on the mac and is not sent to the rPis,
       and not loose in Contents/, because that holds no other .py and a lone one there reads as
       a misplaced file. It sits with the plugin's other mac-side tools instead.
   DEBUG, THREE WAYS:
       - on the group device: off / summary on every send / summary + one line per find my frame
       - on the rPi, with no indigo at all: echo 1 > /home/pi/pibeacon/temp/findmy.debug. The FILE
         WINS while it exists; deleting it hands control back to the dialog. Checked every 5 s
       - in the plugin config: debug area "FindMy", to PLUGIN.LOG. Three blocks per message -
         RECEIVED (what one rPi sent), PROCESSED (the union, which rPi is stale, why memberCount
         is what it is) and MEMBER DEVICES (the slot-by-mac assignment). Between them they answer
         the questions this feature raises: why the count does not match the devices, which rPi is
         contributing what, is an rPi silently stale, and why a named device changed identity
   CHG: the rPi's per-frame debug line is COLUMNS, every field fixed width:
findMyGroup frame sl:01 C0:F8:D7:6F:D5:D6  rssi: -68  dist:separated  status:D0=11|010000 batt:critical  hint:8D  types:07|12        pkt#5
       The status byte is printed as hex, then the two bits that ARE established (battery) split
       by "|" from the six that are NOT. Those six have no confirmed meaning in any published work
       on offline finding, so they are printed raw rather than given invented labels - the same
       treatment as the hint byte, and for the same reason: this is logged to be correlated
       against devices whose identity is known, and a label that later turns out to be wrong is
       worse than no label. "sl:--" means the mac holds no slot, which is normal for most of what
       goes past.
   CHG: the count-change line and "slot NN came into use" are at level 10, ie PLUGIN.LOG ONLY.
       indigo_log_handler sits at INFO, so at 20 every count change was also going into the indigo
       event log - a tag drifting in and out of range all evening should not be writing there.
       THE ONLY findMy line left at 20: "rPi-x is still running the older findMyGroup.py". It is
       actionable, it means that rPi is not being counted, and it is printed once per device.

================================================================================
WHAT WAS MEASURED - NOT WORTH RE-DERIVING
================================================================================
   WHAT TURNS UP IN THE COUNT, settled by experiment and written into pi/findMyGroup.py:
         airtags and find my accessories   YES - one a few cm from the rPi reads about -32 dBm
         airpods                           YES - a case sent types 07 and 12 in ONE frame
         iphones and macs                  NO  - however close they sit. STILL TRUE, see below
       Two iphones 5 cm from the rPi, online and then with wifi and cell off, never produced a
       find my frame. An iphone does not broadcast offline-finding at all: the find my network
       carries LOST and OFFLINE devices, and a phone with a network of its own reports over the
       internet instead.
   A CLAIM THAT AN IPHONE 18 WAS BEING COUNTED WAS MADE HERE TODAY AND IS WITHDRAWN, together
       with both rules drawn from it. It is written up rather than deleted because the same
       mistake was made twice in an hour and the shape of it is worth keeping.
       The member FA:CF:35:CC:93:A9 was taken for the phone because it moved rPi-5 -> rPi-2 while
       the phone was carried that way. IT IS NOT THE PHONE: it is back on rPi-5 with the phone
       far away. That one apparent move is almost certainly one of the out-of-sync artefacts
       fixed in this same version - ie the evidence was produced by the very bug being debugged
       at the time, which is the trap worth remembering. A device that APPEARS to move is not
       evidence of anything while the move detection is itself under repair.
       So the original entry stands unchanged: iphones have not been observed in the count.
       WITHDRAWN WITH IT: "a mac that does not rotate tells a phone from a tag". Wrong twice over
       - the device is not a phone, and a SEPARATED device rotates about once a DAY, not every
       15 minutes. That is written at the top of this very file, "every ~15 min near its owner
       and daily when separated", so half an hour on one mac is what a separated device of any
       kind does, tag included. It distinguishes nothing.
       WHAT THE DEVICE ACTUALLY IS, from the calibration csv: a separated find my accessory
       sitting near rPi-5 - "separated" on every row, ~1 advertisement every 2 s (4-6 per 10 s
       interval), rssi pinned at -59 with the odd -62 so it is not moving, status 0x90 (battery
       bits 10 = low), hint 0x42 present as a separated payload should have. Entirely ordinary.
   CHG: appleTypes no longer calls a lone "12" a TAG. It says "find my only", which is all that is
       known: the mac sends find my and nothing else, which is what was observed - "tag" was an
       inference presented as a fact, and it is the inference that changed, not the evidence.
       It could never have identified one anyway, and this file already said why - a live apple device
       sends nearby-info / handoff / airdrop from a RESOLVABLE PRIVATE address (40-7F) while find
       my uses a key-derived RANDOM STATIC one (C0-FF), so the two never share a mac and the types
       collected under a find my mac are only ever "12". The label contradicted the paragraph
       explaining why it could not work.
       Anything MORE than 12 still means what it said: the mac also does live-apple-device things,
       which a tag cannot.
   appleTypes CANNOT SEPARATE A PHONE FROM A TAG, and nothing should be built on it. Live apple
       devices advertise nearby-info (0x10), handoff (0x0C) and airplay (0x09/0x16) from
       RESOLVABLE PRIVATE addresses, first octet 40-7F; find my uses a key-derived RANDOM STATIC
       address, C0-FF. Different messages, different address space. The state stays - it is free,
       it is evidence, and it is the thing that showed the idea was wrong.
       Because of this, the whole BLEconnect "account for known apple devices" machinery was
       REMOVED: it subtracted phones that were never in the number. Dead machinery that implies a
       relationship is worse than no machinery.
   WHAT RSSI CAN AND CANNOT TELL YOU, measured on one rPi and written into the device dialog:
         - FLAT UNDER ABOUT A METRE. 3 cm and 1 m read the same, so no threshold in that region
           can separate "on the desk" from "in the room" - there is nothing there to measure
         - beyond that, roughly 3 dB per further metre
         - THE ABSOLUTE NUMBERS BELONG TO THE DONGLE: -62 touching the rPi, -89 at four metres on
           the radio measured here. joinRssi -55 or -60 would mean "never counted" on that
           hardware while looking like a perfectly reasonable choice in the menu
       The menus as shipped are join -55..-80 and leave -75..-95: a deliberately narrow join and a
       permissive leave, which is the right way round - join decides what counts as "in", leave
       only has to avoid dropping a tag over a weak patch. CONSEQUENCE: nothing weaker than -80
       can ever join on this hardware, whatever leave is set to.
   MAC ROTATION IS NOT FAST. A "6 minute rotation" was recorded and is WITHDRAWN: the two macs
       were two airtags sitting beside the rPi, not one tag rotating. One of them held its mac for
       190+ packets over six minutes and through being carried across the room. Rotation is the
       usual ~15 minutes near the owner, so slot churn is much less of a problem than feared.
   THE 25% CPU IS NOT THE FIND MY PREFILTER, settled by experiment: the whole counter was disabled
       and the cpu did not move. 25% is what beaconloop costs on that rPi. parseFindMy on a frame
       that turns out not to be find my takes 0.53 us - about 0.1 ms per second at the frame rate
       this house produces, which cannot be 25% of anything. A hex-only pre-scan was written,
       measured at 1.1x, and DELETED: a second decoder to keep in step with the first, for a tenth
       of a microsecond, is a bad trade.
       Three versions were spent adjusting the thing that had been blamed first. The measurement
       that ended it took one checkbox. MEASURE FIRST - recorded in pi/beaconloop.py beside the
       filter, with an instruction not to narrow it again to save cpu.

================================================================================
THE BLE5 LISTENER - AND EVERY RSSI ON A MULTI-RADIO RPI
================================================================================
   FIX: THE BLE5 LISTENER NO LONGER FORWARDS BLE4 ADVERTISEMENTS when a scan radio is running.
       A ble5 controller in extended mode receives legacy ble4 adverts as well, so every ble4
       device was being heard TWICE - once by the ble4 scan radio, once by the ble5 listener - and
       the two do not agree. Measured on an airtag 24 cm from the rPi, same mac, same second: the
       scan radio read -24 while the listener read a near-constant -68.
       THE WINDOW WAS BIMODAL, and a median lands on whichever mode sent more packets. It scored a
       tag at -82 while it sat next to the rPi, put it under joinRssi and dropped it out of the
       members - which is where the whole investigation started.
       extAdvToLegacyHex now takes dropLegacy, set from Event_Type bit 4 ("legacy advertising PDUs
       used"), and the listener passes it whenever hciRoles has a scan radio that is not itself.
       The MAIN scan radio in extended mode does NOT drop them: there it is the only receiver, and
       dropping legacy would lose every ble4 device on the air. Two call sites, different answers.
       The 60 s extListener heartbeat reports how many legacy reports were dropped.
       NOTE THE SCOPE: this polluted every rssi-derived number on a multi-radio rPi - beacon
       distance, room assignment, presence thresholds. Those all get better here.
       hciRoles is used to find the scan radio, NOT useHCIForBeacon: that one is a local of the
       scan setup with no module-level binding, so reading it from the listener thread would have
       raised NameError. Caught before it shipped, noted so it is not "simplified" back.
   WHY THE SURVIVING NUMBERS LOOKED WORSE AT FIRST, and are not: with the drop active an airtag
       moved from 4 m to 3 cm read -62 to -68, while the dropped stream had read -24 at 24 cm. The
       missing number was the PACKET RATE - the surviving stream arrives every 3-5 s, the tag's
       real advertising interval, while the dropped one arrived every ~12 s, one advert in four.
       The -24 readings were not a better measurement; they were a quarter of the adverts on a
       scale about 40 dB adrift. What is left is one radio, a regular cadence, and a value that
       tracks distance: -89 at 4 m, -62 at 3 cm. That is calibratable. The mixture never was.
   CHG: an ESCAPE HATCH, because the drop may have kept the wrong radio on some hardware:
           touch /home/pi/pibeacon/temp/extListener.keepLegacy    -> keep both radios' copies
           rm    /home/pi/pibeacon/temp/extListener.keepLegacy    -> drop them (the default)
       Checked every 5 s and logged when it changes, so an A/B is two commands and no restart.
   CHG: (find my) the signal statistic is the median of the STRONGER HALF of the window, not the
       plain median. The weak copies are attenuated versions of the same transmission, so the
       strong ones are what "how close is this" means; taking the median of that half rather than
       the single maximum keeps one freak reading from deciding. ONE place decides it - smoothed()
       - used by join/leave, the state file, the summary and the csv. "signal taken as" on the
       device, or "rssiStat" in the parameters, switches back to "median".
       This is the statistical workaround; the listener fix above is the real cure. It stays,
       because it is still right for one radio hearing a device over two advertising channels, and
       it is what protects a single-radio rPi.

================================================================================
GREE AND TOSHIBA IR-AC
================================================================================
   FIX: the temperature could not be changed once the unit was in a mode indigo has no name for,
       or in a mode set by the IR-AC action. The ac beeped for every command - it beeps for any
       frame it accepts, changed or not - and kept the temperature it had.
       The ac has ONE setpoint, indigo has two, and WHICH of the two indigo lets you move follows
       indigo's own hvacOperationMode. The IR-AC "set mode" action stored the real ac mode in a
       prop that WINS over indigo's mode but never updated indigo's - so indigo could be showing
       "cool", and moving its cool setpoint, while the unit was heating and the frame was built
       from the HEAT setpoint, which nothing had touched. Same frame every press.
       Three parts: the mode indigo shows now follows the mode the ac was put in; indigo's OWN
       mode control now reaches the ac instead of being overridden back by the prop; and whichever
       setpoint the user moved is written to the one the frame is built from. Auto is untouched -
       it sends the midpoint, so both already count there.
       One resolver, _iracAcMode(), now answers "what mode is the ac in" for both the send path
       and the thermostat action, instead of the two disagreeing.
   FIX: pi/receiveCommands.py, the gree log line - it rebuilt the four messages WITHOUT the fan to
       print them, and buildSequence then falls back to byte 0 for byte 6 of message 3. Byte 0's
       fan field saturates at 3, so the log showed B0 for every speed from "2" upwards while the
       led actually sent 8 + the real fan index. The transmission was always right; the one byte
       the whole fan investigation turned on was misreported in the one place anybody would look.

================================================================================
ACROSS THE PLUGIN
================================================================================
   NEW: after a PLUGIN RESTART or a PROGRAM PUSH to an rPi, nothing goes up-->down for 120 s.
       An rPi that has just had files copied to it and master restarted needs time to come back
       and report; without the wait every beacon and sensor on it goes down and straight back up,
       which is noise in the log and fires triggers on a non-event.
       The suppression already existed (setCurrentlyBooting) and is used by four paths -
       checkRPIStatus, BeaconsCheckPeriod, BLEconnectCheckPeriod and checkForFastDown. Three
       things were missing:
        - it was set to 40-50 s at the end of a plugin restart, and to nothing at all on a push
          that did not go through a dialog button. Both are 120 s now, and the push case is set
          at the one choke point every push really passes through. PARAMETER-only pushes are not
          counted: they are re-read in place, nothing restarts
        - setCurrentlyBooting SET the deadline, so a later caller asking for less CUT an existing
          longer window - a restart asking for 120 s then losing it to a beep's 20 s. It now never
          SHORTENS a suppression that is already running
        - two down paths did not honour it at all: a fast-down queued BEFORE the restart still
          fired inside the window, and a find my member went down because the FIRST message after
          a restart carries one rPi's macs and every tag the others were holding looks gone. Both
          now check, and both leave the device alone until the window closes
   NEW: every device dialog gets a "print all properties and states of this device to the log
       after saving" checkbox (151 dialogs). Check it, save, and the full property set AND state
       set of that device is logged, sorted, one per line, including indigo's own "<state>_ui"
       entries - and it marks WHICH state the indigo State column is actually drawing:
           -- states (10), state column shows 'onOffState' (ie its .ui value if there is one):
              onOffState    = True                      <<< state column
              onOffState.ui = up         09-23 14:22:31 <<< state column
              status.ui     = up         09-23 14:22:31
       FIXED HERE: the marker looked for "<state>_ui" and indigo's states dict holds "<state>.ui",
       so it never marked the uiValue row - the one row it exists to point at. Both spellings are
       accepted now.
       It resets itself, so it never has to be switched off. WHY IT IS A DELAYED ACTION and not a
       print inside validateDeviceConfigUi: indigo writes valuesDict back to the device only AFTER
       the validate call returns, so printing there would show the props as they were BEFORE the
       save. The checkbox is cleared in valuesDict and ONLY on the success path, so a dialog that
       fails validation keeps it ticked and still prints when the field is fixed.
       NOT COVERED: the 15 device types with no ConfigUI block at all (DHT11, DHTxx, mhz-I2C,
       mhz-SERIAL, OUTPUTgpio-4/10/26, i2cADS1x15, i2cPCF8591, rdlidar, BLEminewE8, ..) - they
       have no dialog to put a checkbox in.
   NEW: "this tag is now on:" in the MEMBER dialog - putting a tag back on the device it belongs
       to when the automatic match failed. A mac rotated, the rPi could not prove the new mac was
       the same tag, and it turned up as a fresh findmy_NN beside the device somebody had named
       and built triggers on. Open the device you want to KEEP, pick the one it landed on, save:
       its states are copied onto this device and that other device is DELETED.
       IT IS THE FIRST FIELD IN THE DIALOG. It went in at the bottom first, under the "signal at
       1 metre" explanation and the "One member of the Find My group counter" text - two screens
       of grey - and was simply not found. A thing somebody goes LOOKING for belongs at the top;
       explanation belongs below the control it explains, and short. A dialog that tall can also
       push a field off a smaller screen entirely.
       WHICH DEVICE THE MENU IS ON IS DELIBERATE. It is on the one being kept, so the device that
       disappears is never the one whose dialog was just saved - and the menu lists each candidate
       with its mac, status and lastSeen, because the names are all findmy_NN until somebody
       renames them and the mac is the only way to tell them apart.
       Not copied: slot and created, which describe the DEVICE and not the tag. It keeps its own
       name, slot number and birthday; mac, previousMacs, lineage, signal, distance, battery and
       every per-rPi state move across. The .ui values travel WITH their state, so the indigo
       State column is right at once - which matters when the tag is down and no update is coming.
       NOTHING ELSE HAS TO BE TOLD: findMyAssignSlots rebuilds the slot map from the devices' own
       mac states every pass, so the next message finds the tag on the kept device and gives it
       that slot. The emptied slot number returns to the free pool by itself.
       Same group only, both must be member devices, and never itself - checked rather than
       trusted, because this deletes a device. It resets itself after the save.
       THE PLUGIN CANNOT DO THIS BY ITSELF, which is the point. Nothing in a find my frame says
       two macs are the same tag; the rPi matches rotations on timing and signal and refuses when
       it is not certain. This is where a person says what the radio cannot.
   NEW: the find my GROUP dialog gets a "print members" checkbox of its own, which prints every
       member device as a table - ONE COLUMN PER MEMBER, one row per state:
              slot               | 01                  | 02                  | 03
              -------------------+---------------------+---------------------+--------------------
              * unheard for      | 31s                 | 3.0h                | 4.5h
              * name claimed     | no                  | yes                 | no
              status             | up                  | down                | down
              mac                | D0:75:1A:2B:3C:01   | D0:75:1A:2B:3C:02   | D0:75:1A:2B:3C:03
       TRANSPOSED ON PURPOSE, and that is why it exists alongside the per-device print above. One
       device at a time answers "what does this one say"; a find my group asks "why is THAT one
       different from the others" - which slot is stale, which rPi hears what, which devices are
       still under their birth name and so are the next to be taken over by a new tag. That is a
       comparison ACROSS devices and it only reads if the states line up in rows.
       Every state ANY member has gets a row, so a state missing on one device shows as a BLANK
       CELL rather than the row being dropped - members genuinely differ, and that is worth seeing.
       The two rows marked "*" are worked out for the table and are not states: how long the tag
       has been unheard, and whether the device is still under its birth name. They are exactly
       what findMyAssignSlots ranks a free slot on.
       ONE uiValue ROW, and only one: the one the State column actually draws, which for a member
       device is onOffState.ui. The others only repeat rows already there - status.ui is given the
       SAME padded string as onOffState.ui, and mac.ui was the mac again.
   CHG: the member "mac" state no longer carries a uiValue. It was written as the mac itself, so
       indigo kept a mac.ui that was a second copy of the state - a wasted column in the sql
       logger and a duplicate line everywhere the states are listed. A uiValue is for when the
       display differs from the value, and here it did not.
       LINES STAY UNDER 285 CHARACTERS. With more members than fit, the same rows are printed
       again as a FURTHER SET of columns rather than wrapped - a wrapped row is not a row. The
       sets are EVEN - 13 members come out 7 and 6, not 11 and 2, because a set of 2 beside a set
       of 11 reads as a fault rather than as a table continued. Column
       width is the widest value in that column, capped at 19, which is what it takes to keep a
       mac (17) and a datestamp (19) whole; longer values are cut with "..".
       The names are listed once above the table and the columns are headed by SLOT: a column wide
       enough for "airtag jacket pocket" is a column wide enough to push the table off the screen.
       Delayed a second longer than the member state-list rebuild the same save triggers, so it
       prints what the members have AFTER the rPi checkboxes were applied, not a moment before.
   NEW: checkStateTypeCollisions() at startup - warns when any state name is declared with two
       different types, naming the state and both categories. Silent when there are none, which is
       the case now.
       IT EXISTS BECAUSE IT HAPPENED: renaming the find my member's "form" state to "distance"
       collided with "distance", a REAL on five distance sensors (ultrasound, vl53l0x/l1x,
       vl6180x, vcnl4010) since long before. One state name, two types. Indigo's sql logger makes
       one column per state and fixes its type when the device history table is created, so
       whichever device was logged second could not be written - and the message names neither the
       state nor the device: "One or more failures updating device history". The rename looked
       local and was not: the state TABLES are global, the device types are not. Recovering needs
       the device's history table thrown away, ie the device deleted and recreated.
       That is also why the old find my "distance" indicator was renamed distanceIndicator rather
       than left beside a state that really is metres: IT IS NOT A DISTANCE AND NEVER WAS - it is
       the tag's own idea of how far it is from ITS OWNER. "nearby" on a tag lying next to the rPi
       means the owner is in the house, and says nothing about the rPi at all.
   FIX: (general, all device types) a FORCED state write whose VALUE IS UNCHANGED no longer stamps
       lastSensorChange. Forcing is how a uiValue gets refreshed - the padded date column, or a
       clock inside a display string - and the value is unchanged by definition in that case. It
       was being recorded as a sensor change, which would have turned lastSensorChange into "time
       of the last message" on every device whose display carries a clock. A forced write that
       DOES change the value still counts as a change, as before.
   FIX: a find my MEMBER device came back from its config dialog showing "on" in RED instead of
       "up" in green. Saving a device rebuilds its state list, which resets every uiValue to the
       raw state value, so applyOnOffDisplay() runs ~2 s later to paint it back - and it painted
       the GENERIC on/off look, the defaults for a device whose dialog has no on/off wording and
       no colour scheme. A member device has neither and should not need them: "up"/"down" and
       green/grey are not its user's choice, they are what the device IS.
       findMyMemberDisplay() is now shared between that path and updateFindMyMembers so the rule
       lives in ONE place.
   FIX: WHAT THE INDIGO STATE COLUMN ACTUALLY RENDERS, which cost two versions to find: INDIGO
       DRAWS THE uiValue OF THE STATE IT DISPLAYS, and for a device that supports on/off that is
       onOffState - NOT the state getDeviceDisplayStateId() names. So status_ui was being set
       correctly and nothing was reading it. The padded string goes on both now.
       A uiValue is ALSO invisible everywhere else - the state list, a trigger, a condition, a
       control page. Text that has to be usable there must be a real STATE, which is why the apple
       types ended up as one.
   FIX: "readConfig RPI cleanup rpi: 15 del output devId:661307361 deldevreason:2" on EVERY plugin
       start, for the same device, for ever - and the entry was never actually gone.
       The cleanup deleted any output entry whose VALUE was empty. But "" is not corruption, it is
       exactly what checkDevToPi() writes for an output device that needs no extra parameters, and
       for OUTPUTgpio / OUTPUTi2cRelay the parameters file copies the whole dict straight over,
       "" entries included - so those registrations work and deleting them was deleting live
       devices out of an rPi's parameters.
       It also could not stick: syncSensors -> checkDevToPi put the entry back with the same ""
       within the same start, so all it produced was the message and a rewrite of RPIconf. The two
       had opposite ideas of what "" means and fought about it once per start.
       Only "the device is gone from indigo" drops an entry now. A device that is disabled, or no
       longer points at this rPi, was never this loop's job - checkSensortoPi does that properly.
   FIX: closestRPI and closestRPILast on a find my member device read 0 when they had never been
       set, and 0 is a real rPi number - so a new device claimed rPi 0 was the closest, and the
       first genuine reading then filed that 0 away as where the tag "was".
       Indigo defaults an Integer state to 0 and there is no way to say "unset", so -1 has to be
       written deliberately. New devices are stamped at birth; existing ones are corrected when
       their group's state lists are refreshed - at plugin start and on every save of the group -
       rather than on update, because a device whose rPi has gone quiet gets no updates at all and
       would keep the 0 for ever.
       ONLY when rPi "0" is not one of that group's rPis. If it is, 0 is a real answer and the
       device is left to say so.
   FIX: syncSensors raised KeyError on props["rPiEnable"+piU]. The branch is entered when
       rPiEnable0 exists, but that does not mean every pi in _rpiBeaconList has an entry - a
       device saved when fewer pis existed is missing the higher numbers. Uses .get now.
   FIX: pi/beaconloop.py - the one log(30 left in the whole pi directory, in the findMyGroup
       import hook. It came in with the imported package and was left alone at import time; rPi
       programs log at 20. pi/*.py is back to zero log(30.

==================================================
RELEASE  v2022.192.0  @ 2026-09-03
gree air conditioners, and what it took to make one obey
==================================================

IN SHORT
   Gree AC support is complete and confirmed at the unit: power, mode, temperature, five fan
   speeds and both louvers. A new action sets a whole state in one go. The IR tooling on the rpi
   grew a recorder, a replayer, a carrier scanner and a scripted walk.

THE PROTOCOL, measured from a real remote and confirmed against the unit
   - one press is FOUR messages ~40 ms apart and the unit acts on nothing less. A single frame is
     ignored however often it is repeated.
   - byte 3's high nibble is a MESSAGE INDEX - 5 the full state, 6 and 7 the command bytes only,
     A a terminator carrying no command. IRremoteESP8266 reads it the same way from a different
     remote; four packets on every press is not documented anywhere found.
   - the fan is FIVE speeds. byte 0's fan field is two bits and saturates at 3, so the real
     position rides in byte 6 of message 3. Holding byte 6 constant stops the fan moving at all.
